Weak sequential stability of solutions to a nonisothermal kinetic model for incompressible dilute polymeric fluids
Miroslav Bulíček, Josef Málek, and Endre Süli

Abstract
The paper is concerned with the mathematical analysis of a class of thermodynamically consistent kinetic models for nonisothermal flows of dilute polymeric fluids, based on the identification of energy storage mechanisms and entropy production mechanisms in the fluid under consideration. The model involves a system of nonlinear partial differential equations coupling the unsteady incompressible temperature-dependent Navier–Stokes equations to a temperature-dependent generalization of the classical Fokker–Planck equation and an evolution equation for the absolute temperature. Sequences of smooth solutions to the initial-boundary-value problem, satisfying the available bounds that are uniform with respect to the given data of the model, are shown to converge to a global-in-time large-data weak solution that satisfies an energy inequality, where the absolute temperature satisfies a renormalized variational inequality, implying weak sequential stability of the mathematical model.



1
Introduction

Since the pioneering contributions of Werner Kuhn
[
33
]
, Hans Kramers
[
32
]
and other scientists working at the interface of polymer chemistry and statistical physics during the first half of the twentieth century, kinetic models have been widely and successfully used to describe the motion of polymeric fluids; see, in particular, the second volume of the two-volume monograph by Bird et al.
[
12
]

and the book by Öttinger
[
43
]
for further details. During the past two decades significant progress has been made with the mathematical analysis of kinetic models of dilute polymers that involve the coupling of the incompressible or compressible Navier–Stokes equations to the Fokker–Planck equation, and proofs of the existence of large-data global weak solutions to these models are now available in various physical settings; (see, for example,
[
4
,
5
,
6
,
9
,
16
,
18
,
19
,
27
]
as well as
[
30
,
31
,
37
,
38
,
39
,
40
,
41
]
and the references cited therein for the case of coupled incompressible Navier–Stokes–Fokker–Planck systems, and

[
7
,
8
,
22
,
46
]
for the mathematical analysis of coupled compressible Navier–Stokes–Fokker–Planck systems). While for fully macroscopic models of nonisothermal flows of viscoelastic fluids there is now a growing body of mathematical theory focused on proofs of the existence of a global-in-time large-data weak solutions (cf., for example,
[
1
,
10
,
11
,
14
,
15
,
17
]
; see also
[
28
,
36
]
), the mathematical analysis of nonisothermal kinetic models of dilute polymers is in a much less satisfactory state. Indeed, all of the publications concerned with the mathematical analysis of coupled Navier–Stokes–Fokker–Planck systems cited above are restricted to isothermal flows: the temperature field is tacitly assumed to be homogeneous in space, and the evolution equation describing the temporal and spatial variations of the temperature field is excluded from the model.

The aim of this paper is to develop the mathematical analysis of a class of thermodynamically consistent kinetic models for nonisothermal flows of dilute polymeric fluids, formulated for compressible polymeric fluids by Dostalík et al. in
[
21
]
. While the model derived in
[
21
]
is essentially the same as the one discussed by Grmela and Öttinger in
[
26
,
44
]
,
1
1

1

See, in particular, Sec. III of
[
44
]
, entitled
Nonisothermal kinetic theory of polymeric fluids.
its derivation is substantially different: instead of the so-called GENERIC formalism of Grmela and Öttinger, it is based on the identification of energy storage and entropy production mechanisms in the fluid under consideration, which then yields explicit formulae for the Cauchy stress tensor and for all the fluxes involved. We shall confine our attention here to the physical situation where the solvent is an incompressible heat-conducting Newtonian fluid of constant specific density, resulting in the coupling of the unsteady incompressible temperature-dependent Navier–Stokes equations to a temperature-dependent generalization of the classical Fokker–Planck equation and an evolution equation for the absolute temperature. For clarity and completeness of the exposition, we shall revisit the derivation of the model presented in
[
21
]
, but in the case of an incompressible polymeric fluid, which is of interest to us here. Following the derivation of the model, we shall collect the equations into a system of nonlinear partial differential equations, supplement them with boundary and initial conditions, and state the precise conditions on the data, in preparation for the subsequent mathematical analysis of the model. The main result of the paper is the proof of weak sequential stability of solutions to the mathematical model in the sense that sequences of smooth solutions to the initial-boundary-value problem, satisfying the available bounds that are uniform with respect to the given data, converge to a global-in-time weak solution that satisfies an energy inequality, where the absolute temperature satisfies a renormalized variational inequality. While the result is not an explicit proof of the existence of weak solutions, it is a key step towards the development of a rigorous existence theory for the model. The next section focuses on the derivation of the model. The structure of the rest of the paper is outlined at the end of Section
2
.

2
Derivation of the model

The aim of this subsection is to revisit the derivation of the model presented in
[
21
]
; however, instead of the compressible heat-conducting dilute polymeric fluid flow model considered there, we shall focus on the case of an incompressible heat-conducting dilute polymeric fluid.
Since the precise values of the various physical constants
included in the model stated in
[
21
]
do not influence the mathematical analysis pursued later on in this paper, we shall set all of them (viz. the specific density
\rho_{s}>0
, the Boltzmann constant
k_{\mathrm{B}}>0
, the constant reference temperature
\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}>0
, the specific heat at constant volume
c_{\rm V}>0
,
and the reference polymer molecule length
q_{\mathrm{ref}}>0
) equal to
1
, with the exception of the (constant) hydrodynamic drag coefficient
\zeta
, which we set equal to
1/2
.

Suppose that
T>0
and let
\Omega
be a bounded open simply-connected Lipschitz domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
d\in\{2,3\}
.
For our state variables, we choose the velocity
\boldsymbol{v}
, the internal energy
e
, and the nonnegative probability density function
\varphi
. We have the following governing equations:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mbox{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\,\boldsymbol{v}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $(0,T)\times\Omega$},\\
\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}&=\mbox{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathbb{T}+\boldsymbol{f}&&\qquad\mbox{in $(0,T)\times\Omega$},\\
\dot{e}&=\mathbb{T}:\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})-\mbox{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\,\boldsymbol{j}_{e}&&\qquad\mbox{in $(0,T)\times\Omega$},\\
\dot{\varphi}+\mbox{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}+\mbox{div}_{\boldsymbol{q}}\big((\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi+\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}\big)&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $(0,T)\times\Omega\times D$},\end{aligned}

(2.1)

where
\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}
,
\dot{e}
and
\dot{\varphi}
signify
the material derivatives
2
2

2

The material-derivative
\dot{u}
of a real-valued function
u=u(t,\boldsymbol{x})
is defined by
\dot{u}=\partial_{t}u+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}u
, and the material derivative
\dot{\boldsymbol{u}}
of an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued vector function
\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{u}(t,\boldsymbol{x})
is defined by
\dot{\boldsymbol{u}}=\partial_{t}{\boldsymbol{u}}+(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}})\boldsymbol{u}
. In particular, when
\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\,\boldsymbol{v}=0
as is the case here,
\dot{u}=\partial_{t}u+\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(u\boldsymbol{v})
and
\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}=\partial_{t}{\boldsymbol{v}}+\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{v}\otimes\boldsymbol{v})
, where
\boldsymbol{v}\otimes\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with
(\boldsymbol{v}\otimes\boldsymbol{v})_{i,j}:=v_{i}v_{j}
.
of
\boldsymbol{v}
,
e
and
\varphi
, respectively,
\boldsymbol{f}
is the given density of body forces,
\mathbb{T}
is the Cauchy stress tensor,
\boldsymbol{j}_{e}
,
\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}
and
\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}
are flux-vectors, to be determined in the course of the derivation of the model, and
\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}):=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}+(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})^{\mathrm{T}})
is the symmetric velocity gradient. The partial differential equation (
2.1
)
4

is the temperature-dependent generalization of the Fokker–Planck equation for the evolution of the probability density function
\varphi=\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})
, modelling, at time
t\in[0,T)
and spatial location
\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega
, the random configuration (i.e., orientation vector)
\boldsymbol{q}
of polymer molecules idealized as elastic dumbbells suspended in the solvent (which are assumed not to interact with each other and which move without self-interaction). In eq. (
2.1
)
4
,
D:=B(\boldsymbol{0},\sqrt{b})\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a ball of radius
\sqrt{b}
centred at the origin, and
b>0
.
3
3

3

The extension of the mathematical analysis of the dumbbell model considered here to a bead-spring-chain model, involving
K+1
massless beads instead of just a pair of massless beads, which are linearly coupled with
K
elastic springs, does not involve any conceptual or technical difficulties. For the sake of clarity of the exposition we shall therefore confine our attention to the dumbbell model, corresponding to the case of
K=1
.
The form of the partial differential equation (
2.1
) is motivated by the structure of the Fokker–Planck equation in the isothermal setting (see, for example, Sec. 1.1.4 of the doctoral thesis of Lozinski
[
35
]
and the introductory section of
[
3
]
for the derivation of the Fokker–Planck equation associated with the dumbbell model from Brownian dynamics).

The spring-force associated with the dumbbell is assumed to be of the form

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{q}):=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\left[\,\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})\right]\!,

(2.2)

where
\theta>0
is the absolute temperature. The function
\mathcal{U}
defined by

\mathcal{U}(\boldsymbol{q}):=\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})

is the total spring potential of the dumbbell that represents the polymer molecule suspended in the solvent, which is additively decomposed into its elastic part
\mathcal{U}_{e}

and its entropic part
\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}
.

In what follows, we shall adopt the notational convention that the same symbol,
|\cdot|
, denotes the absolute value of a real number, the Euclidean norm of a
d
-component vector, or the Frobenius norm of a
d\times d
matrix; it will be clear from the context which of these three interpretations is intended in a particular case. The functions
\mathcal{U}_{e}
and
\mathcal{U}_{\eta}
are defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q}):=U_{e}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\left|\boldsymbol{q}\right|^{2}\bigg)\quad\mbox{for $\boldsymbol{q}\in D$}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q}):=U_{\eta}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\left|\boldsymbol{q}\right|^{2}\bigg)\quad\mbox{for $\boldsymbol{q}\in\overline{D}$},

(2.3)

where
\overline{D}
is the closure of
D
, and
U_{e}\in C^{2}([0,b/2);\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\cap L^{1}((0,b/2))
and
U_{\eta}\in C^{1}([0,b/2];\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
are monotonically increasing functions. We shall make further, physically relevant assumptions on
U_{e}
and
U_{\eta}
later on, but for now the above ones will suffice. We note in passing that because

\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q}=\left[U_{e}^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}\right)+\theta\,U_{\eta}^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}\right)\right](\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\boldsymbol{q}),

we find that
\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q}
is a nonnegative scalar multiple of the symmetric positive semidefinite rank-one matrix
\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\boldsymbol{q}
. Therefore,
\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q}=(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})^{\mathrm{T}}=\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\boldsymbol{F}
is a symmetric positive semidefinite rank-one matrix.

Our objective is to determine the Cauchy stress tensor
\mathbb{T}
and the fluxes
\boldsymbol{j}_{e}
,
\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}
and
\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}

in the model (
2.1
)
from the specified constitutive equation for the Helmholtz free energy,
\psi=\psi(\theta,\varphi)
, defined by

\displaystyle\psi(\theta,\varphi):=-\theta(\log\theta-1)+\int_{D}\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+\theta\int_{D}[\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})\varphi+\varphi\log\varphi]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

(2.4)

In order to fulfil this objective, we shall derive an evolution equation for the specific entropy
\eta
of the form

\displaystyle\dot{\eta}+\mbox{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\,\boldsymbol{j}_{\eta}=\xi,

(2.5)

where
\boldsymbol{j}_{\eta}
is the entropy-flux, and require that
\xi\geq 0
, in agreement with the second law of thermodynamics. In the process of doing so, we shall also specify both
\boldsymbol{j}_{\eta}
and
\xi
.

We begin by noting that while both sides of the equality (
2.4
) are functions of
t
and
\boldsymbol{x}
, the Helmholtz free energy
\psi
can also be understood as a functional that maps the pair of functions
\theta=\theta(t,\boldsymbol{x})
and
\varphi=\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})
to
\psi(\theta,\varphi)
, parametrized by
t
and
\boldsymbol{x}
.

The thermodynamic quantities
\psi
,
\theta
,
e
, and
\eta
are related by means of the relationships

\displaystyle\psi:=e-\eta\theta\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\theta}=-\eta,

(2.6)

where
\eta=\eta(t,\boldsymbol{x})
is the specific entropy and
\partial\psi/\partial\theta
denotes the partial Gateaux derivative of
\psi
, understood as a functional, with respect to
\theta
. It then follows from (
2.6
)
2
that

\displaystyle\eta:=-\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\theta}=\log\theta-\int_{D}[\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})\varphi+\varphi\log\varphi]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

(2.7)

Therefore, by (
2.6
)
1
, (
2.4
) and (
2.7
), the internal energy
e
can be expressed as

\displaystyle e=\psi+\eta\theta=\psi-\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\theta}\theta=\theta+\int_{D}\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

(2.8)

The total energy is

\displaystyle E:=\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}+e=\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}+\theta+\int_{D}\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

(2.9)

Taking the scalar product of (
2.1
) with
\boldsymbol{v}
, adding the resulting equality to (
2.1
)
3
and noting (
2.9
), it follows that the evolution of the total energy
E
is governed by the partial differential equation

\displaystyle\dot{E}+\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{j}_{e}-\mathbb{T}\boldsymbol{v})=\boldsymbol{f}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}.

(2.10)

If, as will be the case here, the equation (
2.10
) is supplemented with the boundary condition

\displaystyle(E\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{j}_{e}-\mathbb{T}\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=0\quad\mbox{on $(0,T)\times\Omega$,}

(2.11)

where
\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
is the unit outward normal vector to the boundary
\partial\Omega
of
\Omega
, then (
2.1
)
1
, (
2.10
) and (
2.11
) imply that

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\Omega}E(t,\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{f}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\cdot\boldsymbol{v}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x},

(2.12)

expressing the balance of the total energy.

Next, we derive an evolution equation for the specific entropy
\eta
. Taking the material derivative of both sides of the equality (
2.6
)
1
, using the chain rule on the left-hand side and the product rule on the right-hand side, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\theta}\dot{\theta}+\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\varphi}\dot{\varphi}=\dot{e}-\theta\dot{\eta}-\eta\dot{\theta},

(2.13)

where
(\partial\psi/\partial\theta)\dot{\theta}
is the partial Gateaux derivative of
\psi
with respect to
\theta
acting on
\dot{\theta}
, while
(\partial\psi/\partial\varphi)\dot{\varphi}
is the partial Gateaux derivative of
\psi
with respect to
\varphi
acting on
\dot{\varphi}
. However, since
(\partial\psi/\partial\theta)\dot{\theta}=-\eta\dot{\theta}
thanks to (
2.6
)
2
, the equality (
2.13
) is simplified to

\theta\dot{\eta}=\dot{e}-\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\varphi}\dot{\varphi}.

Next, because

\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\varphi}\dot{\varphi}=\int_{D}\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})\dot{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+\theta\int_{D}[\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})+\log\varphi+1]\dot{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q},

by inserting this into the above equality and using the equations for
\dot{e}
and
\dot{\varphi}
, we find that

\displaystyle\theta\dot{\eta}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{T}:\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})-\mbox{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\,\boldsymbol{j}_{e}+\int_{D}\big[\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta(\log\varphi+1)\big]\,\mbox{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{D}\big[\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta(\log\varphi+1)\big]\,\mbox{div}_{\boldsymbol{q}}\big((\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi+\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}\big)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{T}:\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})-\mbox{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left[\boldsymbol{j}_{e}-\int_{D}\big[\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta(\log\varphi+1)\big]\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{D}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\big[\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta(\log\varphi+1)\big]\cdot\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{D}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\big[\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta(\log\varphi+1)\big]\cdot\big((\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi+\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}\big)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\partial D}\big[\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta(\log\varphi+1)\big]\,\big((\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi+\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}\big)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathrm{d}S(\boldsymbol{q}),

following partial integration on
D
, where
\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{q}}:=\boldsymbol{q}/|\boldsymbol{q}|
with
\boldsymbol{q}\in D
is the unit outward normal vector to the boundary
\partial D
of
D
.
We proceed by imposing the boundary condition

\displaystyle\big((\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi+\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}\big)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{q}}=0\quad\mbox{on $(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D$},

(2.14)

which then annihilates the boundary integral over
\partial D
. Hence,
using (
2.2
) and the symmetry of the matrix-valued function
\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q}
,

\displaystyle\theta\dot{\eta}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{T}:\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})-\mbox{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left[\boldsymbol{j}_{e}-\int_{D}\big[\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta(\log\varphi+1)\big]\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{D}\left\{\big[\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})+(\log\varphi+1)\big]\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta+\theta\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi}{\varphi}\right\}\cdot\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{D}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\big[\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta(\log\varphi+1)\big]\cdot\big((\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi+\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}\big)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{T}:\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})-\mbox{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left[\boldsymbol{j}_{e}-\int_{D}\big[\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta(\log\varphi+1)\big]\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{D}\left\{\big[\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})+(\log\varphi+1)\big]\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta+\theta\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi}{\varphi}\right\}\cdot\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}:\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})-\theta\int_{D}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\cdot(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{D}\big[\boldsymbol{F}+\theta\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi}{\varphi}\big]\cdot\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

We focus our attention on the second integral in the penultimate line. Because
\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{q}}((\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{q})=0
,
partial integration over
D
implies that

\int_{D}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\cdot(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}=\int_{\partial D}\varphi\big((\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{q}}\big)\,\mathrm{d}S(\boldsymbol{q}).

For reasons that will become apparent after we have stated our precise assumptions on the spring potential
\mathcal{U}_{e}
, the probability density function
\varphi
must vanish on
(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D
. Since we have already imposed a boundary condition for
\varphi
on this set (cf. (
2.14
) above), we cannot of course explicitly impose
\varphi=0
on
(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D
as an additional boundary condition. Interestingly, the reason why
\varphi
should vanish on
(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D
is quite different and more indirect. Suffice to say at this point that it is a consequence of the fact that
\mathcal{U}_{e}
will be assumed to be a finitely extensible nonlinear elastic (FENE) type spring potential, which blows up on
\partial D
, and then the requirement that a term of the form
\int_{[0,T]\times\Omega\times D}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})|^{2}\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t
that appears in the energy inequality associated with the model should remain finite is, in fact, the mechanism that forces
\varphi
to vanish on
(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D
; the precise details of this will be given in Section
3
. In any case, as
\varphi
vanishes on this set, the boundary integral stated above is equal to
0
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\theta\dot{\eta}

\displaystyle=\left[\mathbb{T}-\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right]:\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})-\mbox{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left[\boldsymbol{j}_{e}-\int_{D}\big[\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta(\log\varphi+1)\big]\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{D}\left\{\big[\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})+(\log\varphi+1)\big]\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta+\theta\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi}{\varphi}\right\}\cdot\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{D}\big[\boldsymbol{F}+\theta\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi}{\varphi}\big]\cdot\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

Next, we divide this equality by
\theta
with the aim of transforming it into an equation of the desired form (
2.5
), while defining
\mathbb{T}
,
\boldsymbol{j}_{e}
,
\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}
and
\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}
so that the term
\xi
appearing on the right-hand side of (
2.5
) is nonnegative, as required. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\dot{\eta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\theta}\left[\mathbb{T}-\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right]:\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})-\frac{1}{\theta}\mbox{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left[\boldsymbol{j}_{e}-\int_{D}\big[\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta(\log\varphi+1)\big]\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{D}\left\{\big[\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})+(\log\varphi+1)\big]\,\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta}{\theta}+\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi}{\varphi}\right\}\cdot\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{D}\big[\frac{1}{\theta}\boldsymbol{F}+\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi}{\varphi}\big]\cdot\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\theta}\left[\mathbb{T}-\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right]:\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})-\mbox{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left[\frac{\boldsymbol{j}_{e}}{\theta}-\frac{1}{\theta}\int_{D}\big[\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta(\log\varphi+1)\big]\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta}{\theta^{2}}\cdot\left[\boldsymbol{j}_{e}-\int_{D}\big[\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta(\log\varphi+1)\big]\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{D}\left\{\big[\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})+(\log\varphi+1)\big]\,\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta}{\theta}+\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi}{\varphi}\right\}\cdot\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{D}\big[\frac{1}{\theta}\boldsymbol{F}+\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi}{\varphi}\big]\cdot\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

Hence, by transferring the second term appearing on the right-hand side to the left-hand side,

\displaystyle\dot{\eta}+\mbox{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left[\frac{\boldsymbol{j}_{e}}{\theta}-\frac{1}{\theta}\int_{D}\big[\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta(\log\varphi+1)\big]\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\theta}\left[\mathbb{T}-\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right]:\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})-\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta}{\theta^{2}}\cdot\left[\boldsymbol{j}_{e}-\int_{D}\big[\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta(\log\varphi+1)\big]\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{D}\left\{\big[\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})+(\log\varphi+1)\big]\,\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta}{\theta}+\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi}{\varphi}\right\}\cdot\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{D}\big[\frac{1}{\theta}\boldsymbol{F}+\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi}{\varphi}\big]\cdot\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\theta}\left[\mathbb{T}-\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right]:\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})-\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta}{\theta^{2}}\cdot\left[\boldsymbol{j}_{e}-\int_{D}\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{\theta}\int_{D}\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi}{\varphi}\cdot\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}-\int_{D}\big[\frac{1}{\theta}\boldsymbol{F}+\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi}{\varphi}\big]\cdot\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

(2.15)

The left-hand side of the equality (
2
) is now in the desired form. Therefore, it remains to choose

\mathbb{T}
,
\boldsymbol{j}_{e}
,
\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}
and
\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}
so that each of the expressions in the last two lines of (
2
) is nonnegative. Starting from the last term in the last line and proceeding backwards, we first define

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}:=-4\boldsymbol{F}\varphi-4\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi.

(2.16)

Next, we define

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}:=-\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi,

(2.17)

and then we set

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{j}_{e}-\int_{D}\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}=-\kappa(\theta)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta,

(2.18)

where
\kappa(\theta)>0
is the temperature-dependent coefficient of heat conductivity; therefore,

\boldsymbol{j}_{e}=-\kappa(\theta)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta-\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\int_{D}\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

Concerning the first term on the right-hand side of (
2
), because
\mathbb{I}:\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})=\mbox{div}\,\boldsymbol{v}=0
,
the addition of a scalar multiple of the identity matrix
\mathbb{I}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
to the expression in the square brackets has no influence on the value of the right-hand side of the equality. Bearing this in mind, we recall that the deviatoric (traceless) part
\mathbb{S}_{\delta}
of a matrix
\mathbb{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is defined by
\mathbb{S}_{\delta}:=\mathbb{S}-\frac{1}{d}\mathrm{tr}(\mathbb{S})\mathbb{I}
, and we set

\left[\mathbb{T}-\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right]_{\delta}=2\nu(\theta)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}),

where
\nu(\theta)>0
is the temperature-dependent shear viscosity coefficient. Consequently,

2\nu(\theta)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})=\mathbb{T}-\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}-\frac{1}{d}\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbb{T}-\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\mathbb{I};

that is,

\mathbb{T}=\frac{1}{d}\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathbb{T}-\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\mathbb{I}+2\nu(\theta)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})+\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

Motivated by this form, we define the Cauchy stress tensor
\mathbb{T}
by

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}:=-\mathrm{p}\mathbb{I}+2\nu(\theta)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})-2k_{\mathrm{B}}\theta n_{\mathrm{P}}\mathbb{I}+\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q},

where the sum of the first two terms is the usual Navier–Stokes–Fourier viscous stress tensor, and the sum of the last two terms is Kramers’ polymeric stress tensor;
\mathrm{p}=\mathrm{p}(t,\boldsymbol{x})
is the pressure (i.e., the constitutively undetermined part of the spherical stress),
k_{\mathrm{B}}>0
is the Boltzmann constant, and
n_{\mathrm{P}}=n_{\mathrm{P}}(t,\boldsymbol{x})
is the polymer number density, defined by

\displaystyle n_{\mathrm{P}}(t,\boldsymbol{x}):=\int_{D}\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

(2.19)

Scaling the Boltzmann constant to 1, we therefore (re)define

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}:=-\mathrm{p}\mathbb{I}+2\nu(\theta)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})-2\theta n_{\mathrm{P}}\mathbb{I}+\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

(2.20)

Finally, in view of the second term on the left-hand side of (
2
), we define the entropy flux
\boldsymbol{j}_{\eta}
as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{j}_{\eta}:=\frac{\boldsymbol{j}_{e}}{\theta}-\frac{1}{\theta}\int_{D}\big[\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})+\theta(\log\varphi+1)\big]\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}=-\frac{\kappa(\theta)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi}{\theta}+\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\int_{D}[\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi+\varphi\log\varphi]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

(2.21)

Thereby we arrive at the desired form of the evolution equation for the specific entropy:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\eta+\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{v}\eta)+\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left(\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\int_{D}\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\int_{D}\varphi\log\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)-\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left(\frac{\kappa(\theta)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta}{\theta}\right)\\
&\quad=\frac{2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}}{\theta}+\frac{\kappa(\theta)|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2}}+\theta\int_{D}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\\
&\qquad\qquad+\int_{D}\frac{4}{\theta\varphi}\left|\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right|^{2}\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}:=\xi\geq 0,\end{aligned}

(2.22)

which, by recalling the definition of
\eta
(cf. (
2.7
)), integrating over
\Omega
, and using the boundary conditions
\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{x}}|_{\partial\Omega}=0
, (
2.31
)
1
and (
2.32
) then implies that

\begin{split}&\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\Omega}\bigg[-\log\theta+\int_{D}\big[\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})\varphi+\varphi\log\varphi\big]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}}{\theta}+\frac{\kappa(\theta)|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2}}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&\qquad\qquad+\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\theta\int_{D}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+\int_{D}\frac{4}{\theta\varphi}\left|\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right|^{2}\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=0.\end{split}

(2.23)

In order to obtain the required evolution equation for the absolute temperature
\theta
, we note
that, by taking the material derivative of (
2.8
) followed by insertion of equations (
2.1
)
3,4
into the resulting equality, we obtain

\displaystyle\dot{\theta}

\displaystyle=\dot{e}-\int_{D}\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})\dot{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{T}:\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})-\mbox{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\,\boldsymbol{j}_{e}+\int_{D}\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})\big[\mbox{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}+\mbox{div}_{\boldsymbol{q}}\big((\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi+\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}\big)\big]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

It remains to substitute the defining expressions for
\mathbb{T}
,
\boldsymbol{j}_{e}
,
\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{x}}
and
\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}
stated in (
2.20
), (
2.18
), (
2.17
) and (
2.16
) above into the right-hand side of this equality to infer that the evolution equation for the temperature is the following:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \dot{\theta}-\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\kappa(\theta)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta)&=2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}\\
&\qquad+4\int_{D}\left(\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e})\varphi+|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\varphi\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\\
&\qquad+\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}):\int_{D}\theta(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.\end{aligned}

(2.24)

Having completed the derivation of the model, we close this section by collecting the governing equations into a system of nonlinear partial differential equations and supplementing them with boundary and initial conditions.

Statement of the initial-boundary-value problem.
The initial-boundary-value problem that we will be concerned with in the rest of the paper is the following:

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}=0\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{in $(0,T)\times\Omega$},

(2.25)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{v}+\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{v}\otimes\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle\quad=\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left(-\mathrm{p}\mathbb{I}+2\nu(\theta)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})-2\theta n_{\mathrm{P}}\mathbb{I}+\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)+\boldsymbol{f}\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{in $(0,T)\times\Omega$},

(2.26)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\theta

\displaystyle+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta-\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\kappa(\theta)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta)=2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+4\int_{D}\left(\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e})\varphi+|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\varphi\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}):\int_{D}\theta(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q},\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{in $(0,T)\times\Omega$},

(2.27)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi+\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left(\boldsymbol{v}\varphi-\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{q}}\left((\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi-4\boldsymbol{F}\varphi-4\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\right)=0\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{in $(0,T)\times\Omega\times D$},

(2.28)

with

\displaystyle n_{\mathrm{P}}:=\int_{D}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\boldsymbol{F}:=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta},

(2.29)

This system of partial differential equations will be considered subject to the following boundary and initial conditions, where the hypotheses imposed on the initial data are motivated by the requirement that the equations (
2.7
),(
2.9
), (
2.12
), (
2
), involving
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\theta
and
\varphi
, be meaningful:

•

For the velocity field

\boldsymbol{v}
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{0}\quad\mbox{on $(0,T)\times\partial\Omega$}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\boldsymbol{v}(0,\boldsymbol{x})=\boldsymbol{v}_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\quad\mbox{for $\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega$},

(2.30)

with
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}_{0}=0
on
\Omega
in the sense of distributions, and
\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{x}}|_{\partial\Omega}=0
in
W^{-1/2,2}(\partial\Omega)
;

•

For the absolute temperature

\theta
:

\displaystyle\kappa(\theta)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=0\quad\mbox{on $(0,T)\times\partial\Omega$}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\theta(0,\boldsymbol{x})=\theta_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\quad\mbox{for $\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega$};

(2.31)

where
\theta_{0}\in L^{1}(\Omega)
and there exists a positive constant
\theta_{\mathrm{min}}
such that
\theta_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})\geq\theta_{\mathrm{min}}
,
\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega
;

•

For the probability density function

\varphi
:

\displaystyle\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=0\quad\mbox{on $(0,T)\times\partial\Omega\times D$},

(2.32)

where
\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
is the unit outward normal vector to
\partial\Omega
,

\displaystyle((\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi-4\boldsymbol{F}\varphi-4\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{q}}=0\quad\mbox{on $(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D$},

(2.33)

where
\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{q}}
is the unit outward normal vector to
\partial D
, and we impose the initial condition

\displaystyle\varphi(0,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})=\varphi_{0}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\quad\mbox{for $(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\in\Omega\times D$},

(2.34)

where

\varphi_{0}\in L^{1}\log L^{1}(\Omega\times D;\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega;L^{1}(D;\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}))\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi_{0}\in L^{1}(\Omega\times D;\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}).

Assumptions on the material functions.

We shall assume that

\displaystyle\mbox{$U_{e}\in C^{2}([0,b/2);\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\cap L^{1}((0,b/2))$ and $U_{\eta}\in C^{1}([0,b/2];\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})$},\hskip 17.07164pt

U_{e}
is monotonically increasing and such that

\displaystyle\lim_{s\rightarrow(b/2)_{-}}U_{e}(s)=+\infty,\;\lim_{s\rightarrow(b/2)_{-}}U^{\prime}_{e}(s)=+\infty\;\mbox{and}\;0\leq U^{\prime\prime}_{e}(s)\leq c_{e}[U_{e}^{\prime}(s)]^{2}\;\mbox{for all $s\in[0,b/2)$},

(2.35)

\displaystyle\mbox{where $c_{e}>0$ is a positive constant, independent of $s\in[0,b/2)$}.\hskip 14.22636pt

We note in passing that in the case of the classical FENE (finitely extensible nonlinear elastic) potential proposed by Warner
[
47
]
, defined by

\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q}):=\frac{Hb}{2}\log\left(1-\frac{|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}}{b}\right)^{-1},\quad\mbox{$|\boldsymbol{q}|<\sqrt{b}$},

corresponding to

U_{e}(s):=\frac{Hb}{2}\log(1-2s/b)^{-1}

for
s\in[0,b/2)
, where
H>0
is the spring-constant, hypothesis (
2
) is satisfied. The same is true of variants of the FENE potential based on alternative approximations of the inverse
L^{-1}
of the Langevin function
x\mapsto L(x):=\coth(x)-(1/x)
(cf.
[
2
]
and
[
29
]
). Another example of a function that satisfies (
2
) is the FENE-like potential

\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q}):=\frac{Hb}{2}\left[\left(1-\frac{|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}}{b}\right)^{-r}-1\right],\quad\mbox{$|\boldsymbol{q}|<\sqrt{b}$},

with
r\in(0,1)
, corresponding to

U_{e}(s):=\frac{Hb}{2}[(1-2s/b)^{-r}-1]

for
s\in[0,b/2)
.

At the very end of the next section (cf. Step 15 there) we shall demand that
U_{e}
satisfies a stronger assumption than (
2
); specifically, it will be required there that

\displaystyle\mbox{$U_{e}\in C^{2}([0,b/2);\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\cap L^{1}((0,b/2))$ and $U_{\eta}\in C^{1}([0,b/2];\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})$},\hskip 17.07164pt

U_{e}
is monotonically increasing and such that

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69054pt\lim_{s\rightarrow(b/2)_{-}}U_{e}(s)=+\infty,\;\lim_{s\rightarrow(b/2)_{-}}U^{\prime}_{e}(s)=+\infty\;\mbox{and}\;0\leq U^{\prime\prime}_{e}(s)\leq c_{e}(s)[U_{e}^{\prime}(s)]^{2}\;\mbox{for all $s\in[0,b/2)$},

(2.36)

\displaystyle\mbox{where $c_{e}\in C([0,b/2);\mathbb{R}_{>0})$, with $\lim_{s\to b/2_{-}}c_{e}(s)=0$}.\hskip 42.67912pt

Clearly, if (
2
) holds, then so does (
2
). In the case of the classical FENE potential
c_{e}=2/Hb
in (
2
), while (
2
) does not hold. On the other hand, the FENE-like potential stated above satisfies (
2
) with
c_{e}(s):=(2/Hb)(1-2s/b)^{r}
and, a fortiori, (
2
) for all
r\in(0,1)
. As will become clear in the next section (cf. Step 15 there), the imposition of the stronger requirement (
2
) becomes necessary because the evolution equation for the absolute temperature
\theta
in the model generally does not provide a uniform upper bound on
\theta
over
(0,T)\times\Omega
for an initial datum in
L^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}_{>0})
. However, until Step 15 of the next section, we shall only require the weaker assumption (
2
) to hold.

The classical FENE potential and its variants are physically motivated: their purpose is to emulate the finite extensibility of polymer molecules; they do so by ensuring that the magnitude of the spring force
\boldsymbol{q}\in D\mapsto\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{q})
tends to
+\infty
as
\boldsymbol{q}
approaches the boundary
\partial D
of the
bounded
configuration space domain
D
. In the context of the model considered here, this singular behaviour of
\boldsymbol{F}
is encoded in our assumptions on the elastic spring potential
U_{e}
(cf. (
2
) and (
2
)). Unsurprisingly, the singular behaviour of
U_{e}
, and thereby also of
\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q}):=U_{e}(\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2})
, complicates the analysis of the model and has a significant impact on the behaviour of the probability density function
\varphi
as
\boldsymbol{q}
approaches
\partial D
. This becomes perhaps most evident by observing that in order to ensure that the integral

\int_{D}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

appearing in the temperature equation
is finite under our assumptions on
U_{e}
, the function
\varphi
and its normal derivative have to vanish as
\boldsymbol{q}
approaches
\partial D
. We shall make this assertion rigorous in Lemma
1
below.
4
4

4

To give a simple illustration of this point, consider the FENE potential
U_{e}(s):=(Hb/2)\log(1-(2s/b))^{-1}
and suppose that
\phi\in C^{1}([0,b/2];\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}))
.
Let
g(s):=(\phi(s)-\phi(b/2))/(s-b/2)
for
s\in[0,b/2)
with
g(b/2):=\phi^{\prime}(b/2)
, and note that
g\in C([0,b/2])\cap C^{1}([0,b/2))
. Suppose further that
g(s)\leq 0
for all
s\in[0,b/2]
. Then, to ensure that

\infty>\int_{0}^{b/2}|U_{e}^{\prime}(s)|^{2}\phi(s)\,\mathrm{d}s=H^{2}\int_{0}^{b/2}\frac{\phi(s)}{\big(1-\frac{2s}{b}\big)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}s=\frac{H^{2}b^{2}}{4}\left[\phi\bigg(\frac{b}{2}\bigg)\lim_{\sigma\to(b/2)_{-}}\int_{0}^{\sigma}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}s}{\big(s-\frac{b}{2}\big)^{2}}+\lim_{\sigma\to(b/2)_{-}}\int_{0}^{\sigma}\frac{g(s)}{s-\frac{b}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}s\right],

it is necessary that
\phi(b/2)=0
and
g(b/2)=0
, i.e., both
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
must vanish at
s=b/2
.

Our assumptions on
U_{\eta}
imply that there exists a positive constant
C
such that

\displaystyle|U_{\eta}^{\prime}(s)|

\displaystyle\leq C

\displaystyle\quad\mbox{for all $s\in[0,\frac{b}{2}]$}.

(2.37)

For example, the classical Hookean spring-potential
\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q}):=\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}
with
|\boldsymbol{q}|\leq\sqrt{b}
,
corresponding to
U_{\eta}(s)=s
for
s\in[0,b/2]
satisfies the hypotheses imposed on
U_{\eta}
.

The coefficient of heat conductivity
\kappa=\kappa(\theta)
depends on the absolute temperature
\theta
. We shall assume in what follows that
\kappa\in C([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}_{>0})
and

\displaystyle C(1+\theta^{\beta})\leq\kappa(\theta)\leq\hat{C}(1+\theta^{\beta})\quad\mbox{for all $\theta\in[0,\infty)$, where $\beta>\frac{5}{6}$,}

(2.38)

and
C
and
\hat{C}
are positive constants, independent of
\theta
. The motivation for the assumed lower bound on the exponent
\beta
appearing in (
2.38
) will be made clear in the next section in the derivation of the a priori bound on solutions to this coupled system of nonlinear partial differential equations.

Concerning the temperature-dependent shear viscosity coefficient
\nu(\theta)
that appears in the balance of linear momentum equation (
2.26
) we shall assume that

\nu\in BC([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}_{>0})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\nu(\theta)\geq C\quad\mbox{for all $\theta\in[0,\infty)$},

where
C
is a positive constant independent of
\theta
. The density of body forces
\boldsymbol{f}
appearing in (
2.26
) will be assumed to belong to
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Up to the end of Step 14 in Section
3
the spring-potentials
U_{e}
and
U_{\eta}
(cf. (
2.3
)) are assumed to satisfy (
2
); thereafter (
2
) is assumed. As we have yet to prove that the assumed positivity of
\theta_{0}
implies that
\theta(t,\boldsymbol{x})>0
for all
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}
, we shall assume that the material functions
\kappa
and
\nu
are even functions of
\theta
; i.e., that
\kappa(\theta)=\kappa(|\theta|)
and
\nu(\theta)=\nu(|\theta|)
for all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
.

The rest of the paper is structured as follows.

In the next section, we shall derive various formal a priori bounds. By the word
formal
we mean that we shall assume for the moment that the problem has a smooth solution. The first group of these estimates follows the guideline provided by the thermodynamic approach presented above. After checking that
\varphi
is nonnegative and
\theta
is strictly positive, starting from the governing equations (
2.25
)–(
2.28
) and knowing what the correct forms of the internal energy
e
(see (
2.8
)) and the entropy
\eta
(see (
2.7
)) should be, we rederive the evolution equations for
e
,
E
and
\eta
, giving us two fundamental estimates, see (
3.28
) and (
3
) below. The second group of estimates consists of improvements that stem from the assumption (
2.38
) on the heat conductivity coefficient, and better properties of
\varphi
in the interior of
(0,T)\times\Omega\times D
, or up to the boundary provided that
\theta
is bounded above.

We shall then, in Section
4
, state our definition of weak solution. Interestingly, the concept of solution that we consider here is different from any of the concepts used for the incompressible/compressible Navier–Stokes–Fourier system. There, one has at least three natural options
[
23
,
13
,
24
]
: (i) to use the equation for
E
, (ii) to use the equation for
e
, and (iii) to use the equation for
\eta
. While these formulations, when supplemented by conservation of the global total energy, are equivalent at the level of classical (regular enough) solutions, they are,—even if we replace the equality signs in the equations for
e
and
\eta
with inequalities,—different at the level of variational (weak) solutions. It transpires that, for the problem considered here, we cannot use any of these three formulations. This is due to the impossibility of ensuring integrability of the terms
\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\int_{D}\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}
and
\int_{D}\big[\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})\varphi+\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi\log\varphi\big]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}
appearing in the flux terms of the equations for
e
(and hence also for
E
) and for
\eta
, respectively. As a result of these difficulties and in view of the available estimates, we are thus forced to work with the equation for the temperature (
2.27
) in a renormalized sense, and have to replace the ‘=’ sign by the ‘
\geq
’ sign therein.

Finally, also in Section
4
, we provide a rigorous analysis of the model, leading to the key contribution of the paper stated in Theorem
1
: the weak compactness of sequences of solutions to the model (
2.26
)–(
2.38
), which are shown to converge to a global-in-time large-data weak solution that satisfies the energy inequality (
3
) below.

3
Formal a priori estimates

We begin by performing some formal preparatory calculations which are required for the derivation of the desired a priori estimates. We shall assume throughout this section that
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\varphi
and
\theta
are continuous functions of their arguments, defined on
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}
,
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\overline{D}
and
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}
, respectively,
and that
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\varphi
and
\theta
are sufficiently many times continuously differentiable so as to satisfy the partial differential equations that feature in the model, as well as the associated boundary and initial conditions, in the usual pointwise sense. We shall assume that the initial datum
\varphi_{0}
for the Fokker–Planck equation is nonnegative a.e. on
\overline{\Omega}\times\overline{D}
and that the initial datum
\theta_{0}
for the temperature equation is strictly positive a.e. on
\overline{\Omega}
; we shall then prove that the corresponding solution
\varphi
to the Fokker–Planck equation is nonnegative a.e. on
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\overline{D}
, and that the solution
\theta
to the temperature equation is strictly positive a.e. on
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}
.

More precisely, in this section we assume that
\boldsymbol{v}\in C^{1,2}([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with
\boldsymbol{v}|_{[0,T]\times\partial\Omega}=\boldsymbol{0}
,
\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}=0
on
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}
,
\theta\in C^{1,2}([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega})
and
\varphi\in C^{1,2,2}([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\overline{D})
.
Motivated by the form of the fourth term on the right-hand side of the evolution equation (
2.22
) for the specific entropy
\eta
and the final term of the first integral over
D
appearing on the right-hand side of the evolution equation (
2.27
) for the absolute temperature, we shall further suppose that

\int_{D}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})|^{2}}{\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}<\infty\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\int_{D}[\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})|^{2}\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})<\infty

for all
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}
.

In connection with the imposition of the boundary condition (
2.33
) an important remark is in order: because the spring force
\boldsymbol{F}=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}
appearing in (
2.33
) involves the gradient of
\mathcal{U}_{e}
, and the first and second partial derivatives of
\mathcal{U}_{e}
, as well as
\mathcal{U}_{e}
itself, blow up on
\partial D
because of (
2
), for the boundary condition (
2.33
) to be meaningful, it is necessary that
\varphi|_{(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D}=0
. Since we have already imposed a boundary condition on
(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D
in the form of (
2.33
), we cannot of course impose a second boundary condition on
(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D
; the reason why
\varphi
should vanish on
(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D
therefore requires clarification. In this respect, we note that because of (
2
) we have
5
5

5

From (
2
) we have
U^{\prime}_{e}(s)\leq U^{\prime}_{e}(0)+c_{e}\int_{0}^{s}[U^{\prime}_{e}(\sigma)]^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma
for all
s\in(0,b/2)
and then, again by (
2
), it follows that
\int_{0}^{b/2}[U^{\prime}_{e}(\sigma)]^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma=+\infty
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\int_{D}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}=\int_{D}|U_{e}^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)|^{2}|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\geq\int_{b/2\leq|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}<b}|U_{e}^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)|^{2}|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\geq\frac{b}{2}\int_{b/2\leq|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}<b}|U_{e}^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle\geq C(b)\int_{b/4}^{b/2}[U^{\prime}_{e}(\sigma)]^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma=C(b)\!\int_{0}^{b/2}[U^{\prime}_{e}(\sigma)]^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma-C(b)\!\int_{0}^{b/4}[U^{\prime}_{e}(\sigma)]^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma\geq C(b)\!\int_{0}^{b/2}[U^{\prime}_{e}(\sigma)]^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma-C_{1}(b)=+\infty.

\int_{D}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}=+\infty,

and therefore the integral

\displaystyle\int_{D}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

(3.1)

appearing on the right-hand side of the temperature equation (
2.27
) will only be finite if the (nonnegative) function
\varphi
vanishes on
\partial D
; i.e.,

\displaystyle\varphi=0\quad\mbox{on $(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D$}.

(3.2)

This homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition on
\varphi
is therefore imposed
implicitly
(rather than explicitly as a genuine boundary condition) through the requirement that the integral (
3.1
) involving the singular potential
\mathcal{U}_{e}
be finite. In fact, a stronger assertion than (
3.2
) holds, which we shall formulate in the next lemma, and which will be used to justify the absence of various boundary integrals over
\partial D
that would otherwise arise during the course of partial integration with respect to
\boldsymbol{q}
. As solutions to the system of partial differential equations under consideration, which we are formally manipulating at this stage, will be replaced by solutions that are required to satisfy a certain energy (in)equality to be derived later in this section, the presence of the singular potential
\mathcal{U}_{e}
in that energy (in)equality will imply that both
\varphi
and
\varphi(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{q}})
vanish on
(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D
.

Lemma 1

Let
Q:=(0,T)\times\Omega
and suppose that
g\in L^{1}(Q;\mathbb{R}_{>0})
and
\varphi\in L^{1}(Q;W^{1,1}(D;\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}))
. Suppose further that
U\in C^{2}([0,\frac{b}{2});\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
,
U^{\prime}
is nonnegative,
U(s)\to+\infty
and
U^{\prime}(s)\to+\infty
as
s\to(b/2)_{-}
, and that there exists a positive constant
c
such that

\displaystyle|U^{\prime\prime}(s)|\leq c[U^{\prime}(s)]^{2}\quad\forall\,s\in[0,b/2).

(3.3)

Suppose, finally, that the following bound holds:

\int_{Q\times D}g(t,\boldsymbol{x})\left(\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})|^{2}}{\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})}+\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})(1+[U^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)]^{2})\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C.

(3.4)

Then,

\displaystyle g\varphi

\displaystyle\in L^{1}(Q;W^{1,1}_{0}(D))\quad\mbox{and}

(3.5)

\displaystyle g\varphi U^{\prime}

\displaystyle\in L^{1}(Q;W^{1,1}_{0}(D)).

(3.6)

In particular,
\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\cdot)
and
\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\cdot)U^{\prime}(|\cdot|^{2}/2)
have zero trace in
L^{1}(\partial D)
for a.e.
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q
.

Proof
We begin by showing that
g\varphi
and
g\varphi U^{\prime}
both belong to
L^{1}(Q;W^{1,1}(D))
. Thanks to the assumed nonnegativity of
g
,
\varphi
and
U^{\prime}
, we have from (
3.4
) that
g\varphi\in L^{1}(Q\times D)=L^{1}(Q;L^{1}(D))
, and

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\int_{Q\times D}g(t,\boldsymbol{x})\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})U^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{Q\times D}g(t,\boldsymbol{x})\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\left(1+[U^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)]^{2}\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C,

whereby
g\varphi U^{\prime}\in L^{1}(Q\times D)=L^{1}(Q;L^{1}(D))
. Further, we also have that

\displaystyle\int_{Q\times D}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}(g(t,\boldsymbol{x})\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q}))|+|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}(g(t,\boldsymbol{x})\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})U^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2))|\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\int_{Q}g(t,\boldsymbol{x})\int_{D}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})|\big(1+U^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)\big)+\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})|U^{\prime\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)|\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\int_{Q}g(t,\boldsymbol{x})\int_{D}\left(\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})|^{2}}{\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})}+\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\big(1+[U^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)]^{2}\big)\right)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty,

where the constant
C
depends on the diameter
\sqrt{b}
of
D
and the constant
c
from the assumed bound (
3.3
) in the statement of the lemma. Here in the transition from the second line to the third line we made use of the elementary calculation

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})|(1+U^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)

\displaystyle=\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})|}{\sqrt{\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})}}\sqrt{\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})}(1+U^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})|^{2}}{\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})}+\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\big(1+[U^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)]^{2}\big).

Thus,
g\varphi
and
g\varphi U^{\prime}
both belong to
L^{1}(Q;W^{1,1}(D;\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}))
. Hence, by the trace theorem, we infer that the traces of
g\varphi
and
g\varphi U^{\prime}
on
\partial D
are well-defined and belong to
L^{1}(Q;L^{1}(\partial D;\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}))
.

To complete the proofs of (
3.5
) and (
3.6
) it remains to show that the traces of
g\varphi
and
g\varphi U^{\prime}
are equal to
0
a.e. on
Q\times\partial D
. We shall first show this for
g\varphi U^{\prime}
, and then proceed analogously in the simpler situation of
g\varphi
.

Let us therefore show that the trace of
g\varphi U^{\prime}
is equal to
0
a.e. on
Q\times\partial D
. To this end, for
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q
we introduce

u(t,\boldsymbol{x},r):=\int_{\partial B_{r}}\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})U^{\prime}(r^{2}/2)\,\mathrm{d}S(\boldsymbol{q}),\quad 0<r<\sqrt{b},

where
B_{r}:=B(\mathbf{0},r)
. Thanks to the fact that
g\varphi U^{\prime}\in L^{1}(Q;W^{1,1}(D;\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}))
, it follows from the trace theorem that
0\leq\int_{Q}g(t,\boldsymbol{x})\int_{\partial B_{r}}\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})U^{\prime}(r^{2}/2)\,\mathrm{d}S(\boldsymbol{q})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{Q}g(t,\boldsymbol{x})u(t,\boldsymbol{x},r)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
for all
r\in(0,\sqrt{b})
. Hence, the integrand of the last integral must be finite for a.e.
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q
and for all
r\in(0,\sqrt{b})
. As, by hypothesis,
g(t,\boldsymbol{x})>0
for a.e.
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q
, it then follows that
u(t,\boldsymbol{x},r)
must be finite for a.e.
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q
and for all
r\in(0,\sqrt{b})
. This means that
u(t,\boldsymbol{x},r)
is well-defined for a.e.
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q
and for all
r\in(0,\sqrt{b})
. Then, from the definition of
u
and (
3.4
), it follows that

\int_{0}^{\sqrt{b}}U^{\prime}(r^{2}/2)u(t,\boldsymbol{x},r)\,\mathrm{d}r=\int_{D}\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})[U^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)]^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}<\infty

(3.7)

for almost all
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q
. In addition, since
\varphi U^{\prime}\in W^{1,1}(D;\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
for almost all
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q
, we have
u(t,\boldsymbol{x},\cdot)\in\mathcal{C}([0,\sqrt{b}];\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
for almost all
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q
. Clearly,
u
is a nonnegative function of its arguments. Assume for a moment that, for some
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q
,
\lim_{r\to(\sqrt{b})_{-}}u(t,\boldsymbol{x},r)>0
. Then, thanks to the continuity of
u
with respect to its third argument, there exist
\varepsilon>0
and
r_{0}\in(0,\sqrt{b})
such that
u(t,\boldsymbol{x},r)\geq\varepsilon
for all
r\in[r_{0},\sqrt{b}]
. Consequently,

\displaystyle+\infty>\int_{0}^{\sqrt{b}}U^{\prime}(r^{2}/2)u(t,\boldsymbol{x},r)\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\geq\varepsilon\int_{r_{0}}^{\sqrt{b}}U^{\prime}(r^{2}/2)\,\mathrm{d}r=\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}\int_{r_{0}^{2}/2}^{b/2}U^{\prime}(s)\frac{\,\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{s}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{b}}\left(\lim_{s\to(b/2)_{-}}U(s)-U(r_{0}^{2}/2)\right)=+\infty.

Thus, we have arrived at a contradiction, which then implies that, contrary to our supposition that
\lim_{r\to(\sqrt{b})_{-}}u(t,\boldsymbol{x},r)>0
for some
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q
, in fact,

\lim_{r\to(\sqrt{b})_{-}}u(t,\boldsymbol{x},r)=0\quad\mbox{for a.e.~$(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q$}.

This implies that, for a.e.
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q
,

\lim_{r\to(\sqrt{b})_{-}}\int_{\partial B_{r}}\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})U^{\prime}(r^{2}/2)\,\mathrm{d}S(\boldsymbol{q})=0.

Thanks to the assumed nonnegativity of
\varphi
and
U^{\prime}
this means that
\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})U^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)=0
for a.e.
(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\in Q\times\partial D
. That completes the proof of (
3.6
).

To complete the proof of (
3.5
), we need to show that the trace of
g\varphi
is equal to
0
a.e. on
Q\times\partial D
. Similarly as in the case of
g\varphi U^{\prime}
above, we now define

u(t,\boldsymbol{x},r):=\int_{\partial B_{r}}\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\,\mathrm{d}S(\boldsymbol{q}),\quad 0<r<\sqrt{b},

where, again
B_{r}:=B(\boldsymbol{0},r)
. Instead of (
3.7
), we then have

\int_{0}^{\sqrt{b}}U^{\prime}(r^{2}/2)u(t,\boldsymbol{x},r)\,\mathrm{d}r=\int_{D}\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})U^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})(1+[U^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)]^{2})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}<\infty.

By an argument based on contradiction, identical to the one in the case of (
3.6
), we deduce that

\lim_{r\to(\sqrt{b})_{-}}u(t,\boldsymbol{x},r)=0\quad\mbox{for a.e.~$(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q$}.

This implies that, for a.e.
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q
,

\lim_{r\to(\sqrt{b})_{-}}\int_{\partial B_{r}}\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\,\mathrm{d}S(\boldsymbol{q})=0.

Thanks to the assumed nonnegativity of
\varphi
this means that
\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})=0
for a.e.
(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\in Q\times\partial D
. That completes the proof of (
3.5
).

\Box

Remark 1

In order to illustrate the relevance of Lemma
1
suppose that
\theta
and
\varphi
are such that the inequality (
3.49
), which we shall prove below, holds, i.e., that, for each positive real number
\theta_{\mathrm{max}}
,

\int_{Q_{\theta_{\mathrm{max}}}}\int_{D}\left(\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}}{\theta}\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\leq C(\theta_{\mathrm{max}}),

where
Q_{\theta_{\mathrm{max}}}:=\{(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q\,:\,0\leq\theta(t,\boldsymbol{x})\leq\theta_{\mathrm{max}}\}
and
C(\theta_{\mathrm{max}})
is a positive constant depending on
\theta_{\mathrm{max}}
. It then follows from Lemma
1
with
Q
there replaced by
Q_{\theta_{\rm max}}
and
g
chosen to be identically equal to
1
, that the terms

\varphi(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{q}}
and
\varphi(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{q}}
, involved in the zero normal flux boundary condition (
2.33
), i.e., in the boundary condition

0=\left((\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi+\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{q}}=\big(\varphi(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{q}-4\varphi(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta})-4\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\big)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{q}},

that was imposed for
\varphi
on
(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D
, both vanish on
Q_{\theta_{\mathrm{max}}}\times\partial D
, and therefore by passing to the limit
\theta_{\textrm{max}}\to+\infty
, also on
Q\times\partial D=(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D
. In fact, the term
\varphi(\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta})
also vanishes on
(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D
, because by hypothesis
\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\in C^{1}(\overline{D})
and the function
\varphi
vanishes on
(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D
. Thus, the boundary condition (
2.33
) reduces to
\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{q}}=0
on
(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D
, which is of a similar form as the boundary condition (
2.32
) imposed for
\varphi
on
(0,T)\times\partial\Omega\times D
. For related considerations in the isothermal setting, we refer Zhang &
Zhang
[
48
]
, Liu & Liu
[
34
]
and Masmoudi
[
37
]
; see in particular Theorem 1.1 in
[
34
]
and Remark 3.6 in
[
37
]
.

Motivated by Lemma
1
, in the formal calculations that follow we shall therefore assume that
\varphi
vanishes on
(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D
, and that
\varphi(\cdot,\cdot,\boldsymbol{q})
decays to
0
sufficiently fast as
\boldsymbol{q}\to\partial D
to ensure that integrals over
\partial D
are well defined; moreover, if in the course of our calculations boundary integrals over
\partial D
happen to disappear following partial integration over
D
, then it will be understood that this is either because of the boundary condition (
2.33
) or because
\varphi(\cdot,\cdot,\boldsymbol{q})
decays to
0
sufficiently fast as
\boldsymbol{q}\to\partial D
to annihilate the boundary integral over
\partial D
in question. In particular, we shall suppose that

\displaystyle\varphi\in L^{1}(Q;W^{1,1}_{0}(D;\mathbb{R}))\quad\mbox{and}\quad\varphi\,U_{e}^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)\in L^{1}(Q;W^{1,1}_{0}(D;\mathbb{R})).

(3.8)

Because, by hypothesis,
\varphi\in C^{1,2,2}([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\overline{D}))
and
\boldsymbol{q}\mapsto U_{e}^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)\in C^{1}(D)
, it follows that
\varphi\,U_{e}^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)\in C([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times D))
and
\lim_{\boldsymbol{q}\to\partial D}\varphi\,U_{e}^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)=0
. Therefore,
\varphi\,U_{e}^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)
is uniformly continuous on
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\overline{D}
, and thereby it is also bounded on
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\overline{D}
; that is,

\displaystyle\max_{(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\overline{D}}|\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})|U_{e}^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)=:C_{0}<\infty.

We are now ready to embark on the derivation of the required (formal) a priori bounds. For the sake of clarity of the exposition, we split the process into sixteen separate steps.

As
\varphi
is a probability density function, it is required to be nonnegative. However, the nonnegativity of
\varphi
is a property that should be deduced from the nonnegativity of the initial datum
\varphi_{0}
for the Fokker–Planck equation (using a parabolic minimum principle, for example,) instead of assuming,
a priori
, that it holds. Unfortunately, the parabolicity of the Fokker–Planck equation isn’t automatically guaranteed, as it hinges on the positivity of the absolute temperature
\theta
. The absolute temperature is, for physical reasons, positive; however, this property should again not be assumed, but should be inferred from the positivity of the initial temperature
\theta_{0}
. However, to deduce the positivity of
\theta
from the positivity of
\theta_{0}
, one needs nonnegativity of
\varphi
.

To break this vicious circle, for
z\in\mathbb{R}
let
[z]_{+}:=\max\{z,0\}~(\geq 0)
,
[z]_{-}:=\min\{z,0\}~(\leq 0)
, and replace for the moment the initial-boundary-value problem (
2.28
), (
2.32
), (
2.33
), (
2.34
) for the Fokker–Planck equation with the following:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi

\displaystyle+\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left(\boldsymbol{v}\varphi-[\theta]_{+}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi\right)

\displaystyle+\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{q}}\left((\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi-4\boldsymbol{F}[\varphi]_{+}-4[\theta]_{+}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\right)=0\quad\mbox{in $(0,T)\times\Omega\times D$},

(3.9)

with
\boldsymbol{F}:=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}
,
subject to the following boundary and initial conditions:

\displaystyle[\theta]_{+}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=0\quad\mbox{on $(0,T)\times\partial\Omega\times D$},

(3.10)

where
\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
is the unit outward normal vector to
\partial\Omega
, and

\displaystyle((\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi-4\boldsymbol{F}[\varphi]_{+}-4[\theta]_{+}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{q}}=0\quad\mbox{on $(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D$},

(3.11)

where
\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{q}}:=\boldsymbol{q}/|\boldsymbol{q}|
is the unit outward normal vector to
\partial D
, and we impose the initial condition

\displaystyle\varphi(0,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})=\varphi_{0}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\quad\mbox{for $(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\in\Omega\times D$},

(3.12)

where
\varphi_{0}\geq 0
a.e. on
\overline{\Omega}\times D
.
From the perspective of mathematical modelling, this initial-boundary-value problem is equivalent to (
2.28
), (
2.32
), (
2.33
), (
2.34
) because, as noted above, the probability density function
\varphi
is by definition nonnegative and the absolute temperature
\theta
is, for physical reasons, positive.

In our initial step, Step 0, of the analysis that follows we shall prove that the assumed nonnegativity of
\varphi_{0}
a.e. on
\overline{\Omega}\times\overline{D}
implies nonnegativity of
\varphi
, viewed as a solution of the problem (
3
)–(
3.12
), a.e. on
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\overline{D}
. Using the nonnegativity of
\varphi
thus proved, we shall then prove in Step 1 that the positivity of the initial temperature
\theta_{0}
a.e. on
\overline{\Omega}
guarantees positivity of
\theta
a.e. on
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}
. Having proved the nonnegativity of
\varphi
and the positivity of
\theta
, the initial-boundary-value problem (
3
)–(
3.12
) will then collapse to its original form (
2.28
), (
2.32
), (
2.33
), (
2.34
), and in the remaining steps of the analysis we shall therefore continue working with that original form.

Step 0.
In this step we shall prove that
the assumed nonnegativity of
\varphi_{0}
implies the nonnegativity of
\varphi
, viewed as a solution of the problem (
3
)–(
3.12
), a.e. on
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\overline{D}
. To this end, we multiply the partial differential equation (
3
) by
[\varphi]_{-}
, integrate the resulting equality over
\Omega\times D
, and perform partial integration in all terms that involve
\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
and
\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{q}}
using the homogeneous boundary conditions (
3.10
) and (
3.11
). As
\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\,\boldsymbol{v}=0
and
\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{q}}((\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{q})=0
, it follows by decomposing
\varphi
as
[\varphi]_{+}+[\varphi]_{-}
that, for all
t\in(0,T)
,

\frac{1}{2}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\Omega\times D}|[\varphi]_{-}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Omega\times D}[\theta]_{+}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}[\varphi]_{-}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+4\int_{\Omega\times D}[\theta]_{+}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}[\varphi]_{-}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=0,

thanks to the fact that the interiors of the supports
\mathrm{supp}([\varphi]_{+})
and
\mathrm{supp}([\varphi]_{-})

of the functions
[\varphi]_{+}
and
[\varphi]_{-}
are disjoint sets. Hence,

\int_{\Omega\times D}|[\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})]_{-}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\leq\int_{\Omega\times D}[\varphi(0,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})]_{-}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=\int_{\Omega\times D}[\varphi_{0}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})]_{-}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=0

for all
t\in[0,T]
. Consequently
\varphi\geq 0
a.e. on
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\overline{D}
.

Step 1.
Having proved the nonnegativity of
\varphi
, we next establish a
minimum principle
for the absolute temperature,
\theta
. To this end, we choose
\theta_{\mathrm{min}}\in(0,1)
such that
\theta_{\mathrm{min}}\leq\mbox{ess.inf}_{x\in\Omega}\,\theta_{0}(x)
and consider the function
H
defined by

\displaystyle H(t,\boldsymbol{x}):=\theta(t,\boldsymbol{x})-\theta_{\mathrm{min}}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\quad\mbox{for $t\in[0,T]$ and $\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega$},

(3.13)

with
\alpha>0
to be fixed below. For future reference, we also note that, thanks to our assumptions concerning the functions
\mathcal{U}_{e}
and
\mathcal{U}_{\eta}
(cf. equations (
2
) and (
2.37
)), we have the following:

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}||\boldsymbol{q}|=U^{\prime}_{\eta}\left(\frac{|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}}{2}\right)|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}\leq C\quad\mbox{for all $\boldsymbol{q}\in D$}

and

\displaystyle 0\leq\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}

\displaystyle=|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}U_{\eta}^{\prime}\left(\frac{|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}}{2}\right)U_{e}^{\prime}\left(\frac{|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}}{2}\right)\leq C|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}\left(1+\left[U_{e}^{\prime}\left(\frac{|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}}{2}\right)\right]^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C(1+|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2})\quad\mbox{for all $\boldsymbol{q}\in D$}.

Furthermore, because

\displaystyle\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}=dU_{e}^{\prime}\left(\frac{|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}}{2}\right)+|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}U_{e}^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}}{2}\right),

and
U_{e}\in C^{2}([0,b/4])
, it follows from (
2
) that

\displaystyle 0\leq\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\leq C\quad\mbox{for $|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}\leq\frac{b}{2}$},

where
C
is a positive constant, independent of
\boldsymbol{q}
, and

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\leq\frac{d}{2}\left(1+\frac{2}{b}|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}\left|U_{e}^{\prime}\left(\frac{|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}}{2}\right)\right|^{2}\right)+|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}U_{e}^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{2}\left(1+\frac{2}{b}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\right)+|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}\left[U_{e}^{\prime}\left(\frac{|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}}{2}\right)\right]^{2}\left\{\left[U_{e}^{\prime}\left(\frac{|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}}{2}\right)\right]^{-2}U_{e}^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}}{2}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{2}\left(1+\frac{2}{b}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\right)+|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\left\{\left[U_{e}^{\prime}\left(\frac{|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}}{2}\right)\right]^{-2}U_{e}^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}}{2}\right)\right\}\quad\mbox{for $\frac{b}{2}\leq|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}<b$}.

Thanks to (
2
), the (nonnegative) expression appearing in the curly brackets in the last line is, for all
\boldsymbol{q}\in D
such that
\frac{b}{2}\leq|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}<b
, bounded by a positive constant independent of
\boldsymbol{q}
. In summary, then, writing
C
for a generic positive constant independent of
\boldsymbol{q}
, we have shown that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}||\boldsymbol{q}|&\leq C\quad&&\mbox{for all $\boldsymbol{q}\in D$},\\
0\leq\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}&\leq C(1+|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2})\quad&&\mbox{for all $\boldsymbol{q}\in D$},\\
0\leq\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}&\leq C(1+|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2})\quad&&\mbox{for all $\boldsymbol{q}\in D$}.\end{aligned}

(3.14)

With these preparations, we return to the temperature equation (
2.27
) subject to the boundary and initial conditions (
2.31
), multiply equation (
2.27
) by
(H(t,\boldsymbol{x}))_{-}:=\min\{H(t,\boldsymbol{x}),0\}~(\leq 0)
, where
H
is the function defined by (
