3.13
), integrate the resulting equality over
\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega
and perform partial integration over
D
in the first term of the first integral on the right-hand side of (
2.27
). Hence,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}\theta\,H_{-}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\{\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega\,:\,H(t,\boldsymbol{x})\leq 0\}}\kappa(\theta)|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Omega}2\nu(\theta)|H_{-}||\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&\qquad+4\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}|\int_{D}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+4\int_{\Omega}\theta|H_{-}|\int_{\partial D}\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\cdot{\boldsymbol{n}}_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathrm{d}S(\boldsymbol{q})\\
&\quad=4\int_{\Omega}\theta\,|H_{-}|\int_{D}(\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+4\int_{\Omega}\theta\,H_{-}\int_{D}(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&\qquad+\int_{\Omega}\theta\,H_{-}\,\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}):\int_{D}(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}=:\mathrm{T}_{1}+\mathrm{T}_{2}+\mathrm{T}_{3},\end{aligned}

(3.15)

where we have used the trivial equality
H_{-}=-|H_{-}|
. We proceed by bounding the three terms on the right-hand side of this equality. Clearly,

\theta|H_{-}|=(H+\theta_{\mathrm{min}}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t})|H_{-}|\leq\theta_{\mathrm{min}}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}|H_{-}|\leq|H_{-}|
for all
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in[0,T]\times\Omega
.
Therefore, by (
3.14
)
3
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{1}:=

\displaystyle 4\int_{\Omega}\theta\,|H_{-}|\int_{D}(\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\leq 4\int_{\{x\in\Omega\,:\,\theta\geq 0\}}\theta\,|H_{-}|\int_{D}(\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\quad\leq 4C\int_{\{x\in\Omega\,:\,\theta\geq 0\}}\theta\,|H_{-}|\int_{D}(1+|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\quad\leq 4C\theta_{\rm min}\int_{\{x\in\Omega\,:\,\theta\geq 0\}}\,|H_{-}|\,\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\int_{D}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+4C\theta_{\rm min}\int_{\{x\in\Omega\,:\,\theta\geq 0\}}\,|H_{-}|\int_{D}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\quad\leq 4C_{1}\theta_{\rm min}\int_{\Omega}\,|H_{-}|\,\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+4C\theta_{\rm min}\int_{\Omega}\,|H_{-}|\int_{D}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x},

where
C
here is the constant from inequality (
3.14
)
3
and

C_{1}:=C\,\max_{(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}}\int_{D}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

Next, we consider the second term on the right-hand side of the inequality (
3.15
). Clearly,

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{2}:=4\int_{\Omega}\theta\,H_{-}\int_{D}(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\leq\frac{1}{\theta_{\rm min}}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}|\,\theta^{2}\int_{D}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}|^{2}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle+4\theta_{\rm min}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}|\,\int_{D}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{2}}{\theta_{\rm min}}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}|\,\theta^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+4\theta_{\rm min}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}|\int_{D}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x},

where

C_{2}:=\max_{(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}}\int_{D}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}|^{2}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

Finally, we consider the third term on the right-hand side of (
3.15
):

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{3}:=\int_{\Omega}\theta\,H_{-}\,\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}):\int_{D}(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\leq\frac{C_{3}}{\theta_{\rm min}}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}|\,\theta^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\theta_{\rm\min}\int_{\Omega}2\nu(\theta)|H_{-}||\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x},

where

C_{3}:=\frac{1}{8}|D|\max_{(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}}\frac{1}{\nu(\theta)}\int_{D}|(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\otimes\boldsymbol{q}))\varphi|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

We then substitute these bounds on
\mathrm{T}_{1},\mathrm{T}_{2}
and

\mathrm{T}_{3}

in (
3.15
) and choose
\theta_{\rm min}\in(0,1)
so small that
(C+1)\theta_{\rm min}\leq 1
. This enables us to absorb the second term of the bound on
\mathrm{T}_{1}
and the second term of the bound on
\mathrm{T}_{2}
into the fourth term on the left-hand side of (
3.15
); it also enables us to absorb the second term in the bound on
\mathrm{T}_{3}
into the third term on the left-hand side of (
3.15
). We note further that
the final term on the left-hand side vanishes thanks to (
3.8
).

Hence, recalling the definition (
3.13
) of the function
H
, we have from (
3.15
)
the inequality

\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}\theta\left(\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{min}}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\right)_{-}\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\leq 4C_{1}\theta_{\rm min}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}|\,\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\frac{C_{2}+C_{3}}{\theta_{\rm min}}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}|\,\theta^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

Equivalently, we have the following inequality:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\Omega}\left[\left(\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{min}}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\right)_{-}\right]^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}-\alpha\theta_{\mathrm{min}}\int_{\Omega}\left(\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{min}}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\right)_{-}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 4C_{1}\theta_{\rm min}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}|\,\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\frac{C_{2}+C_{3}}{\theta_{\rm min}}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}|\,\theta^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x},

whereby, because

-\left(\theta-\theta_{\mathrm{min}}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\right)_{-}=-H_{-}=|H_{-}|,

also

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\Omega}(H_{-})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\leq 4C_{1}\theta_{\rm min}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}|\,\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\frac{C_{2}+C_{3}}{\theta_{\rm min}}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}|\,\theta^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}-\alpha\theta_{\mathrm{min}}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}|\,\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle=(4C_{1}-\alpha)\,\theta_{\rm min}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}|\,\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\frac{C_{2}+C_{3}}{\theta_{\rm min}}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}|\,\theta^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

We shall now focus our attention on the last term on the right-hand side of this inequality.

If
\theta(t,\boldsymbol{x})\geq 0

at a point
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}
, then

0\leq\theta(t,\boldsymbol{x})=\theta(t,\boldsymbol{x})-\theta_{\rm min}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}+\theta_{\rm min}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}=H(t,\boldsymbol{x})+\theta_{\rm min}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}.

Consequently, because
|H_{-}|\,H\leq 0
,

|H_{-}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|\,\theta(t,\boldsymbol{x})\leq\theta_{\rm min}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\,|H_{-}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|,

whereby also

|H_{-}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|\,\theta^{2}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\leq\theta_{\rm min}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\,|H_{-}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|\,\theta(t,\boldsymbol{x})\leq\theta_{\rm min}^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-2\alpha t}\,|H_{-}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|\leq\theta_{\rm min}^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\,|H_{-}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|,

with a strict inequality when
H(t,\boldsymbol{x})<0
and equality when
H(t,\boldsymbol{x})\geq 0
.

If, on the other hand
\theta(t,\boldsymbol{x})<0
, then letting

C_{\ast}:=\frac{1}{\theta_{\rm min}}\mathrm{e}^{\alpha T}\max_{(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}}|\theta(t,\boldsymbol{x})|^{2},

we have

\theta^{2}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\leq\max_{(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}}|\theta(t,\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}\leq C_{\ast}\theta_{\rm min}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t},

whereby also

\theta^{2}(t,\boldsymbol{x})+C_{\ast}\theta(t,\boldsymbol{x})<C_{\ast}\theta_{\rm min}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}.

Thus,

\theta^{2}(t,\boldsymbol{x})<C_{\ast}(\theta_{\rm min}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}-\theta(t,\boldsymbol{x}))=C_{\ast}(-H(t,\boldsymbol{x}))=C_{\ast}\,|H_{-}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|,

and therefore also

|H_{-}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|\,\theta^{2}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\leq C_{\ast}|H_{-}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|^{2},

with a strict inequality when
H(t,\boldsymbol{x})<0
and equality when
H(t,\boldsymbol{x})\geq 0
.
Hence, for each
t\in(0,T)
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\leq(4C_{1}-\alpha)\,\theta_{\rm min}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|\,\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\frac{C_{2}+C_{3}}{\theta_{\rm min}}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|\,\theta^{2}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle=(4C_{1}-\alpha)\,\theta_{\rm min}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|\,\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{C_{2}+C_{3}}{\theta_{\rm min}}\int_{\{\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega\,:\,\theta(t,\boldsymbol{x})\geq 0\}}|H_{-}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|\,\theta^{2}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{C_{2}+C_{3}}{\theta_{\rm min}}\int_{\{\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega\,:\,\theta(t,\boldsymbol{x})<0\}}|H_{-}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|\,\theta^{2}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\leq(4C_{1}-\alpha)\,\theta_{\rm min}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|\,\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\qquad+(C_{2}+C_{3})\,\theta_{\rm min}\,\int_{\{\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega\,:\,\theta(t,\boldsymbol{x})\geq 0\}}|H_{-}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|\,\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{C_{2}+C_{3}}{\theta_{\rm min}}C_{\ast}\int_{\{\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega\,:\,\theta(t,\boldsymbol{x})<0\}}|H_{-}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\leq(4C_{1}+C_{2}+C_{3}-\alpha)\,\theta_{\rm min}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|\,\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{C_{2}+C_{3}}{\theta_{\rm min}}C_{\ast}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

Now we fix the value of the constant
\alpha
by setting
\alpha=2(4C_{1}+C_{2}+C_{3})>0
. Thus,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{\alpha}{2}\,\theta_{\rm min}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}|\,\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\frac{C_{2}+C_{3}}{\theta_{\rm min}}C_{\ast}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{2}+C_{3}}{\theta_{\rm min}}C_{\ast}\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

As
|H_{-}(0,\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}=((\theta(0,\boldsymbol{x})-\theta_{\mathrm{min}})_{-})^{2}=((\theta_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})-\theta_{\mathrm{min}})_{-})^{2}=0
for all
\boldsymbol{x}\in\overline{\Omega}
, it follows by Gronwall’s lemma that

\int_{\Omega}|H_{-}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\leq 0

for all
t\in[0,T]
. Thus, we deduce that

H_{-}(t,\boldsymbol{x})=0\quad\mbox{for all $(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in\overline{Q}$}.

In other words,
H\geq 0
on
\overline{Q}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\theta(t,\boldsymbol{x})\geq\theta_{\mathrm{min}}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\geq\theta_{\mathrm{min}}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha T}\,\,(>0)\quad\mbox{for all $(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in\overline{Q}$},

(3.16)

with
\theta_{\mathrm{min}}\in(0,1)
and
\alpha=2(4C_{1}+C_{2}+C_{3})
, where
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}>0
are as above. Thus, we have established a strictly positive lower bound on
\theta
over
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}
.

Step 2.

Next, we bound the
polymer number density

n_{\mathrm{P}}=\int_{D}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}
(cf. (
2.19
) and (
2.29
)). We integrate the Fokker–Planck equation (
2.28
) over
D
and use the assumed zero normal flux boundary condition (
2.33
) on
(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D
to deduce that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left(\int_{D}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)+\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left(\boldsymbol{v}\left(\int_{D}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\right)-\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left(\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left(\int_{D}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=0\quad\mbox{in $(0,T)\times\Omega\times D$},

(3.17)

subject to the following zero normal flux boundary condition for the function
\int_{D}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}
(which arises from integrating over the set
D
the zero normal flux boundary condition (
2.32
) for
\varphi
, imposed on
(0,T)\times\partial\Omega\times D
):

\displaystyle\left(\boldsymbol{v}\left(\int_{D}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)-\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left(\int_{D}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=0\quad\mbox{on $(0,T)\times\partial\Omega\times D$}

(3.18)

and the initial condition

\left(\int_{D}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)(0,\boldsymbol{x})=\int_{D}\varphi_{0}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\quad\mbox{for $\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega$},

obtained by integrating the initial condition (
2.34
) imposed on
\varphi
over the set
D
.
By the parabolic maximum principle applied to the function
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\mapsto\int_{D}\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}
(recall from Step 1 that
\theta
is strictly positive on
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}
, which guarantees parabolicity of the partial differential equation (
3.17
) as an evolution equation for the function
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\to\int_{D}\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}
), we then have, for all
t\in[0,T]
, the inequality

\displaystyle\underset{x\in\Omega}{\mbox{ess.inf}}\int_{D}\varphi_{0}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\leq\int_{D}\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\leq\underset{x\in\Omega}{\mbox{ess.sup}}\int_{D}\varphi_{0}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

(3.19)

Thanks to the nonnegativity of
\varphi
(cf. Step 0), it follows from (
3.19
) that

\displaystyle\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(Q;L^{1}(D))}\leq\|\varphi_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega;L^{1}(D))}

(3.20)

where, as previously,
Q:=(0,T)\times\Omega
.

Step 3.

As a first step towards deriving evolution equations for the internal energy
e
and the specific entropy
\eta
, defined, respectively, by

e:=\theta+\int_{D}\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\eta:=\log\theta-\int_{D}[\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})\varphi+\varphi\log\varphi]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q},

(cf. (
2.8
) and (
2.7
)) recall from (
2.27
) that the evolution equation for
\theta
is

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\theta+\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{v}\theta)&-\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\kappa(\theta)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta)\\
&=2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}+4\int_{D}\left(\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e})\varphi+|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\varphi\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\\
&\quad+\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}):\int_{D}\theta(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.\end{aligned}

(3.21)

Dividing (
3.21
) by
\theta
(note that Step 1 guarantees that
\theta
is bounded below by a positive constant throughout
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}
) then gives

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\log\theta+\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{v}\log\theta)-\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\kappa(\theta)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\log\theta)=\frac{2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}}{\theta}+\frac{\kappa(\theta)|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2}}\\
&\quad+\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}):\int_{D}(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+4\int_{D}\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\varphi(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e})+\frac{\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}}{\theta}\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.\end{aligned}

(3.22)

Step 4.

As a second step towards deriving an evolution equation for the internal energy we multiply the Fokker–Planck equation (
2.28
) by
\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})
and integrate the resulting equality over
D
. Partial integration with respect to
\boldsymbol{q}
in the final term then gives

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\left(\int_{D}\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)+\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left(\boldsymbol{v}\int_{D}\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)&-\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left(\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\int_{D}\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\\
&-\int_{D}\left((\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi+\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}\right)\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}=0,\end{aligned}

(3.23)

where

\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}:=-4\boldsymbol{F}\varphi-4\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi=-4\varphi(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta})-4\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi.

Because
e:=\theta+\int_{D}\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}
, summing (
3.21
) and (
3.23
) and recalling the definition (
2.20
) of the Cauchy stress tensor, we find that
e
satisfies the partial differential equation (
2.1
)
3
; that is,

\partial_{t}e+\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{v}e)+\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left(-\kappa(\theta)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta-\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\int_{D}\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)=\mathbb{T}:\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})\quad\mbox{in $(0,T)\times\Omega$},

where the expression in parentheses in the last term on the left-hand side is what was defined in (
2.18
) as the
energy flux
\boldsymbol{j}_{e}=\boldsymbol{j}_{e}(t,\boldsymbol{x})
.

Step 5.

As a second step towards deriving an evolution equation for the specific entropy, we return to the Fokker–Planck equation (
2.28
), but this time we multiply it by
\mathcal{U}_{\eta}
and again integrate the resulting equality over
D
and perform partial integration; hence, we deduce that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left(\int_{D}\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)+\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left(\boldsymbol{v}\int_{D}\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)-\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left(\theta\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\int_{D}\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{D}\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}):(\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}-4\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\varphi+\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi)\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{D}\left[\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}):(\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta})\varphi-4\varphi(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta})-4\theta\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}|^{2}-4\theta(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta})\right]\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q},

(3.24)

by recalling the definition (
2.2
) of the spring force
\boldsymbol{F}
.

Step 6.

As a third step towards deriving an evolution equation for the specific entropy, we multiply the Fokker–Planck equation by
(1+\log(\varphi+\sigma))\zeta
, where
\sigma>0
and
\zeta\in C^{\infty}_{0}(Q)
, integrate the resulting equality over
Q\times D
, perform partial integration with respect to
t
and
\boldsymbol{x}
using that
\zeta
is smooth with compact support, and also with respect to
\boldsymbol{q}
using the zero normal flux boundary conditions imposed on
\varphi
. We then pass to the limit
\sigma\to 0_{+}
in the resulting equality, term by term, using the monotone/dominated convergence theorem. Having passed to the limit
\sigma\to 0
, we reverse the partial integration with respect to
t
and
\boldsymbol{x}
using the assumed smoothness of
\varphi
. Since the resulting equality holds for all test functions
\zeta\in C^{\infty}_{0}(Q)
, it follows that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left(\int_{D}\varphi\log\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)+\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left(\boldsymbol{v}\int_{D}\varphi\log\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)-\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left(\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\int_{D}\varphi\log\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{D}\left[\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}:(\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi)-4\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi-4\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi-4\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}-\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\right]\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{D}\left[-4\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi-4\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi-4\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}-\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\right]\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q},

(3.25)

where in the transition to the last line we made use of the fact that

\int_{D}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}:(\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}:\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}=-(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}:\mathbb{I})\int_{D}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}=-(\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\int_{D}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}=0,

by partial integration with respect to
\boldsymbol{q}
, using the implicitly imposed boundary condition (
3.2
), and recalling the divergence-free property (
2.25
) of the velocity field
\boldsymbol{v}
.

Step 7.

We shall now combine Steps 3, 5 and 6 to infer the evolution equation for the specific entropy
\eta
. Subtracting (
3
) and (
3
) from (
3.22
) and recalling the definition (
2.7
) of the specific entropy
\eta
, it follows that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\eta+\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{v}\eta)+\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left(\theta\int_{D}\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\int_{D}\varphi\log\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)-\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left(\frac{\kappa(\theta)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta}{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}}{\theta}+\frac{\kappa(\theta)|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2}}+\theta\int_{D}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+4\theta\int_{D}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle\qquad+4\int_{D}\left[\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\varphi}{\theta}+2(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e})+2\varphi(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e})+2\theta(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta})+\theta\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}|^{2}\right]\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}}{\theta}+\frac{\kappa(\theta)|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2}}+\theta\int_{D}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+\int_{D}\frac{4}{\theta\varphi}\left|\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right|^{2}\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

(3.26)

Step 8.
Next, we shall establish the balance of total energy. We take the scalar product of (
2.26
) with
\boldsymbol{v}
, integrate the resulting equality over
(0,t)\times\Omega
, where
t\in(0,T)
, and perform partial integration with respect to
\boldsymbol{x}
using (
2.30
). We then combine this with (
3.21
) integrated over
(0,t)\times\Omega
after performing partial integration using the boundary condition (
2.31
), and with (
3.23
) integrated over
(0,t)\times\Omega
after performing partial integration using the boundary condition (
2.32
), to deduce that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{v}(t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Omega}\theta(t)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Omega\times D}\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi(t)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&\qquad=\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Omega}\theta_{0}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Omega\times D}\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi_{0}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{f}(s)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}(s)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s.\end{aligned}

(3.27)

Looking back at the definition (
2.8
) of the internal energy
e=e(t,\boldsymbol{x})
and of the total energy
E
defined in (
2.9
), i.e.,

E(t,\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{v}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}+e(t,\boldsymbol{x})=\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{v}(t,\boldsymbol{x})|^{2}+\theta(t,\boldsymbol{x})+\int_{D}\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q},

one sees that the process leading to (
3.27
) coincides with the integration of the evolution equation (
2.10
) for the total energy
E
, first over
\Omega
in conjunction with the use of the divergence theorem and the relevant boundary conditions to deduce (
2.12
), and then the integration of (
2.12
) over
t
, which yields

\int_{\Omega}E(t,\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=\int_{\Omega}E(0,\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{f}(s,\boldsymbol{x})\cdot\boldsymbol{v}(s,\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s.

Comparing this with (
3.27
), we see that the equality (
3.27
) derived above expresses the balance of total energy, in fact.

Applying Gronwall’s inequality to (
3.27
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\underset{t\in(0,T)}{\mbox{ess.sup}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\theta(t)\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}+\|\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi\|_{L^{1}(\Omega\times D)}\right)\\
&\quad\leq\mathrm{e}^{T}\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}+\|\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega\times D)}+\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Q)}\right)\leq C.\end{aligned}

(3.28)

Here and hereafter
C
signifies a generic positive constant that depends only on the data.

Step 9.

In this crucial step, we derive the formal energy equality that lies at the heart of the compactness argument presented in Section
4
. We multiply (
3
) by
-1
and integrate the resulting equality over
\Omega
. Using the boundary condition (
2.30
) to eliminate the boundary integral originating from the second term on the left-hand side of (
3
) upon integration over
\Omega
, the boundary condition (
2.32
) to eliminate the boundary integral that comes from integrating the third term on the left-hand side of (
3
) over
\Omega
, and the boundary condition (
2.31
) to eliminate the boundary integral that stems from integrating the fourth term on the left-hand side of (
3
) over
\Omega
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\Omega}-\eta\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}}{\theta}+\frac{\kappa(\theta)|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2}}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&\quad+\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\theta\int_{D}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+\int_{D}\frac{4}{\theta\varphi}\left|\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right|^{2}\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=0.\end{aligned}

(3.29)

We note that because the second and third terms on the left-hand side of (
3.29
) are nonnegative, the
total entropy
,
t\in[0,T]\mapsto\int_{\Omega}\eta(t,\boldsymbol{x})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}
, is an increasing function of
t
, as is physically expected.

Thanks to the definition (
2.7
) of the specific entropy
\eta
we have

-\eta=-\log\theta+\int_{D}[\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi+\varphi\log\varphi]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

By substituting this into (
3.29
) it follows that

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\Omega}\bigg[-\log\theta+\int_{D}[\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi+\varphi\log\varphi]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}}{\theta}+\frac{\kappa(\theta)|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2}}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\theta\int_{D}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+\int_{D}\frac{4}{\theta\varphi}\left|\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right|^{2}\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=0.

By integrating equation (
3.17
) over the domain
\Omega
and using the boundary condition (
3.18
), it follows that
\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\Omega\times D}(-\varphi+1)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=0
; therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\Omega}\bigg[-\log\theta+\int_{D}\big[\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi+[\varphi(\log\varphi-1)+1]\big]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}}{\theta}+\frac{\kappa(\theta)|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2}}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\theta\int_{D}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+\int_{D}\frac{4}{\theta\varphi}\left|\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right|^{2}\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=0.

Defining

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(s):=s-1-\log s\quad\mbox{for $s>0$}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\mathcal{F}(s):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}s(\log s-1)+1&\mbox{for $s>0$,}\\
1&\mbox{for $s=0$,}\end{array}\right.

we then have

\displaystyle\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\mathcal{H}(\theta)+\int_{D}[\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi+\mathcal{F}(\varphi)]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\!\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}}{\theta}+\frac{\kappa(\theta)|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2}}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\theta\int_{D}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+\int_{D}\frac{4}{\theta\varphi}\left|\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right|^{2}\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\Omega}(\theta-1)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\Omega}\theta\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

Note that
s\mapsto\mathcal{H}(s)
and
s\mapsto\mathcal{F}(s)
are strictly convex nonnegative functions of
s
, which only vanish at
s=1
. By integrating this equality with respect to
t
, it follows that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\mathcal{H}(\theta(t))+\int_{D}[\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi(t)+\mathcal{F}(\varphi(t))]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{0}^{t}\!\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}}{\theta}+\frac{\kappa(\theta)|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2}}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\theta\int_{D}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+\int_{D}\frac{4}{\theta\varphi}\left|\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right|^{2}\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\mathcal{H}(\theta_{0})+\int_{D}[\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi_{0}+\mathcal{F}(\varphi_{0})]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Omega}\theta(t)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}-\int_{\Omega}\theta_{0}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

Adding this to the balance of total energy stated in (
3.27
) then yields the following
energy equality
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{v}(t)|^{2}+\mathcal{H}(\theta(t))\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Omega\times D}[\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi(t)+\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi(t)+\mathcal{F}(\varphi(t))]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&\qquad+\int_{0}^{t}\!\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}}{\theta}+\frac{\kappa(\theta)|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2}}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\qquad+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\theta\int_{D}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+\int_{D}\frac{4}{\theta\varphi}\left|\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right|^{2}\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&=\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}|^{2}+\mathcal{H}(\theta_{0})\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Omega\times D}[\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi_{0}+\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi_{0}+\mathcal{F}(\varphi_{0})]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{f}(s)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}(s)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s.\end{aligned}

(3.32)

This formal energy equality will play a crucial role in Section
4
. In particular, once a solution is known to satisfy (
3.32
) (or a weaker version of (
3.32
), with the equality sign replaced by the symbol
\leq
), it will automatically follow that one can meet the hypotheses of Lemma
1
, as a consequence of which the functions
\varphi
and
\varphi\,\mathcal{U}_{e}
will be forced to have zero trace on
(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D
. In other words, instead of being explicitly imposed, these homogeneous boundary conditions for
\varphi
and
\varphi\,\mathcal{U}_{e}
will be implied by the energy equality (or an energy inequality, where the equality sign in (
3.32
) is replaced by the symbol
\leq
).

The energy equality (
3.32
) further implies that

\displaystyle\underset{t\in(0,T)}{\mbox{ess.sup}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\mathcal{H}(\theta(t))\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}+\|\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi(t)\|_{L^{1}(\Omega\times D)}+\|\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi(t)\|_{L^{1}(\Omega\times D)}+\|\mathcal{F}(\varphi(t))\|_{L^{1}(\Omega\times D)}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{0}^{T}\|\log\theta\|^{2}_{W^{1,2}(\Omega)}+\|\theta^{\beta/2}\|^{2}_{W^{1,2}(\Omega)}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{Q}\frac{2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}}{\theta}+\frac{\kappa(\theta)|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{Q}\left[\theta\int_{D}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+\int_{D}\frac{4}{\theta\varphi}\left|\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right|^{2}\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right]\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\bigg(\,\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\mathcal{H}(\theta_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}+\|\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega\times D)}+\|\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega\times D)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\|\mathcal{F}(\varphi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\Omega\times D)}+\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Q)}\bigg)\leq C,

(3.33)

where
\beta>\frac{5}{6}
, as was assumed in (
2.38
). In connection with the final integral on the left-hand side of (
3
) we note that

\displaystyle\int_{Q\times D}\frac{1}{\theta\varphi}\left|\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right|^{2}\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{Q\times D}\frac{1}{\theta\varphi}\left|\theta\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\left(\varphi\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}\right)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Hence, by defining the rescaled probability density function

\displaystyle\widehat{\varphi}:=\varphi\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}},

(3.34)

we then have from (
3
) that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{Q\times D}\frac{1}{\theta\varphi}\left|\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right|^{2}\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\quad=\int_{Q\times D}\theta\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\widehat{\varphi}|^{2}}{\widehat{\varphi}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C.\end{aligned}

(3.35)

Furthermore, recalling the definition of
\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}
from (
2.16
), we have from (
3.35
) that

\displaystyle\int_{Q\times D}\frac{1}{\theta\varphi}\,|\,\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C.

(3.36)

We also note that by adding
\int_{\Omega}1\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=|\Omega|
to both sides of the energy equality (
3
), the norm
\|\mathcal{H}(\theta(t))\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}
on the left-hand side of (
3
) is replaced by
\int_{\Omega}(\theta(t)-\log\theta(t))\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}
and
\|\mathcal{H}(\theta_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}
on the right-hand side is replaced by
\int_{\Omega}(\theta_{0}-\log\theta_{0})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}
. Because

|\theta_{0}-\log\theta_{0}|\leq\theta_{0}+|\log\theta_{0}|\leq\theta_{0}+\max\{\theta_{0},|\log\theta_{\mathrm{min}}|\}

(cf. Step 1 for the choice of
\theta_{\mathrm{min}}\in(0,1)
), and

\theta(t)-\log\theta(t)\geq\frac{1}{4}(\theta(t)+|\log(\theta(t))|\geq 0,

it follows in particular that

\underset{t\in(0,T)}{\mbox{ess.sup}}\left(\|\theta(t)\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}+\|\log\theta(t)\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\right)\leq C.

Step 10.

We note at this point in relation to the integral

\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

appearing in the definition of the Cauchy stress tensor
\mathbb{T}
that because the function
\theta
is independent of the configuration space variable
\boldsymbol{q}\in D
, recalling the definition (
3.34
) of the rescaled probability density function
\widehat{\varphi}
, this integral can be rewritten in an equivalent form as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}&=\theta\int_{D}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}\otimes\boldsymbol{q}\right)\varphi\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\\
&=-\theta\int_{D}\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}\right)\otimes\left(\boldsymbol{q}\,\varphi\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\\
&=d\,\theta\left(\int_{D}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}\varphi\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\,\mathbb{I}+\theta\int_{D}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\left(\varphi\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}\right)\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\\
&=d\,\theta\,n_{\text{p}}\,\mathbb{I}+\theta\int_{D}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\widehat{\varphi}\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\\
&=d\,\theta\,n_{\text{p}}\,\mathbb{I}+\theta\int_{D}\sqrt{\widehat{\varphi}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}\left(\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\widehat{\varphi}}{\sqrt{\widehat{\varphi}}}\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\\
&=d\,\theta\,n_{\text{p}}\,\mathbb{I}+\int_{D}\sqrt{\theta}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{2\theta}}\,\sqrt{\widehat{\varphi}}\left(\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\sqrt{\theta}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{2\theta}}\,\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\widehat{\varphi}}{\sqrt{\widehat{\varphi}}}\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\\
&=d\,\theta\,n_{\text{p}}\,\mathbb{I}+\int_{D}\sqrt{\theta}\,\sqrt{\varphi}\left(\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\sqrt{\theta}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{2\theta}}\,\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\widehat{\varphi}}{\sqrt{\widehat{\varphi}}}\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.\end{aligned}

(3.37)

In the transition from the second displayed line to the third displayed line we performed partial integration with respect to
\boldsymbol{q}
and made use of the implicitly imposed homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition (
3.2
). Since, by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and the bound (
3.35
),

\displaystyle\int_{Q}\int_{D}\left|\sqrt{\theta}\,\sqrt{\varphi}\left(\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\sqrt{\theta}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{2\theta}}\,\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\widehat{\varphi}}{\sqrt{\widehat{\varphi}}}\right)\right|\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\left(\int_{Q}\theta\left(\int_{D}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\times\left(\int_{Q\times D}\left|\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\sqrt{\theta}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{2\theta}}\,\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\widehat{\varphi}}{\sqrt{\widehat{\varphi}}}\right|^{2}\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{b}\left(\int_{Q}\theta\,n_{\text{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{Q\times D}\theta\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\widehat{\varphi}|^{2}}{\widehat{\varphi}}\,\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C\sqrt{b}\left(\int_{Q}\theta\,n_{\text{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where, as before,
n_{\text{p}}(t,\boldsymbol{x}):=\int_{D}\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}
and, by (
3.20
),

\left(\int_{Q}\theta\,n_{\text{p}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\|\varphi_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(Q;L^{1}(D))}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{Q}\theta\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

it follows from the bound on the second term on the left-hand side of inequality (
3.28
) that

\sqrt{\theta}\,\sqrt{\varphi}\left(\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\sqrt{\theta}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{2\theta}}\,\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\widehat{\varphi}}{\sqrt{\widehat{\varphi}}}\right)\in L^{1}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega\times D;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})).

Similarly,

d\,\theta\,n_{\text{p}}\,\mathbb{I}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})).

Thus, the polymeric contributions to the Cauchy stress tensor
\mathbb{T}
, that is, the tensors

d\,\theta\,n_{\text{p}\,}\mathbb{I}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q},

belong to
L^{1}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}))
, and therefore they are well-defined under the stated assumptions on the data in the sense that they have finite Frobenius norm, a.e. on
Q
. We note in passing that the right-hand side of (
3.37
) can be further transformed as follows:

\displaystyle\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}=d\,\theta\,n_{\text{p}}\,\mathbb{I}+\theta\int_{D}\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\log\widehat{\varphi}\otimes\boldsymbol{q}\right)\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

(3.38)

Step 11.

To proceed, we require a bound on
\theta
in the norm of
L^{r}(Q)=L^{r}(0,T;L^{r}(\Omega))
, for a suitable
r>1
. We begin by noting that the bounds (
3.28
) and (
3
) yield

\displaystyle\|\theta\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))}\leq C\quad\mbox{and}\quad\|\theta^{\beta/2}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega))}\leq C,

(3.39)

where
C
is a positive constant that depends only on the data, and
\beta>\frac{5}{6}
.
As
\beta>\frac{5}{6}>\frac{1}{3}
, it follows that
1<\frac{2}{3}+\beta<3\beta
, so we can interpolate between
L^{1}(\Omega)
and
L^{3\beta}(\Omega)
to obtain the following bound in
L^{\frac{2}{3}+\beta}(Q)=L^{\frac{2}{3}+\beta}(0,T;L^{\frac{2}{3}+\beta}(\Omega))
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{0}^{T}\|\theta\|_{L^{\frac{2}{3}+\beta}(\Omega)}^{\frac{2}{3}+\beta}\,\mathrm{d}t&\leq\int_{0}^{T}\|\theta\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}^{\frac{2}{3}}\|\theta\|_{L^{3\beta}(\Omega)}^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq\|\theta\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))}^{\frac{2}{3}}\int_{0}^{T}\|\theta\|_{L^{3\beta}(\Omega)}^{\beta}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\|\theta\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))}^{\frac{2}{3}}\int_{0}^{T}\|\theta^{\frac{\beta}{2}}\|_{L^{6}(\Omega)}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq C\|\theta\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))}^{\frac{2}{3}}\int_{0}^{T}\|\theta^{\frac{\beta}{2}}\|_{W^{1,2}(\Omega)}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C,\end{aligned}

(3.40)

where in the transition to the last inequality, we have used the bounds of (
3.39
).

Step 12.

Next, we derive an additional bound on the velocity field
\boldsymbol{v}
. To this end, we define

p:=2-\frac{6}{5+3\beta}~.

Again, because
\beta>\frac{5}{6}>\frac{1}{3}
, it follows that
p\in(1,2)
. Hence, by Young’s inequality, the bound (
3
), and the assumed strict positivity of the shear viscosity coefficient
\nu(\theta)
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{Q}|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t&=\int_{Q}\left(\frac{|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}}{\theta}\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}\theta^{\frac{p}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq\frac{p}{2}\int_{Q}\frac{|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}}{\theta}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{2-p}{2}\int_{Q}\theta^{\frac{p}{2-p}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq C+\frac{3}{5+3\beta}\int_{Q}\theta^{\frac{2-\frac{6}{5+3\beta}}{2-2+\frac{6}{5+3\beta}}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=C+\frac{3}{5+3\beta}\int_{Q}\theta^{\frac{2}{3}+\beta}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C,\end{aligned}

(3.41)

where the last inequality follows from the bound (
3.40
).

Our assumption that
\beta>\frac{5}{6}
guarantees that, with
p
as defined above,
\boldsymbol{v}\in L^{\frac{5p}{3}}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\frac{5p}{3}>2
. Indeed, because
\boldsymbol{v}\in L^{p}(0,T;W^{1,p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
by parabolic interpolation
6
6

6

Lemma (Parabolic Interpolation, cf. Thm. 2.1 in
[
20
]
). Suppose that
\Omega
is a bounded open Lipschitz domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d\geq 2
. Let
p
,
q\in[1,\infty)
,
s\in[p,\infty)
, and suppose that

\gamma:=\frac{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{s}}{\frac{1}{d}-\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{p}}\in[0,1].

Then, there exists a constant
c>0
such that

\|v\|_{L^{r}(0,T;L^{s}(\Omega))}\leq c\|\nabla v\|^{\gamma}_{L^{q}(Q)}\|v\|^{1-\gamma}_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))}

for all
v\in L^{q}(0,T;W^{1,q}(\Omega))\cap L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))
, with

r:=\frac{s(q(p+d)-dp)}{(s-p)d}\in(1,\infty].

we have that

\displaystyle\int_{Q}|\boldsymbol{v}|^{\frac{5p}{3}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C,

where
\frac{5p}{3}=\frac{10}{3}\frac{2+3\beta}{5+3\beta}>2
thanks to our assumption that
\beta>\frac{5}{6}
.

Step 13.

We proceed by deriving a bound on
\mathbb{T}
. Because by hypothesis
\nu(\cdot)
is bounded from below and from above by positive constants, (
3.41
) implies that the term
\nu(\theta)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})
is bounded in
L^{p}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
with
p
as defined above. Next, we bound the polymeric part of the stress tensor. Recall from (
3.37
) that

\displaystyle\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle=d\,\theta\,n_{\mathrm{P}}\,\mathbb{I}+\int_{D}\sqrt{\theta}\,\sqrt{\varphi}\left(\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\sqrt{\theta}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{2\theta}}\,\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\widehat{\varphi}}{\sqrt{\widehat{\varphi}}}\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle=d\,\theta\,n_{\mathrm{P}}\,\mathbb{I}+\int_{D}\underbrace{\left(\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\widehat{\varphi}}{\sqrt{\widehat{\varphi}}}\sqrt{\theta\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}\otimes\boldsymbol{q}\right)}_{z_{1}}\underbrace{\sqrt{\widehat{\varphi}\,\theta\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}}_{z_{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q},

where
\widehat{\varphi}
is the rescaled probability density function defined in (
4.8
).
Thanks to (
3.35
), the term
z_{1}
belongs to
L^{2}(Q\times D;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
. Also,

\displaystyle\int_{Q\times D}z_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{Q\times D}\varphi\,\theta\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{Q}\theta\left(\int_{D}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{Q}\theta\,n_{\mathrm{P}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\|n_{\mathrm{P}}\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)}\int_{Q}\theta\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C,

whereby
z_{2}\in L^{2}(Q\times D)
. Hence, for a suitable
q\in(1,2)
, to be fixed below,

\displaystyle\int_{Q}\left|\theta\int_{D}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\widehat{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{Q}\left|\int_{D}z_{1}z_{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{Q}\left(\int_{D}z_{1}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)^{\frac{q}{2}}\left(\int_{D}z_{2}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)^{\frac{q}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{Q}\left(\int_{D}z_{1}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)^{\frac{q}{2}}\left(n_{\mathrm{P}}\,\theta\right)^{\frac{q}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{Q\times D}z_{1}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{q}{2}}\left(\int_{Q}(n_{\mathrm{P}}\,\theta)^{\frac{q}{2-q}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{2-q}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{Q\times D}z_{1}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{q}{2}}\|n_{\mathrm{P}}\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)}^{\frac{q}{2}}\left(\int_{Q}\theta^{\frac{q}{2-q}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{2-q}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\int_{Q}\theta^{\frac{q}{2-q}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{2-q}{2}}.

Because, thanks to (
3.40
),
\theta\in L^{\frac{2}{3}+\beta}(Q)
, we choose
q\in(1,2)
by setting

\frac{q}{2-q}=\frac{2}{3}+\beta.

Expressing
q
in terms of
\beta
from this equality, it follows that

q=\frac{4+6\beta}{5+3\beta}=2-\frac{6}{5+3\beta}=p.

Consequently,

\displaystyle\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\in L^{p}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})\quad\mbox{with}\quad p=2-\frac{6}{5+3\beta},\quad\beta>\frac{5}{6}.

Recall that

\mathbb{T}:=-\mathrm{p}\mathbb{I}+2\nu(\theta)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})-2\theta n_{\mathrm{P}}\mathbb{I}+\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

Hence, the sum of the second and fourth terms of
\mathbb{T}
belongs to
L^{p}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
. That is,

\displaystyle\int_{Q}|\mathbb{S}|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{Q}|\mathbb{T}+\mathrm{p}\mathbb{I}+2\theta n_{\mathrm{P}}\mathbb{I}|^{p}

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\quad\mbox{for}\quad p=2-\frac{6}{5+3\beta}\quad\mbox{with}\quad\beta>\frac{5}{6}.

Furthermore, since for
\beta>\frac{1}{3}
(which holds in our case, since it was assumed that
\beta>\frac{5}{6}
) one has

\frac{2}{3}+\beta>2-\frac{6}{5+3\beta}=p,

it follows that

2\theta n_{\mathrm{P}}\mathbb{I}\in L^{\frac{2}{3}+\beta}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})\subset L^{p}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}),

and therefore also

\displaystyle\int_{Q}|\mathbb{T}+\mathrm{p}\mathbb{I}|^{p}

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\quad\mbox{for}\quad p=2-\frac{6}{5+3\beta}\quad\mbox{with}\quad\beta>\frac{5}{6}.

Step 14.

Next, we derive bounds on the probability density function
\varphi
. We begin by noting that, thanks to (
3.20
), (
3.28
), (
3
) and (
3.36
) we have that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(Q;L^{1}(D))}+\|\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega\times D))}+\|\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega\times D))}\\
&\quad+\|\varphi\log\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega\times D))}+\left\|\frac{\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}}{\sqrt{\theta\varphi}}\right\|_{L^{2}(Q\times D)}+\left\|\sqrt{\frac{\theta}{\varphi}}\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi\right\|_{L^{2}(Q\times D)}\leq C.\end{aligned}

(3.42)

Suppose that
\widetilde{D}
is an arbitrary open subset of
D
such that
\widetilde{D}\subset\joinrel\subset D
(that is, the closure of
\widetilde{D}
is contained in the open ball
D
). Let us define

K_{\widetilde{D}}:=\max\{\sup_{\boldsymbol{q}\in\widetilde{D}}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})|,\,\sup_{\boldsymbol{q}\in\widetilde{D}}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})|\}\,\,(<\infty)

and note the elementary inequality
|a+b+c|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}|a|^{2}-2(|b|^{2}+|c|^{2})
for
a,b,c\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}\frac{1}{\theta\varphi}\left|\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi+\theta\varphi(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta})+\varphi(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e})\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\qquad\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t-2K_{\widetilde{D}}^{2}\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}\varphi\left(\theta+\frac{1}{\theta}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t.\end{aligned}

(3.43)

Now,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}\varphi\left(\theta+\frac{1}{\theta}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{Q}\left(\theta+\frac{1}{\theta}\right)\left(\int_{\widetilde{D}}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{Q}\left(\theta+\frac{1}{\theta}\right)\left(\int_{D}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{Q}\left(\theta+\frac{1}{\theta}\right)n_{\mathrm{P}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\|n_{\mathrm{P}}\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)}\int_{Q}\left(\theta+\frac{1}{\theta}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Using (
3.20
), (
3.16
) and (
3.39
) it follows that

0\leq\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}\varphi\left(\theta+\frac{1}{\theta}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C.

Substituting this bound into the right-hand side of inequality (
3.43
) and recalling that thanks to (
3.35
) the expression on the left-hand side of (
3.43
) is bounded by a constant, it then follows from (
3
) that there exists a positive constant
C_{\widetilde{D}}
, dependent on
\widetilde{D}
, such that

\frac{1}{2}\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C_{\widetilde{D}}.

In addition, it trivially follows from the bound on the last term on the left-hand side of (
3.42
) that

\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C.

Thus, by combining the last two bounds, we have the following bound:

\displaystyle\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}\theta\left(|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi}|^{2}+|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{\varphi}|^{2}\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C_{\widetilde{D}}.

(3.44)

Thanks to (
3.16
), the function
\theta
(which is independent of
\boldsymbol{q}\in\widetilde{D}
) is bounded below by a positive constant on
\overline{Q}
. In addition, (
3.20
) implies that

\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|\sqrt{\varphi}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C.

Combining this with (
3.44
), it follows that

\displaystyle\|\sqrt{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega\times\widetilde{D}))}\leq C_{\widetilde{D}}.

(3.45)

By parabolic interpolation between this inequality and the inequality

\displaystyle\|\sqrt{\varphi}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega\times\widetilde{D}))}\leq C_{\widetilde{D}}

implied by (
3.20
), it follows that

\displaystyle\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|\varphi|^{1+\delta}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C_{\widetilde{D}}\quad\mbox{for some $\delta>0$}.

(3.46)

A simple calculation yields
1+\delta=1+\frac{1}{d}\in(1,2)
for
d=2,3
, independently of
\widetilde{D}
.

Step 15.

In this final step, we derive bounds on
\varphi
up to the boundary of
D
. To do this, suppose that
G\in L^{\infty}([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
. It follows from the bound stated in (
3.35
) that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} C&\geq\int_{Q\times D}\frac{G(\theta)}{\theta\varphi}\left|\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\int_{Q\times D}\bigg[\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\theta\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}|^{2}+\frac{\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}}{\theta}\\
&\qquad\qquad+2\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+2\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+2\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\bigg]G(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t.\end{aligned}

(3.47)

We proceed by considering, for
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q
, the integral over
D
of the expression appearing in the square brackets on the right-hand side of (
3.47
), that is,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(t,\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle:=\int_{D}\bigg[\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\theta\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}|^{2}+\frac{\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}}{\theta}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+2\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+2\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+2\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

We shall use the elementary inequality
2a\cdot b\geq-\frac{1}{2}|a|^{2}-2|b|^{2}
, with
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, to partially absorb the fourth term into the first term and the sixth term into the third term. Indeed, because

\int_{D}2\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\geq-\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}-2\int_{D}\theta\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

and

\int_{D}2\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\geq-\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\frac{\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}}{\theta}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}-2\int_{D}\theta\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q},

it follows (by dropping the nonnegative second term appearing in the square brackets in the definition of
\mathcal{A}(t,\boldsymbol{x})
above) that

\mathcal{A}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\left[\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}}{\theta}\right]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}-4\int_{D}\theta\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+2\int_{D}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

Next, we perform partial integration in the last term on the right-hand side of this inequality using the implicitly imposed boundary condition
\varphi|_{Q\times\partial D}=0
(cf. (
3.2
)) to deduce that

\mathcal{A}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\left[\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}}{\theta}\right]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}-4\int_{D}\theta\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}-2\int_{D}\varphi\cdot\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

As
|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{q})|\leq C
for all
\boldsymbol{q}\in\overline{D}
, and
\theta
is independent of
\boldsymbol{q}
, it follows from (
3.20
) that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(t,\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle\geq-C\theta+\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\left[\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}}{\theta}\right]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}-2\int_{D}\varphi\cdot\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle=-C\theta+\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\frac{\varphi}{\theta}\left(|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}-4\theta\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.

Now, let
\theta_{\mathrm{max}}~(>\theta_{\mathrm{min}})
be an arbitrary positive constant, and let

Q_{\theta_{\mathrm{max}}}:=\{(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in\overline{Q}\,:\,0\leq\theta(t,\boldsymbol{x})\leq\theta_{\mathrm{max}}\}.

Importantly, as indicated in the previous section, at this point we need to demand that
U_{e}
satisfies (
2
) rather than the weaker hypothesis (
2
). Then, because by (
2
)

\lim_{|\boldsymbol{q}|\to(\sqrt{b})_{-}}\frac{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})}{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})|^{2}}=0,

it follows that there exists a
\delta=\delta(\theta_{\mathrm{max}})\in(0,1)
sufficiently small such that

|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}-4\theta_{\mathrm{max}}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}=|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\left(1-4\theta_{\mathrm{max}}\frac{\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}}{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\quad\mbox{for all $\boldsymbol{q}\in D\setminus(1-\delta)D$}.

Hence, for any
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q_{\theta_{\mathrm{max}}}
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(t,\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle\geq-C\theta+\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{(1-\delta)D}\frac{\varphi}{\theta}\left(|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}-4\theta\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle\quad\,+\frac{1}{2}\int_{D\setminus(1-\delta)D}\frac{\varphi}{\theta}\left(|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}-4\theta\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle\geq-C\theta+\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{(1-\delta)D}\frac{\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}}{\theta}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}-2\int_{(1-\delta)D}\varphi\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle\quad\,+\frac{1}{4}\int_{D\setminus(1-\delta)D}\frac{\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}}{\theta}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle\geq-C\theta+\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+\frac{1}{4}\int_{D}\frac{\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}}{\theta}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}-2\int_{(1-\delta)D}\varphi\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle\geq-C\theta+\frac{1}{4}\int_{D}\left(\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}}{\theta}\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}-2K(\theta_{\mathrm{max}})\int_{(1-\delta)D}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q},

where

K(\theta_{\mathrm{max}}):=\max_{\boldsymbol{q}\in(1-\delta)\overline{D}}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})<\infty,

thanks to the uniform continuity of
\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}
on compact subsets of
D
. Because, by (
3.20
),

0\leq\int_{(1-\delta)D}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\leq\int_{D}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\leq\|\varphi_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega;L^{1}(D))}

and the constant
K(\theta_{\mathrm{max}})
is positive, we have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(t,\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle\geq-(C\theta+2K(\theta_{\mathrm{max}})\|\varphi_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega;L^{1}(D))})+\frac{1}{4}\int_{D}\left(\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}}{\theta}\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle\geq-(C\theta_{\mathrm{max}}+2K(\theta_{\mathrm{max}})\|\varphi_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega;L^{1}(D))})+\frac{1}{4}\int_{D}\left(\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}}{\theta}\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

for all
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q_{\theta_{\mathrm{max}}}
. In other words,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\int_{D}\left(\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}}{\theta}\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\leq\mathcal{A}(t,\boldsymbol{x})+{C}(\theta_{\mathrm{max}}),

(3.48)

where
{C}(\theta_{\mathrm{max}})
is a positive constant which depends on
\theta_{\mathrm{max}}
. We return with this bound to the inequality (
3.47
) and take

G(\theta):=1\!\!1_{[0,\theta_{\mathrm{max}}]}(\theta)\quad\mbox{for $\theta\in[0,\infty)$}

there. Clearly,
G(\theta(t,\boldsymbol{x}))=1\!\!1_{[0,\theta_{\mathrm{max}}]}(\theta(t,\boldsymbol{x}))=1\!\!1_{Q_{\theta_{\mathrm{max}}}}(t,\boldsymbol{x})
for all
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q
, and therefore (
3.47
) and (
3.48
) imply that

\displaystyle\int_{Q\times D}1\!\!1_{Q_{\theta_{\mathrm{max}}}}\left(\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}}{\theta}\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{Q\times D}G(\theta)\left(\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}}{\theta}\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C+C(\theta_{\mathrm{max}})|Q|,

In other words,

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{\theta_{\mathrm{max}}}\times D}\left(\frac{\theta|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}+\frac{\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}}{\theta}\right)\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C+C(\theta_{\mathrm{max}})|Q|.

(3.49)

Applying Lemma
1
with
Q
there replaced by
Q_{\theta_{\mathrm{max}}}
and
g(t,\boldsymbol{x}):=1
for all
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q_{\theta_{\mathrm{max}}}
, we deduce that
\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\cdot)
and
\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\cdot)U_{e}^{\prime}(|\cdot|^{2}/2)
have well-defined traces in
L^{1}(\partial D)
which are equal to zero for a.e.
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q_{\theta_{\mathrm{max}}}
.
Because, by (
3.28
),

\|\theta\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))}\leq\mathrm{e}^{T}\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}+\|\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi_{0}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega\times D)}+\frac{1}{2}\|\boldsymbol{f}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Q)}\right)<\infty,

whereby
\theta
is finite a.e. on
Q
, in the limit of
\theta_{\mathrm{max}}\to+\infty
the increasing nested family of sets
\{Q_{\theta_{\mathrm{max}}}\}
exhausts
Q
. Therefore,
\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\cdot)
has a well-defined trace
\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\cdot)|_{\partial D}\in L^{1}(\partial D)
for a.e.
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q
and
\varphi|_{\partial D}
is equal to
0
a.e. on
Q\times\partial D
; also,
\varphi(t,\boldsymbol{x},\cdot)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}(\cdot)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{q}}|_{\partial D}\in L^{1}(\partial D)
for a.e.
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q
, and it, too, vanishes on
\partial D
for a.e.
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q
. We draw the reader’s attention to the fact that, although
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\in C^{1}(D;\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the second assertion in the previous sentence does not directly follow from the fact that
\varphi|_{\partial D}=0
, as
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{q}}=U_{e}^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)|\boldsymbol{q}|=\sqrt{b}\,U_{e}^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)=\infty
when
\boldsymbol{q}\in\partial D
.

4
Weak sequential stability

We begin by stating the definition of a weak solution to the problem under consideration.

Definition 1
(Weak solution)

Suppose that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a bounded, open, simply connected set in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d\in\{2,3\}
, with Lipschitz continuous boundary
\partial\Omega
, and
T>0
. Assume that the initial data
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}
,
\theta_{0}
,
\varphi_{0}
and the source term
\boldsymbol{f}
satisfy the following properties:

•

\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}_{0}=0
in the sense of distributions on
\Omega
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{x}}|_{\partial\Omega}=0
in
W^{-1/2,2}(\partial\Omega)
;

•

\theta_{0}\in L^{1}(\Omega)
,
\theta_{0}\geq\theta_{\mathrm{min}}>0
;

•

\varphi_{0}\in L^{1}\log L^{1}(\Omega\times D)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega;L^{1}(D))
,
\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi_{0}\in L^{1}(\Omega\times D)
,
\varphi_{0}\geq 0
;

•

\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{2}(Q)
.

Let

\boldsymbol{F}:=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}:=-4\boldsymbol{F}\varphi-4\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi.

Suppose further that
\beta>5/6
and
p:=2-\frac{6}{5+3\beta}
. We shall say that a triple of functions
(\boldsymbol{v},\theta,\varphi)
is a weak solution to the problem under consideration provided that:

•

\boldsymbol{v}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}_{0,\mathrm{div}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))\cap L^{p}(0,T;W^{1,p}_{0,\mathrm{div}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{v}\in L^{s}(0,T;W^{-1,s^{\prime}}_{0,\mathrm{div}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
for some
s>1
, where
s^{\prime}:=s/(s-1)
is the Hölder conjugate of
s
;

•

\theta\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}_{>0}))\cap L^{\frac{2}{3}+\beta}(Q)
,
\theta^{\beta/2}\in L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega))
and

\log\theta\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega))
;

•

\frac{\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}}{\theta}\in L^{1}(Q),\quad\frac{\kappa(\theta)|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2}}\in L^{1}(Q),

\frac{\theta}{\varphi}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi|^{2}\in L^{1}(Q\times D),\quad\frac{1}{\theta\varphi}|\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\in L^{1}(Q\times D),

2\nu(\theta)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})-2\theta n_{\mathrm{P}}\mathbb{I}+\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\in L^{p}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d});

•

\varphi\!\in\!L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}\log L^{1}(\Omega\times D;\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}))
,
\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega\times D))
,
\partial_{t}\varphi\!\in\![C([0,T];C^{1}(\overline{\Omega\times D}))]^{*}
;

furthermore, the velocity field
\boldsymbol{v}
satisfies the balance of linear momentum equation in the following weak sense:

\displaystyle-\int_{Q}\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t-\int_{Q}(\boldsymbol{v}\otimes\boldsymbol{v}):\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{Q}\mathbb{S}:\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{w})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}(0)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{Q}\boldsymbol{f}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

for all test functions
\boldsymbol{w}\in C^{1}_{0}([0,T);W^{1,s}_{0,\mathrm{div}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, where

\mathbb{S}=2\nu(\theta)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})+\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q};

for all positive integers
k
the temperature
\theta
satisfies the following renormalized variational inequality:

\displaystyle-\int_{Q}T_{k}(\theta)\,\partial_{t}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t-\int_{Q}\boldsymbol{v}\,T_{k}(\theta)\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{Q}\kappa(\theta)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}T_{k}(\theta)\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}

\displaystyle\quad\geq\int_{\Omega}T_{k}(\theta_{0})\,\psi(0)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{Q}2\nu(\theta)T_{k}^{\prime}(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{Q}\left(T_{k}^{\prime}(\theta)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}):\int_{D}\theta(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{Q}\left(T_{k}^{\prime}(\theta)\int_{D}\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

for all test functions
\psi\in C^{1}_{0}([0,T);W^{1,z}(\Omega))
with
z>d
such that
\psi\geq 0
on
\overline{Q}
, where

T_{k}(s):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{s}{|s|}\max\{k,|s|\}&\mbox{for $s\neq 0$},\\
0&\mbox{for $s=0$};\end{array}\right.

and the probability density function
\varphi
satisfies the Fokker–Planck equation in the following weak sense:

\displaystyle-\int_{Q\times D}\varphi\,\partial_{t}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t-\int_{Q\times D}\boldsymbol{v}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{Q\times D}\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{Q\times D}((\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi+\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}})\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\Omega\times D}\varphi_{0}\,\zeta(0)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\qquad\forall\,\zeta\in C^{1}_{0}([0,T);C^{1}(\overline{\Omega\times D})).

Finally, a weak solution is understood to be energy-dissipative in the sense that for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
it satisfies the following energy inequality, with
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{F}
as defined in (
3
):

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{v}(t)|^{2}+\mathcal{H}(\theta(t))\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Omega\times D}[\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi(t)+\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi(t)+\mathcal{F}(\varphi(t))]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&\qquad+\int_{0}^{t}\!\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}}{\theta}+\frac{\kappa(\theta)|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2}}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\qquad+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\theta\int_{D}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+\int_{D}\frac{4}{\theta\varphi}\left|\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right|^{2}\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
