&\leq\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}|^{2}+\mathcal{H}(\theta_{0})\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Omega\times D}[\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi_{0}+\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi_{0}+\mathcal{F}(\varphi_{0})]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{f}(s)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}(s)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s.\end{aligned}

(4.1)

The central result of the paper is the following theorem,
which guarantees weak sequential stability of the nonisothermal kinetic model under consideration.

Theorem 1

Under the stated assumptions on the data, sequences of smooth solutions to the initial-boundary-value problem (
2.26
)–(
2.31
) for the system of nonlinear partial differential equations under consideration, which satisfy the energy equality (
3.32
), converge to an energy-dissipative weak solution of the problem.

The rest of the paper is devoted to the proof of this theorem. Because the proof is long, we draw the reader’s attention to the fact that, even though the argument is seemingly interrupted by titles of subsections, the proof runs through several subsections and ends at the end of Section 3.

Proof.
Suppose that we have a sequence of functions
((\boldsymbol{v}^{n},\theta^{n},\varphi^{n}))_{n=1}^{\infty}
with
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\in C^{1,2}([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\theta^{n}\in C^{1,2}([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega})
and
\varphi^{n}\in C^{1,2,2}([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\overline{D})
, satisfying the inequality (
4.1
), the equations (
2.26
), (
2.25
),
(
3
) and (
2.27
) subject to the boundary and initial conditions (
2.30
), (
3.10
), (
3.11
), (
2.34
), and (
2.31
), i.e., for all
n=1,2,\ldots
,

\boldsymbol{v}^{n}=\boldsymbol{0}\;\;\mbox{on $(0,T)\times\partial\Omega$},\quad\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(0,\boldsymbol{x})=\boldsymbol{v}_{0}^{n}(x)\;\;\mbox{for $\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega$},

\kappa(\theta^{n})\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta^{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=0,\;\;\mbox{on $(0,T)\times\partial\Omega$},\quad\theta^{n}(0,\boldsymbol{x})=\theta_{0}^{n}(\boldsymbol{x})\;\;\mbox{for $\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega$},

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\left(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\varphi^{n}-[\theta^{n}]_{+}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi^{n}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=0\;\;\mbox{on $(0,T)\times\partial\Omega\times D$},\\
((\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi^{n}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}^{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{q}}=0\;\;\mbox{on $(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D$},\end{array}\right.\quad\!\!\varphi^{n}(0,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})=\varphi_{0}^{n}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\;\;\mbox{for $(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\in\Omega\times D$},

where
(\boldsymbol{v}_{0}^{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
,
(\theta_{0}^{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
,
(\varphi_{0}^{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
are sequences of functions defined on
\overline{\Omega}
,
\overline{\Omega}
and
\overline{\Omega}\times\overline{D}
, respectively, such that
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}_{0}\in C^{2}(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\theta^{n}_{0}\in C^{2}(\overline{\Omega})
,

\varphi^{n}_{0}\in C^{2,2}(\overline{\Omega}\times\overline{D})
,

\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}_{0}^{n}=0
on
\Omega
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}^{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=0
on
\partial\Omega
,
\theta_{0}^{n}\geq\theta_{\mathrm{min}}>0
on
\overline{\Omega}
, and
\varphi_{0}^{n}\geq 0
on
\overline{\Omega}\times\overline{D}
for all
n=1,2,\ldots
; with
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}_{0}\to\boldsymbol{v}_{0}
in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\theta^{n}_{0}\to\theta_{0}
in
L^{1}(\Omega)
,
\varphi_{0}^{n}\to\varphi_{0}
in
L^{1}(\Omega\times D)
,
\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi_{0}^{n}\to\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi_{0}
in
L^{1}(\Omega\times D)
,
\mathcal{F}(\varphi_{0}^{n})\to\mathcal{F}(\varphi_{0})
in
L^{1}(\Omega\times D)
, as
n\to\infty
, and

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}^{n}

\displaystyle:=-\boldsymbol{F}^{n}[\varphi^{n}]_{+}-[\theta^{n}]_{+}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi^{n}\qquad\mbox{with}\qquad\boldsymbol{F}^{n}:=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}.

As in Step 0 in the previous section, the assumed nonnegativity of
\varphi_{0}^{n}
on
\overline{\Omega}\times\overline{D}
implies the nonnegativity of
\varphi^{n}
on
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\overline{D}
and, as in Step 1, the assumed positive lower bound on
\theta_{0}^{n}
on
\overline{\Omega}
implies the positivity of
\theta^{n}
on
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}
. Consequently,
[\varphi^{n}]_{+}
and
[\theta^{n}]_{+}

appearing in the equations above can be replaced by

\varphi^{n}
and
\theta^{n}
; therefore, we shall remove the now redundant symbol
[\cdot]_{+}
in what follows.

Because
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}=\mathbf{0}
on
\partial\Omega
and
\theta^{n}
is strictly positive on
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}
, it follows that the zero normal flux boundary condition for
\varphi^{n}
on
(0,T)\times\partial\Omega\times D
collapses to the following homogeneous Neumann boundary condition:

\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi^{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=0\quad\mbox{on $(0,T)\times\partial\Omega\times D$.}

We note further that because

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}^{n}&:=-\boldsymbol{F}^{n}\varphi^{n}-\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi^{n}=-(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta})\varphi^{n}-\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi^{n}\\
&=-\theta^{n}\left[\varphi^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\left(\frac{\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}\right)+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi^{n}\right]=-\theta^{n}\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}\varphi^{n}\right)\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}\right),\end{aligned}

(4.2)

the zero normal flux boundary condition (
2.33
) imposed for
\varphi^{n}
on
(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D
can be restated as follows:

\left[(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi^{n}-\theta^{n}\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}\varphi^{n}\right)\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}\right)\right]\cdot\boldsymbol{n}_{\boldsymbol{q}}=0\quad\mbox{on $(0,T)\times\Omega\times\partial D$}.

We shall use the bounds established in Steps 1–15 in Section
3
to pass to the respective limits in the sequences
(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
,
(\theta^{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
and
(\varphi^{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
.

4.1
Limit passage in the balance of linear momentum equation

The a priori bounds (
3.20
)–(
3.42
) and (
3.44
) imply the existence of weakly convergent subsequences (not indicated) such that, as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{v}^{n}&\rightharpoonup^{\ast}\boldsymbol{v}\quad&&\mbox{weakly-$\ast$ in $L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))$},\\
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}&\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{v}\quad&&\mbox{weakly in $L^{\frac{5}{3}\frac{2+3\beta}{5+3\beta}}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d}))$},\\
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}&\rightharpoonup\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}\quad&&\mbox{weakly in $L^{\frac{4+6\beta}{5+3\beta}}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})$},\\
\mathbb{S}^{n}&\rightharpoonup\overline{\mathbb{S}}\quad&&\mbox{weakly in $L^{p}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})$},\quad p:=2-\frac{6}{5+3\beta}=\frac{4+6\beta}{5+3\beta},\end{aligned}

(4.3)

with
\beta>\frac{5}{6}
, where

\mathbb{S}^{n}:=2\nu(\theta^{n})\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})+\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F^{n}}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}=\nu(\theta^{n})\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})+\int_{D}((\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta})\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q},

and
\overline{\mathbb{S}}
is a weak limit, to be identified. From the balance of linear momentum equation (
2.26
) satisfied by
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}
one has a uniform bound on
\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}
in
L^{s}(0,T;W^{-1,s^{\prime}}_{0,\mathrm{div}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
for some
s>1
, where
s^{\prime}:=\frac{s}{s-1}
, which then implies the existence of a subsequence (not indicated) such that, as
n\to\infty
,

\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\rightharpoonup\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{v}\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{s}(0,T;W^{-1,s^{\prime}}_{0,\mathrm{div}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))$ for some $s>1$}.

Thanks to the compact embedding of
W^{1,\frac{4+6\beta}{5+3\beta}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
in
L^{r}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
1\leq r<\frac{6(2+3\beta)}{11+3\beta}
for
d=2,3
, and noting that
6>\frac{6(2+3\beta)}{11+3\beta}>\frac{10}{3}\frac{2+3\beta}{5+3\beta}>2
for
\beta>\frac{5}{6}
, the Aubin–Lions lemma implies that, as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{v}\quad\mbox{strongly in $L^{r}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d})$ for $1\leq r\leq\frac{10}{3}\frac{2+3\beta}{5+3\beta}$}

(4.4)

for
d=2,3
and
\beta>\frac{5}{6}
, and in particular, as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{v}\quad\mbox{strongly in $L^{2}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d})$ for $d=2,3$}.

(4.5)

These convergence results then enable us to pass to the limit in the sequence of balance of linear momentum equations as
n\rightarrow\infty
to deduce that, for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
,

\displaystyle-\int_{Q}\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t-\int_{Q}(\boldsymbol{v}\otimes\boldsymbol{v}):\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{Q}\overline{\mathbb{S}}:\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{w})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}(0)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{Q}\boldsymbol{f}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

(4.6)

for all test functions
\boldsymbol{w}\in C^{1}_{0}([0,T);W^{1,s}_{0,\mathrm{div}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
for some
s>1
, where the weak limit
\overline{\mathbb{S}}
is yet to be identified.

4.2
Limit passage in the sequence of absolute temperatures

For the sequence
(\theta^{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
, we have from (
3.40
), (
3
) and (
3.39
) that, as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \theta^{n}&\rightharpoonup\theta\quad&&\mbox{weakly in $L^{\frac{2}{3}+\beta}(Q)$},\\
\log\theta^{n}&\rightharpoonup\overline{\log\theta}\quad&&\mbox{weakly in $L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega))$},\\
(\theta^{n})^{\frac{\beta}{2}}&\rightharpoonup\overline{\theta^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}\quad&&\mbox{weakly in $L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega))$},\end{aligned}

(4.7)

where
\overline{\log\theta}
and
\overline{\theta^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}

are weak limits, to be identified.

For
k\geq 1
we introduce the real-valued continuous piecewise-linear cutoff function
T_{k}\in C^{0,1}(\mathbb{R})
defined by

T_{k}(s):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{s}{|s|}\max\{k,|s|\}&\mbox{for $s\neq 0$},\\
0&\mbox{for $s=0$}.\end{array}\right.

Clearly,
T_{k}(s)=s
for
s\in[-k,k]
and
T_{k}(s)=k\,\mathrm{sign}(s)
for
|s|\geq k
. Let
T_{k,\varepsilon}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
be a mollification of
T_{k}
, with
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, such that

\displaystyle T_{k,\varepsilon}(s)

\displaystyle=s\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{for $|s|\leq k$},

\displaystyle T_{k,\varepsilon}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{s}{|s|}\left(k+\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon\right)\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{for $|s|\geq k+\varepsilon$},

\displaystyle T_{k,\varepsilon}(s)

\displaystyle=-\frac{s}{2\varepsilon|s|}\left(s^{2}-2\frac{s}{|s|}(k+\varepsilon)s+k^{2}\right)\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{for $k\leq|s|\leq k+\varepsilon$}.

Thus,

0\leq T^{\prime}_{k,\varepsilon}(s)\leq 1,\quad sT^{\prime\prime}_{k,\varepsilon}(s)\leq 0\quad\mbox{and}\quad|T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(s)|\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\quad\mbox{for all $s\in\mathbb{R}$}.

We multiply by
T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}
the temperature equation satisfied by
\theta^{n}
to obtain a renormalized version of the equation; hence,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n})&+\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\,T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n}))-\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\kappa(\theta^{n})\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n}))=2\nu(\theta^{n})T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})|^{2}\\
&+T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}):\int_{D}\theta^{n}(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}-T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(\theta^{n})\kappa(\theta^{n})|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta^{n}|^{2}\\
&+T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\int_{D}\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi^{n}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}.\end{aligned}

(4.8)

Since
T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}
has compact support
[-k-\varepsilon,k+\varepsilon]\subset\mathbb{R}
, we deduce from (
4.8
) that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n})\|_{\mathcal{M}(0,T;(W^{1,z}(\Omega))^{\ast})}\leq C(k,\varepsilon)\quad\mbox{for all $z\in(d,\infty)$},

(4.9)

where, for a Banach space
X
,
\mathcal{M}(0,T;X^{*})
denotes the continuous dual space of
C([0,T];X)
, that is, the Banach space of
X^{*}
-valued (finite, signed) Radon measures on
[0,T]
.

To show that the bound (
4.9
) holds, we test (
4.8
) with
\chi\in C([0,T];W^{1,z}(\Omega))
, where
z>d
, and move the second and third term from the left-hand side of the resulting equality to the right-hand side. Hence,

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{t}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n}),\chi\rangle=\int_{Q}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\,T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n})\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\chi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{Q}\kappa(\theta^{n})\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n})\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\chi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{Q}2\nu(\theta^{n})T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})|^{2}\chi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{Q}T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}):\left(\int_{D}\theta^{n}(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\chi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{Q}T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(\theta^{n})\kappa(\theta^{n})|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta^{n}|^{2}\chi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{Q}T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\left(\int_{D}\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi^{n}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\chi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=:\mathrm{T}_{1}+\mathrm{T}_{2}+\mathrm{T}_{3}+\mathrm{T}_{4}+\mathrm{T}_{5}+\mathrm{T}_{6}.

Then, by (
4.3
)
1
and since
|T_{k,\varepsilon}(s)|\leq k+\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon<k+1
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

|\mathrm{T}_{1}|\leq\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}\|T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n})\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)}\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\chi\|_{L^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}\leq C(k)\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\chi\|_{L^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}.

To bound the term
\mathrm{T}_{2}
, we note that
\kappa(\theta^{n})\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n})=\kappa(\theta^{n})\theta^{n}T^{\prime}_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n})\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\log\theta^{n}
. Thus, by (
4.7
)
2
and because
T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}
has compact support
[-k-\varepsilon,k+\varepsilon]
, we have the following:

|\mathrm{T}_{2}|\leq C(k)\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\chi\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}.

To deal with the term
\mathrm{T}_{3}
, we note that
\nu(\theta^{n})T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})|^{2}=\left(T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\theta^{n}\right)\frac{\nu(\theta^{n})|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})|^{2}}{\theta^{n}}
. Thus, again using that
T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}
has compact support in conjunction with the bound on the third term from the second line of (
3
), with
\boldsymbol{v}
and
\theta
there replaced by
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}
and
\theta^{n}
, we have

|\mathrm{T}_{3}|\leq C(k)\|\chi\|_{C([0,T];L^{\infty}(\Omega))}.

To bound the term
\mathrm{T}_{4}
, we write
T^{\prime}_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n})\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\theta^{n}=(T^{\prime}_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n})(\theta^{n})^{\frac{3}{2}})(\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})/\sqrt{\theta^{n}})
, use that
T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}
has compact support and
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\otimes\boldsymbol{q}\in C(\overline{D};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
, together with the bound on the third term of the second line of (
3
), with
\boldsymbol{v}
and
\theta
replaced by
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}
and
\theta^{n}
, and the bound on the first term on the left-hand side of inequality (
3.42
), with
\varphi
replaced by
\varphi^{n}
; thus,

|\mathrm{T}_{4}|\leq C(k)\|\chi\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}.

For the term
\mathrm{T}_{5}
, we rewrite
T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(\theta^{n})\kappa(\theta^{n})|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta^{n}|^{2}=T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(\theta^{n})\kappa(\theta^{n})(\theta^{n})^{2}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\log\theta^{n}|^{2}
and use (
4.7
)
2
to find that

|\mathrm{T}_{5}|\leq\frac{C(k)}{\varepsilon}\|\chi\|_{C([0,T];L^{\infty}(\Omega))}.

Finally, to deal with the term
\mathrm{T}_{6}
, we note that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\left(\int_{D}\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi^{n}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\chi\\
&\quad=T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\theta^{n}\left(\int_{D}\frac{\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}}{\sqrt{\theta^{n}}}\cdot\frac{\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi^{n}+\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi^{n}}{\sqrt{\theta^{n}}{\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\chi.\end{aligned}

(4.10)

We recall the bound that appears
in the third line of (
3
) with
\theta
and
\varphi
replaced by
\theta^{n}
and
\varphi^{n}
, the bound (
3.49
) with
\theta
and
\varphi
replaced by
\theta^{n}
and
\varphi^{n}
and
\theta_{\mathrm{max}}
chosen to be equal to
k+\varepsilon
, and note that for
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q\setminus Q_{\theta_{\mathrm{max}}}=Q\setminus Q_{k+\varepsilon}
the expression appearing in the last line above is equal to zero because
T^{\prime}_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n}(t,\boldsymbol{x}))=0
for all such
(t,\boldsymbol{x})
, whereby the integral of the expression (
4.10
) over
Q
is equal to its integral over
Q_{\theta_{\mathrm{max}}}=Q_{k+\varepsilon}
. Therefore, integrating (
4.10
) over
Q
and applying Hölder’s inequality, we find that

|\mathrm{T}_{6}|\leq C(k)\|\chi\|_{C([0,T];L^{\infty}(\Omega))}.

It remains to collect the bounds on the terms
\mathrm{T}_{1},\ldots,\mathrm{T}_{6}
and note that
C([0,T];W^{1,z}(\Omega))
is, for
z>d
, continuously embedded in
L^{1}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega))
,
L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega))
,
C([0,T];L^{\infty}(\Omega))
, and
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
, and therefore we have

|\langle\partial_{t}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n}),\chi\rangle|\leq|\mathrm{T}_{1}|+|\mathrm{T}_{2}|+|\mathrm{T}_{3}|+|\mathrm{T}_{4}|+|\mathrm{T}_{5}|+|\mathrm{T}_{6}|\leq C(k,\varepsilon)\|\chi\|_{C([0,T];W^{1,z}(\Omega))},

which directly implies (
4.9
).

In addition,

\int_{Q}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n})|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C(k)\int_{Q}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta^{n}|^{2}}{(\theta^{n})^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C(k).

Hence,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n})\rightharpoonup\overline{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)}\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))$},

(4.11)

where
\overline{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)}
is a weak limit, to be identified.

Now, (
4.9
) and (
4.11
), via a generalization of the Aubin–Lions lemma (cf. Corollary 7.9 in
[
45
]
), imply that as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n})\rightarrow\overline{T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)}\quad\mbox{strongly in $L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))$},

(4.12)

where
\overline{T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)}
is a strong limit, to be identified.

We note that

\int_{Q}|\theta^{n}-T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n})|\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{\{(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in Q\,:\,\theta^{n}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\geq k\}}|\theta^{n}|\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{C}{k^{\alpha}}\quad\mbox{with $\alpha:=\beta-\frac{1}{3}>0$},

where the second inequality is a consequence of (
3.40
) applied to
\theta^{n}
(recall that
\frac{2}{3}+\beta>1
). Therefore, by the triangle inequality,

\int_{Q}|\theta^{m}-\theta^{n}|\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{Q}|T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{m})-T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n})|\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{C}{k^{\alpha}}\quad\mbox{for all $m,n\in\mathbb{N}$}.

Let us now choose a real number
\delta>0
and then fix
k=k(\delta)
such that

\frac{C}{k^{\alpha}}\leq\frac{\delta}{2},

where
C
is the positive constant from the above inequality. According to (
4.12
) the sequence
(T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n}))_{n=1}^{\infty}
is (strongly) convergent in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))=L^{2}(Q)
and, therefore, because
Q=(0,T)\times\Omega
is a bounded set, it is also strongly convergent in
L^{1}(Q)
. Thus, with
k=k(\delta)
fixed as above, there exists a positive integer
N_{0}=N_{0}(\delta)
such that

\int_{Q}|T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{m})-T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n})|\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{\delta}{2}\quad\forall\,m,n\geq N_{0}.

Hence, we have shown that for any
\delta>0
there exists a positive integer
N_{0}=N_{0}(\delta)
such that

\int_{Q}|\theta^{m}-\theta^{n}|\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\delta\quad\forall\,m,n\geq N_{0}.

In other words,
(\theta^{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{1}(Q)
, and therefore, thanks to the completeness of
L^{1}(Q)
, as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\theta^{n}\rightarrow\theta\quad\mbox{strongly in $L^{1}(Q)$}.

(4.13)

Hence, for a subsequence (not indicated),

\displaystyle\theta^{n}\rightarrow\theta\quad\mbox{a.e.~on $Q$}.

(4.14)

This then implies that
\log\theta^{n}\rightarrow\log\theta
a.e. on
Q
and
(\theta^{n})^{\frac{\beta}{2}}\rightarrow\theta^{\frac{\beta}{2}}
a.e. on
Q
. Thanks to the bound appearing in the second line of inequality (
3
), with
\theta
replaced by
\theta^{n}
there, we find that the sequences
(\log\theta^{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
and
((\theta^{n})^{\frac{\beta}{2}})_{n=1}^{\infty}
are equi-integrable in
L^{1}(Q)
. Thus, by Vitali’s convergence theorem, we have the following strong convergence results:

\displaystyle\log\theta^{n}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\log\theta\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{strongly in $L^{1}(Q)$},

\displaystyle(\theta^{n})^{\frac{\beta}{2}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\theta^{\frac{\beta}{2}}\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{strongly in $L^{1}(Q)$},

as
n\to\infty
. Therefore, by the uniqueness of the weak limit, from (
4.7
) we have that, as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \log\theta^{n}&\rightharpoonup{\log\theta}\quad&&\mbox{weakly in $L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega))$},\\
(\theta^{n})^{\frac{\beta}{2}}&\rightharpoonup{\theta^{\frac{\beta}{2}}}\quad&&\mbox{weakly in $L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega))$}.\end{aligned}

(4.15)

In addition, since
T_{k,\varepsilon}\in C^{0,1}(\mathbb{R})
with Lipschitz constant equal to
1
, it follows from (
4.13
) that, as
n\to\infty
,

\|T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n})-T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)\|_{L^{1}(Q)}\leq\|\theta^{n}-\theta\|_{L^{1}(Q)}\rightarrow 0,

and therefore, thanks to the uniqueness of the strong limit,
\overline{T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)}=T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)
. Thus, we have identified
\overline{T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)}
; that is, as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n})\rightarrow T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)\quad\mbox{strongly in $L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))$}.

(4.16)

Now, (
4.16
), (
4.11
) and the uniqueness of the weak limit yield that

\overline{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)}=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)
, and therefore

\displaystyle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n})\rightharpoonup\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))$}.

Further, we have from (
4.9
) that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n})\rightharpoonup^{\ast}\partial_{t}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)\quad\mbox{weakly-$\ast$ in $\mathcal{M}(0,T;(W^{1,z}(\Omega))^{\ast})$ for all $z>d$}.

(4.17)

Since the (distributional) time derivative of
T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)
is a (finite signed) Radon measure, which then implies that
T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)\in BV(0,T;(W^{1,z}(\Omega))^{\ast})
for all
z>d
, it makes sense to define, for all
t\in(0,T)
,

T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta(t_{+},\cdot)):=\lim_{s\to t_{+}}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta(s,\cdot))

and

T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta(t_{-},\cdot)):=\lim_{s\to t_{-}}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta(s,\cdot)),

where both limits are considered in the space
(W^{1,z}(\Omega))^{\ast}
,
z>d
.
7
7

7

See, for example, Proposition 4.2 in
[
42
]
, which is also available from

https://hal.science/hal-01363799v1
, concerning the existence of one-sided limits of functions contained in
BV(0,T;E)
, where
E
is a complete metric space.

In addition, we also have, for all
k\geq 1
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
that, as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\kappa(\theta^{n})\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n})=\kappa(\theta^{n})T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta^{n}\rightharpoonup\kappa(\theta)T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta=\kappa(\theta)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{2}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d})$}.

(4.18)

Using (
4.17
), (
4.4
) (with
r=2
), (
4.16
) and (
4.18
) we can now pass to the limit on the left-hand side of the weak formulation of (
4.8
) as
n\to\infty
. Since the right-hand side of (
4.8
) also involves
\varphi^{n}
, we need to establish suitable convergence results for
\varphi^{n}
to be able to pass to the limit there.

Therefore, in the next subsection we explore the convergence properties of the sequence of probability density functions
(\varphi^{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
.

4.3
Limit passage in the sequence of probability density functions

Thanks to (
3.36
), we have that

\int_{Q\times D}\frac{1}{\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}}|\boldsymbol{j}^{n}_{\varphi}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C.

Therefore, as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}}}\boldsymbol{j}^{n}_{\varphi}\rightharpoonup\overline{\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta\varphi}}\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}}\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{2}(Q\times D;\mathbb{R}^{d})$},

(4.19)

where
\overline{\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta\varphi}}\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}}
is a weak limit, to be identified.

Further, from (
3.35
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{Q\times D}\theta^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{4}\int_{Q\times D}\theta^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\widehat{\varphi^{n}}|^{2}}{\widehat{\varphi^{n}}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C,

(4.20)

and therefore, as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\sqrt{\theta^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}}\rightharpoonup\overline{\sqrt{\theta\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{\varphi\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}}\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{2}(Q\times D;\mathbb{R}^{d})$},

(4.21)

where
\overline{\sqrt{\theta\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{\varphi\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}}
is a weak limit, to be identified.

It follows from (
3.42
) that, for all
n\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\|\varphi^{n}\log\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega\times D))}\leq C.

(4.22)

Thus, by de la Vallée Poussin’s criterion,

\displaystyle\varphi^{n}\rightharpoonup\varphi\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{p}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega\times D))$}\quad\mbox{for all $p\in[1,\infty)$},

(4.23)

as
n\to\infty
. It further follows from the last bound in (
3.44
) that, for all
n\geq 1
,

\|\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\|_{L^{2}(Q\times D)}\leq C.

Therefore, as
n\to\infty
,

\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\rightharpoonup\overline{\sqrt{\theta}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi}}\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{2}(Q\times D;\mathbb{R}^{d})$},

where
\overline{\sqrt{\theta}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi}}
is a weak limit, to be identified.

We recall that we have already shown above that
\theta^{n}\to\theta
a.e. on
Q
as
n\to\infty
. In order to identify the various unidentified weak limits listed above that involve the sequences
(\theta^{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
and
(\varphi^{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
, it therefore remains to be shown that, as
n\to\infty
,

\varphi^{n}\to\varphi\quad\mbox{a.e.~on $Q\times D$}.

We shall do so via a compensated compactness argument based on the div-curl lemma. To this end, however, we need to establish additional estimates on expressions entering in the Fokker–Planck equation satisfied by
\varphi^{n}
.
For ease of writing, suppose that
d=3
(for
d=2
the argument is identical). We begin by noting that the Fokker–Planck equation satisfied by
\varphi^{n}
is, in fact, the divergence with respect to the variables
(t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q})\in(0,T)\times\Omega\times D\subset\mathbb{R}^{7}
of the vector function

\boldsymbol{H}^{n}:=(\varphi^{n},\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\varphi^{n}-\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi^{n},(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi^{n}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}^{n}).

In terms of this notation, the Fokker–Planck equation satisfied by
\varphi^{n}
is simply

\mathrm{div}_{t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q}}(\boldsymbol{H}^{n})=0\quad\mbox{for all $n\geq 1$}.

In a sequence of steps, we shall next bound
\varphi^{n}
in the norm of
L^{r}(\Omega\times\widetilde{D})
for some
r\in(1,2)
, and each of
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\varphi^{n}
,
\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi^{n}
,
(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi^{n}
and
\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}^{n}
in the norm of
L^{r}(Q\times\widetilde{D};\mathbb{R}^{3})
for some
r\in(1,2)
, independently of
n
, which will then imply that the sequence
\boldsymbol{H}^{n}
is bounded in
L^{r}(Q\times\widetilde{D};\mathbb{R}^{7})
for some
r\in(1,2)
.

According to (
3.46
), for any open subset
\widetilde{D}
of
D
such that
\widetilde{D}\subset\joinrel\subset D
,

\displaystyle\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{1+\delta}(Q\times\widetilde{D})}\leq C_{\widetilde{D}}

(4.24)

for some
\delta>0
(independent of
\widetilde{D}
) and all
n\geq 1
. Hence,

\displaystyle\varphi^{n}\rightharpoonup\varphi\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{1+\delta}(Q\times\widetilde{D})$}

(4.25)

as
n\to\infty
.

It follows from (
3.44
) and the fact that
\theta
is bounded below by a positive constant on
Q
(cf. (
3.16
)) that

\int_{Q}\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\widetilde{D})}\leq C_{\widetilde{D}}.

Because
\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(Q;L^{1}(D))}\leq C
, also
\|\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\|_{L^{\infty}(Q;L^{2}(D))}\leq C
, by which
\|\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\|_{L^{2}(Q;W^{1,2}(\widetilde{D}))}\leq C_{\widetilde{D}}
. Hence, by Sobolev embedding,
\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{1}(Q;L^{3}(\widetilde{D}))}=\|\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\|_{L^{2}(Q;L^{6}(\widetilde{D}))}^{2}\leq C_{\widetilde{D}}
.

Next, let
r\in(1,\frac{25p}{15p+12}]
with
p=2-\frac{6}{5+3\beta}=\frac{2(2+3\beta)}{5+3\beta}
. We note that
\frac{25p}{15p+12}=\frac{25(2+3\beta)}{60+63\beta}>1
for all
\beta>\frac{5}{6}
, and therefore the interval
(1,\frac{25p}{15p+12}]
is not empty. For such
r
, we then define
\gamma:=\frac{5p}{6r}
and
\lambda:=\frac{3-r}{2r}
. Hence,
r\gamma=\frac{5p}{6}=\frac{5}{3}\frac{2+3\beta}{5+3\beta}>1
and

0<(1-\lambda)r\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}\leq 1.

Thus, by function space interpolation and Hölder’s inequality,

\displaystyle\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\varphi^{n}|^{r}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{Q}|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}|^{r}\left(\int_{\widetilde{D}}|\varphi^{n}|^{r}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{Q}|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}|^{r}\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{r}(\widetilde{D})}^{r}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{Q}|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}|^{r}\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{1}(\widetilde{D})}^{\lambda r}\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{3}(\widetilde{D})}^{(1-\lambda)r}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(Q;L^{1}(D))}^{\lambda r}\left(\int_{Q}|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}|^{r\gamma}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\left(\int_{Q}\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{3}(\widetilde{D})}^{(1-\lambda)r\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma}}.

Because
0<(1-\lambda)r\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}\leq 1
and the sequence
(\varphi^{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
is bounded in
L^{1}(Q;L^{3}(\widetilde{D}))
, it then follows that

\displaystyle\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\varphi^{n}|^{r}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(Q;L^{1}(D))}^{\lambda r}\|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|_{L^{\frac{5p}{6}}(Q)}^{r}\left(\int_{Q}\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{3}(\widetilde{D})}^{(1-\lambda)r\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma}}\leq C_{\widetilde{D}}.

(4.26)

Next, let
r\in\left(1,\frac{2+2\delta}{2+\delta}\right)
; thus
r<2
and
1<\frac{r}{2-r}<1+\delta
. Then, it follows by (
3.44
) that

\displaystyle\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi^{n}|^{r}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=2^{r}\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}|^{r}|\sqrt{\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}}|^{r}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq 2^{r}\left(\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}\theta^{n}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{r}{2}}\left(\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}|^{\frac{r}{2-r}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{2-r}{r}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\int_{Q}|\theta^{n}|^{\frac{r}{2-r}}\left(\int_{\widetilde{D}}|\varphi^{n}|^{\frac{r}{2-r}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{2-r}{2}}.

By interpolation,

\|\varphi^{n}\|^{\frac{r}{2-r}}_{L^{\frac{r}{2-r}}(\widetilde{D})}\leq\|\varphi^{n}\|^{\frac{(1-\lambda)r}{2-r}}_{L^{1}(\widetilde{D})}\|\varphi^{n}\|^{\frac{\lambda r}{2-r}}_{L^{1+\delta}(\widetilde{D})},

where

\frac{2-r}{r}=\frac{1-\lambda}{1}+\frac{\lambda}{1+\delta}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\frac{2(1-r)}{r}=-\lambda\frac{\delta}{1+\delta}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\lambda=\frac{2(1+\delta)(r-1)}{\delta r}.

Clearly,
\lambda\in(0,1)
. Hence,

\displaystyle\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi^{n}|^{r}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\int_{Q}|\theta^{n}|^{\frac{r}{2-r}}\|\varphi^{n}\|^{\frac{\lambda r}{2-r}}_{L^{1+\delta}(\widetilde{D})}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{2-r}{2}}

\displaystyle=C\left(\int_{Q}|\theta^{n}|^{\frac{r}{2-r}}\|\varphi^{n}\|^{\frac{2(1+\delta)(r-1)}{\delta(2-r)}}_{L^{1+\delta}(\widetilde{D})}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{2-r}{2}}.

Now, let
\gamma=\frac{\delta(2-r)}{2(r-1)}
and let
\gamma^{\prime}=\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}
; evidently,
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}>1
. Hence, by Hölder’s inequality,

\displaystyle\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi^{n}|^{r}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\int_{Q}|\theta^{n}|^{\frac{r\gamma^{\prime}}{2-r}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{2-r}{2\gamma^{\prime}}}\left(\int_{Q}\|\varphi^{n}\|^{\frac{2(1+\delta)(r-1)}{\delta(2-r)}\gamma}_{L^{1+\delta}(\widetilde{D})}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{2-r}{2\gamma}}

\displaystyle=C\left(\int_{Q}|\theta^{n}|^{\frac{r\gamma^{\prime}}{2-r}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{2-r}{2\gamma^{\prime}}}\left(\int_{Q}\|\varphi^{n}\|^{1+\delta}_{L^{1+\delta}(\widetilde{D})}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{2-r}{2\gamma}}

\displaystyle=C\left(\int_{Q}|\theta^{n}|^{\frac{r\gamma^{\prime}}{2-r}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{2-r}{2\gamma^{\prime}}}\|\varphi^{n}\|^{\frac{(1+\delta)(2-r)}{2\gamma}}_{L^{1+\delta}(Q\times\widetilde{D})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\widetilde{D}}\left(\int_{Q}|\theta^{n}|^{\frac{r\gamma^{\prime}}{2-r}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{2-r}{2\gamma^{\prime}}},

where the last inequality follows from (
3.46
). According to (
3.40
) the right-hand side of the last inequality will be bounded by a constant, independent of
n
, provided that

\displaystyle\frac{r\gamma^{\prime}}{2-r}\leq\frac{2}{3}+\beta\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\frac{r}{2-r}\,\frac{\delta(2-r)}{\delta(2-r)-2(r-1)}\leq\frac{2}{3}+\beta\quad

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\frac{\delta r}{\delta(2-r)-2(r-1)}\leq\frac{2}{3}+\beta

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad 3\delta r\leq(2+3\beta)(2\delta+2)-(2+3\beta)(\delta+2)r\quad

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad r\leq\frac{2(2+3\beta)(\delta+1)}{3\delta+(2+3\beta)(\delta+2)};

that is, if

1<r\leq\frac{2(2+3\beta)(\delta+1)}{3\delta+(2+3\beta)(\delta+2)}.

We note that the fraction on the right-hand side of this inequality is
>1
thanks to our assumption that
\beta>\frac{5}{6}~(>\frac{1}{3})
and is
<\frac{2+2\delta}{2+\delta}<2
, so if this inequality is satisfied, then automatically
r\in\left(1,\frac{2+2\delta}{2+\delta}\right)
. In particular, we can set

r=\frac{2(2+3\beta)(\delta+1)}{3\delta+(2+3\beta)(\delta+2)}.

With this choice of
r
we then have that

\displaystyle\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi^{n}|^{r}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq C_{\widetilde{D}}.

(4.27)

It remains to derive a bound on the final component of
\boldsymbol{H}^{n}
, which is
(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi^{n}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}^{n}
. For any
r\in(1,p)
, where
p=2-\frac{6}{5+3\beta}
, by Hölder’s inequality and interpolation with
\lambda:=\frac{3-r}{2r}\in(0,1)
, recalling that
|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}\leq b
for all
\boldsymbol{q}\in D
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi^{n}|^{r}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t&\leq b^{\frac{r}{2}}\int_{Q}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}|^{r}\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{r}(\widetilde{D})}^{r}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq b^{\frac{r}{2}}\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{r}{p}}\left(\int_{Q}\|\varphi^{n}\|^{\frac{pr}{p-r}}_{L^{r}(\widetilde{D})}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{p-r}{p}}\\
&\leq b^{\frac{r}{2}}\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{r}{p}}\left(\int_{Q}\|\varphi^{n}\|^{\frac{\lambda pr}{p-r}}_{L^{1}(\widetilde{D})}\|\varphi^{n}\|^{\frac{(1-\lambda)pr}{p-r}}_{L^{3}(\widetilde{D})}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{p-r}{p}}\\
&\leq b^{\frac{r}{2}}\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\|_{L^{p}(Q)}^{r}\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(Q;L^{1}(D))}^{\lambda r}\left(\int_{D}\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{3}(\widetilde{D})}^{\frac{3p(r-1)}{2(p-r)}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{p-r}{p}}\leq C_{\widetilde{D}}\end{aligned}

(4.28)

for all
n\geq 1
, provided that
r\in(1,p)
is such that

0<\frac{3p(r-1)}{2(p-r)}\leq 1\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad 1<r\leq\frac{5p}{3p+2}(<p).

For example, we can take (again, because by assumption
\beta>\frac{5}{6}~(>\frac{1}{3})
)

r=\frac{5p}{3p+2}=\frac{5(2+3\beta)}{11+12\beta}\in(1,2).

We move on to bound
\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}^{n}:=-\boldsymbol{F}^{n}\varphi^{n}-\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi^{n}
and recall that
\boldsymbol{F}^{n}=\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}
;
hence we need to bound
\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}^{n}:=-(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta})\varphi^{n}-\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi^{n}
. Thanks to (
4.27
) we already have a bound on the final summand in
\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}^{n}
; therefore, it remains to deal with the first two summands of
\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}^{n}
. Since
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}
and
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}
are uniformly continuous and therefore bounded functions on
\overline{\widetilde{D}}
, we only need to derive bounds on
\varphi^{n}
and
\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}
in the norm of
L^{r}(Q\times\widetilde{D})
for some
r\in(1,2)
.

For any
r\in(1,\frac{5}{3}]
and
\lambda:=\frac{3-r}{2r}\in(0,1)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|\varphi^{n}|^{r}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{Q}\|\varphi^{n}\|^{r}_{L^{r}(\widetilde{D})}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{Q}\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{1}(\widetilde{D})}^{\lambda r}\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{3}(\widetilde{D})}^{(1-\lambda)r}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(Q;L^{1}(D))}^{\lambda r}\int_{D}\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{3}(\widetilde{D})}^{(1-\lambda)r}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C_{\widetilde{D}},

because

(1-\lambda)r=\frac{3(r-1)}{2}\leq 1\quad\mbox{for $r\in(1,\frac{5}{3}]$}.

Similarly, for any
r\in(1,\frac{3}{2}]
and
\lambda:=\frac{3-r}{2r}
, we have
r<\beta+\frac{2}{3}
for
\beta>\frac{5}{6}
; therefore,
\gamma:=\frac{2+3\beta}{3r}>1
. Since
\frac{2}{3r-1}<1
, it follows that

0<(1-\lambda)r\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}<1.

Hence,

\displaystyle\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}|^{r}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{Q}|\theta^{n}|^{r}\|\varphi\|^{r}_{L^{r}(\widetilde{D})}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{Q}|\theta^{n}|^{r}\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{1}(\widetilde{D})}^{\lambda r}\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{3}(\widetilde{D})}^{(1-\lambda)r}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\|\varphi^{n}\|^{\lambda r}_{L^{\infty}(Q;L^{1}(\widetilde{D}))}\left(\int_{Q}|\theta^{n}|^{\gamma r}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\left(\int_{Q}\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{3}(\widetilde{D})}^{(1-\lambda)r\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=\|\varphi^{n}\|^{\lambda r}_{L^{\infty}(Q;L^{1}(\widetilde{D}))}\|\theta^{n}\|^{r}_{L^{\frac{2}{3}+\beta}(Q)}\left(\int_{Q}\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{3}(\widetilde{D})}^{(1-\lambda)r\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma}}\leq C_{\widetilde{D}}.

These bounds on
(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi^{n}
,
\varphi^{n}
and
\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}
, together with (
4.27
), imply that, for some
r\in(1,2)
, independent of
\widetilde{D}
and
n
,

\displaystyle\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi^{n}+\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}^{n}|^{r}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C_{\widetilde{D}}\quad\mbox{for all $n\geq 1$.}

(4.29)

Combining the bounds (
4.24
), (
4.26
), (
4.27
), (
4.28
) and (
4.29
) it then follows that, for some
\delta\in(0,1)
,

\|\boldsymbol{H}^{n}\|_{L^{1+\delta}((0,T)\times\Omega\times\widetilde{D})}\leq C_{\widetilde{D}}\quad\mbox{for all $n\geq 1$}.

Recall further that
\mbox{div}_{t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q}}(\boldsymbol{H}^{n})=0
.

In preparation for the application of the div-curl lemma, we consider another 7-component vector-function:

\boldsymbol{Q}^{n}:=(T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{n}),\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{0}),

where
\boldsymbol{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
and we note that
\|\boldsymbol{Q}^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(Q\times D)}\leq k
for each
k\geq 1
and every
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Next, we bound
\mbox{curl}_{t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q}}(\boldsymbol{Q}^{n}):=\nabla_{t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q}}\boldsymbol{Q}^{n}-(\nabla_{t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q}}\boldsymbol{Q}^{n})^{\mathrm{T}}
. Clearly,

\displaystyle\|\mbox{curl}_{t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q}}(\boldsymbol{Q}^{n})\|_{L^{2}(Q\times\widetilde{D})}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q}}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{n})\|_{L^{2}(Q\times\widetilde{D})}=2\|T^{\prime}_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{n})\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{2}(Q\times\widetilde{D})}

\displaystyle=4\|T^{\prime}_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{n})\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\|_{L^{2}(Q\times\widetilde{D})}

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{k+\varepsilon}\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\|_{L^{2}(Q\times\widetilde{D})}\leq 2\sqrt{k+\varepsilon}\,C_{\widetilde{D}},

where the last inequality follows from (
3.45
).

In summary then,
(\boldsymbol{H}^{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
is a bounded sequence in
L^{1+\delta}((0,T)\times\Omega\times\widetilde{D};\mathbb{R}^{7})
for some
\delta\in(0,1)
and
\mbox{div}_{t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q}}(\boldsymbol{H}^{n})=0
, whereby
\mbox{div}_{t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q}}(\boldsymbol{H}^{n})
is, trivially, precompact in
W^{-1,2}((0,T)\times\Omega\times\widetilde{D}):=[W^{1,2}_{0}((0,T)\times\Omega\times\widetilde{D})]^{\ast}
. Furthermore,
(\boldsymbol{Q}^{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
is a bounded sequence in
L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Omega\times\widetilde{D};\mathbb{R}^{7})
and
\mbox{curl}_{t,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{q}}(\boldsymbol{H}^{n})
, is precompact in
W^{-1,2}((0,T)\times\Omega\times\widetilde{D};\mathbb{R}^{7\times 7}):=[W^{1,2}_{0}((0,T)\times\Omega\times\widetilde{D};\mathbb{R}^{7\times 7})]^{\ast}
thanks to the compact embedding of
L^{2}((0,T)\times\Omega\times\widetilde{D};\mathbb{R}^{7\times 7})
into
W^{-1,2}((0,T)\times\Omega\times\widetilde{D};\mathbb{R}^{7\times 7})
.

Thus, by the div-curl lemma, for each
k\geq 1
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{H}^{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{n}=\varphi^{n}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{n})\rightharpoonup\varphi\,\overline{T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi)}\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{1+\delta^{\prime}}((0,T)\times\Omega\times\widetilde{D};\mathbb{R}^{7})$},

(4.30)

where
0<\delta^{\prime}<\delta
and
\overline{T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi)}\in L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Omega\times\widetilde{D})=L^{\infty}(Q\times\widetilde{D})
is the weak-
\ast
limit of the bounded sequence
(T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{n}))_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset L^{\infty}(Q\times\widetilde{D})
, to be identified. In order to identify
\overline{T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi)}
we note that, thanks to the monotonicity of the function
T_{k,\varepsilon}
, one has that

(T_{k,\varepsilon}(a)-T_{k,\varepsilon}(b))^{2}\leq(T_{k,\varepsilon}(a)-T_{k,\varepsilon}(b))(a-b)\quad\forall\,a,b\in\mathbb{R}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}(T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{n})-T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{m}))^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}(T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{n})-T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{m}))(\varphi^{n}-\varphi^{m})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Passing to the limit
m\rightarrow\infty
, with
n\geq 1
,
k\geq 1
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
fixed, using the weak lower-semicontinuity of the
L^{2}(Q\times\widetilde{D})
norm, it then follows that

\displaystyle\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}(T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{n})-\overline{T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi)})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{lim.inf}_{m\to\infty}\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}(T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{n})-T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{m}))(\varphi^{n}-\varphi^{m})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}(T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{n})-\overline{T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi)})(\varphi^{n}-\varphi)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}(T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{n})\varphi^{n}-\overline{T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi)}(\varphi^{n}-\varphi)-T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{n})\varphi)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t,

where in the transition to the second line we have used (
4.25
) and (
4.30
). Now we pass to the limit
n\rightarrow\infty
using (
4.30
) in the first term on the right-hand side, (
4.25
) in the second term on the right-hand side and the definition of
\overline{T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi)}
in the final term on the right-hand side. Hence,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}(T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{n})-\overline{T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi)})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}(\overline{T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi)}\varphi-0-\overline{T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi)}\varphi)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t=0.

Thus we have shown that, as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{n})\to\overline{T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi)}\quad\mbox{strongly in $L^{2}(Q\times\widetilde{D})$}

for each
k\geq 1
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, and therefore also

\displaystyle T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{n})\to\overline{T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi)}\quad\mbox{strongly in $L^{1}(Q\times\widetilde{D})$}.

(4.31)

Now, by the triangle inequality,

\int_{\Omega\times\widetilde{D}}|\varphi^{n}-\varphi^{m}|\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{n})-{T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{m})}|+|\varphi^{n}-T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{n})|+|\varphi^{m}-T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{m})|\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Hence, using (
4.23
) and weak lower-semicontinuity in the
L^{1}(Q\times D)
norm in conjunction with (
4.31
), it follows by passing to the
\mathrm{lim.inf}_{m\to\infty}
that

\displaystyle\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|\varphi^{n}-\varphi|\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{n})-\overline{T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi)}|\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|\varphi^{n}|\,1\!\!1_{\{|\varphi^{n}|>k\}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad+\mathrm{lim.inf}_{m\to\infty}\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|\varphi^{m}-T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{m})|\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{n})-\overline{T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi)}|\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|\varphi^{n}\log\varphi^{n}|\frac{\,1\!\!1_{\{|\varphi^{n}|>k\}}}{|\log\varphi^{n}|}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad+\mathrm{lim.inf}_{m\to\infty}\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|\varphi^{m}-T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{m})|\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{n})-\overline{T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi)}|\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{C}{\log k}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathrm{lim.inf}_{m\to\infty}\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|\varphi^{m}-T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{m})|\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{n})-\overline{T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi)}|\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{C}{\log k}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathrm{lim.sup}_{m\to\infty}\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|\varphi^{m}\log\varphi^{m}|\frac{\,1\!\!1_{\{|\varphi^{m}|>k\}}}{|\log\varphi^{m}|}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi^{n})-\overline{T_{k,\varepsilon}(\varphi)}|\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{2C}{\log k},

for all
k>1
and all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, where we have used (
4.22
) in transitioning from the second inequality to the third inequality and from the fourth inequality to the fifth inequality. Hence, by first passing to the limit
n\to\infty
using (
4.31
) with
k>1
held fixed, and then by passing to the limit
k\to\infty
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\varphi^{n}\to\varphi\quad\mbox{strongly in $L^{1}(Q\times\widetilde{D})$}.

(4.32)

This then implies that, for a subsequence, not indicated,

\displaystyle\varphi^{n}\to\varphi\quad\mbox{a.e.~in $Q\times\widetilde{D}$}.

By selecting a sequence of nested sets
(\widetilde{D}_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{\infty}
such that
\widetilde{D}_{\ell}\subset\joinrel\subset D
and
\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\widetilde{D}_{\ell}=D
, and using that
\varphi^{n}\to\varphi
a.e. in
\Omega\times\widetilde{D}_{\ell}
for each
\ell\geq 1
, the application of a diagonal procedure then produces a subsequence (that is, once again, not relabelled) such that, as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\varphi^{n}\to\varphi\quad\mbox{a.e.~in $Q\times D$}.

(4.33)

Thanks to Vitali’s convergence theorem, (
4.23
) and (
4.33
) finally imply that

\displaystyle\varphi^{n}\to\varphi\quad\mbox{strongly in $L^{1}(Q\times D)$}.

(4.34)

4.4
Limit passage in the sequence of stress tensors

The pointwise convergence results (
4.14
) and (
4.33
) now enable us to identify the still unidentified weak limit
\overline{\mathbb{S}}
of the sequence
(\mathbb{S}^{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset L^{p}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
, with
p=2-\frac{6}{5+3\beta}
and
\beta>\frac{5}{6}
, which appeared above:

\mathbb{S}^{n}=2\nu(\theta^{n})\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})+\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}^{n}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q},

We begin by considering the first summand in
\mathbb{S}^{n}
. As
\nu\in BC([0,\infty);\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
and
\theta^{n}\to\theta
a.e. on
Q
, the sequence of measurable functions
(\nu(\theta^{n}))_{n=1}^{\infty}
converges to
\nu(\theta)
a.e. on
Q
and
\|\nu(\theta^{n})\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)}\leq C
for all
n\geq 1
. Furthermore, because of (
4.3
), we have that, as
n\to\infty
,

\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\rightharpoonup\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{\frac{4+6\beta}{5+3\beta}}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})$},\quad\beta>\frac{5}{6},

and therefore also
\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\rightharpoonup\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})
weakly in
L^{1}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
, as
n\to\infty
. Hence, by Proposition 2.61 on p.183 in
[
25
]
,
\nu(\theta^{n})\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\rightharpoonup\nu(\theta)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})
in
L^{1}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
as
n\to\infty
. Since
(\nu(\theta^{n})\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}))_{n=1}^{\infty}
is a bounded sequence in
L^{\frac{4+6\beta}{5+3\beta}}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
with
\beta>\frac{5}{6}
, and
\frac{4+6\beta}{5+3\beta}>1
, there exists a weakly convergent subsequence (not indicated) such that
\nu(\theta^{n})\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\rightharpoonup\overline{\nu(\theta)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})}
in
L^{\frac{4+6\beta}{5+3\beta}}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})\subset L^{1}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
as
n\to\infty
. By the uniqueness of the weak limit,
\overline{\nu(\theta)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})}=\nu(\theta)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})
, and therefore as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\nu(\theta^{n})\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\rightharpoonup\nu(\theta)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{\frac{4+6\beta}{5+3\beta}}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})$ with $\beta>\frac{5}{6}$}.

(4.35)

We continue by considering the weak limit of the second summand of
\mathbb{S}^{n}
. Let us suppose to this end that
\boldsymbol{w}\in C([0,T];C^{1}_{0}(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and that
\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{w}=0
on
Q
. Then

\displaystyle\int_{Q}\left(\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}^{n}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right):\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{Q}\left(\int_{D}((\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta})\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right):\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\int_{Q}\left(\int_{D}\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\,\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\left(\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{2\theta^{n}}}\,\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\widehat{\varphi^{n}}}{\sqrt{\widehat{\varphi^{n}}}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right):\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=2\int_{Q}\left(\int_{D}\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\,\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\left(\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\sqrt{\theta^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}}\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{{\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right):\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t,

(4.36)

where in the transition to the second line we used (
3.37
) together with the fact that
\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{w}=0
in order to eliminate the first term on the right-hand side of (
3.37
).

Motivated by the form of the expression in the last line of (
4.4
), we begin by showing that

\displaystyle\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\,\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\rightharpoonup\sqrt{\theta}\,\sqrt{\varphi}\quad\mbox{strongly in $L^{2}(Q\times D)$}

(4.37)

and

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{q}\otimes\sqrt{\theta^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}}\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{{\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}}}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\sqrt{\theta\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{{\varphi\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}}\\
\ \mbox{weakly in $L^{2}(Q\times D;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})$}.\end{aligned}

(4.38)

as
n\to\infty
.

Because
|\sqrt{a}-\sqrt{b}|\leq\sqrt{|a-b|}
for all
a,b\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, it follows that

\|\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\,\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}-\sqrt{\theta}\,\sqrt{\varphi}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Q\times D)}\leq\|\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}-\theta\varphi\|_{L^{1}(Q\times D)}.

We will show that the right-hand side of this inequality tends to 0 as
n\to\infty
. Because
\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}\to\theta\varphi
a.e. on
Q\times D
, thanks to Vitali’s theorem, it suffices to show that the sequence
(\theta^{n}\varphi^{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
is equi-integrable. We begin by noting that, for
\alpha\in(0,1)
, Hölder’s inequality implies that

\displaystyle\int_{D}\varphi^{n}|\log\varphi^{n}|^{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

\displaystyle=\int_{D}(\varphi^{n})^{1-\alpha}(\varphi^{n}|\log\varphi^{n}|)^{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\leq\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{1}(D)}^{1-\alpha}\left(\int_{D}\varphi^{n}|\log\varphi^{n}|\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)^{\alpha}.

Hence, by Young’s inequality,

\displaystyle\int_{Q\times D}|\theta^{n}|^{1+\alpha}\varphi^{n}|\log\varphi^{n}|^{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(Q;L^{1}(D))}^{1-\alpha}\int_{Q}|\theta^{n}|^{1+\alpha}\left(\int_{D}\varphi^{n}|\log\varphi^{n}|\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)^{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(Q;L^{1}(D))}^{1-\alpha}\int_{Q}\left((1-\alpha)|\theta^{n}|^{\frac{1+\alpha}{1-\alpha}}+\alpha\int_{D}\varphi^{n}|\log\varphi^{n}|\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(Q;L^{1}(D))}^{1-\alpha}\left(\|\theta^{n}\|_{L^{\frac{1+\alpha}{1-\alpha}}(Q)}^{\frac{1+\alpha}{1-\alpha}}+\|\varphi^{n}\log\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{1}(Q\times D)}\right).

We set
\frac{1+\alpha}{1-\alpha}=\frac{2}{3}+\beta
with
\beta>\frac{5}{6}
, whereby
\alpha=\frac{3\beta-1}{3\beta+5}\in(0,1)
. Then it follows that, for all
n\geq 1
,

\int_{Q\times D}|\theta^{n}|^{1+\alpha}\varphi^{n}|\log\varphi^{n}|^{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C,

where
C
is a positive constant independent of
n
, which then implies that the sequence
(\theta^{n}\varphi^{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
is equi-integrable. That completes the proof of (
4.37
).

Concerning (
4.38
), it directly follows from (
4.21
) that the weak limit exists, and therefore

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{q}\otimes\sqrt{\theta^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}}\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{{\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}}}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\overline{\sqrt{\theta\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{{\varphi\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}}}\\
\ \mbox{weakly in $L^{2}(Q\times D;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})$}\end{aligned}

(4.39)

as
n\to\infty
. Therefore, also

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\sqrt{\theta^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}}\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{{\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}}}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\overline{\sqrt{\theta\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{{\varphi\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}}}

weakly in
L^{2}(Q\times\widetilde{D};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
as
n\to\infty
for any
\widetilde{D}\subset\joinrel\subset D
, and the weak limit is independent of the choice of
\widetilde{D}
. We shall identify the weak limit by proving that, as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\sqrt{\theta^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}}\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{{\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}}}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\sqrt{\theta\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{{\varphi\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}}

\displaystyle\ \mbox{weakly in $L^{1}(Q\times\widetilde{D};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})$}.

By the uniqueness of the weak limit it will then follow that, as
n\to\infty
,

\overline{\sqrt{\theta\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{{\varphi\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}}}=\sqrt{\theta\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{{\varphi\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}},

as required.

To this end, we shall prove that

\displaystyle\sqrt{\theta^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}}\to\sqrt{\theta\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}\quad\mbox{strongly in $L^{2}(Q\times\widetilde{D})$}

(4.40)

and that

\displaystyle\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{{\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}}}\rightharpoonup\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{{\varphi\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}}\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{2}(Q\times\widetilde{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})$}

(4.41)

as
n\to\infty
.

To prove (
4.40
), we begin by noting that, by (
4.13
),
\theta^{n}\to\theta
strongly in
L^{1}(Q)
; also, by (
4.7
)
1
,
\theta^{n}\rightharpoonup\theta
weakly in
L^{\frac{2}{3}+\beta}(Q)
with
\beta>\frac{5}{6}
. Therefore,
\theta^{n}\to\theta
strongly in
L^{r}(Q)
for all
r\in[1,\frac{2}{3}+\beta)
. For a subsequence (not indicated)
\theta^{n}\to\theta
a.e. on
Q
. Because
\theta^{n}\geq\theta_{\min}>0
on
\overline{Q}
, we have

\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}=\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}}{\theta^{n}}+\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\in C(\overline{Q\times\widetilde{D}}),

\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}\to\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}\quad\mbox{a.e.~on $Q\times\widetilde{D}$}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad 0<\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}\leq 1\quad\mbox{a.e.~on $Q\times\widetilde{D}$}.

Thus, by the dominated convergence theorem,

\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}\to\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}\quad\mbox{strongly in $L^{1}(Q\times\widetilde{D})$}.

However, since
\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}\in L^{\infty}(Q\times\widetilde{D})
and
\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}\in L^{\infty}(Q\times\widetilde{D})
, this implies that

\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}\to\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}\quad\mbox{strongly in $L^{s}(Q\times\widetilde{D})$}\quad\mbox{for all $s\in[1,\infty)$},

and, in particular, for
s\in(\frac{3\beta+2}{3\beta-1},\infty)
(where
\frac{3\beta+2}{3\beta-1}
is the Hölder conjugate of
\frac{2}{3}+\beta
, with
\beta>\frac{5}{6}
). It therefore follows that the product

\theta^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}\to\theta\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}\quad\mbox{strongly in $L^{1}(Q\times\widetilde{D})$}.

This directly implies (
4.40
) using the elementary inequality
|\sqrt{a}-\sqrt{b}|\leq\sqrt{|a-b|}
for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

The proof of (
4.41
) is similar. According to (
4.32
),
\varphi^{n}\to\varphi
strongly in
L^{1}(Q\times\widetilde{D})
and, by (
4.25
) (cf. also (
3.46
)),
\varphi^{n}\to\varphi
weakly in
L^{1+\delta}(Q\times\widetilde{D})
; it follows that
\varphi^{n}\to\varphi
strongly in
L^{r}(Q\times\widetilde{D})
for all
r\in[1,1+\delta)
as
n\to\infty
. Also, by the same reasoning as above,

\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}\to\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}\quad\mbox{strongly in $L^{s}(Q\times\widetilde{D})$}\quad\mbox{for all $s\in[1,\infty)$},

and in particular for
s\in(1+\frac{1}{\delta},\infty)
(where
1+\frac{1}{\delta}
is the Hölder conjugate of
1+\delta
). Therefore,

\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}\to\varphi\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}\quad\mbox{strongly in $L^{1}(Q\times\widetilde{D})$,}

whereby

\displaystyle\sqrt{\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}}\to\sqrt{\varphi\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}\quad\mbox{strongly in $L^{2}(Q\times\widetilde{D})$}

(4.42)

as
n\to\infty
. It follows from (
4.20
) and the lower bound

\theta^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}\geq\theta_{\min}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}}{\theta_{\min}}-\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}\geq C_{\widetilde{D}}>0\quad\mbox{on $\overline{Q\times\widetilde{D}}$},

guaranteed by the uniform continuity of
\mathcal{U}_{e}
and
\mathcal{U}_{\eta}
on
\overline{\widetilde{D}}
, that

\displaystyle\int_{Q\times\widetilde{D}}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C_{\widetilde{D}}

for all
n\geq 1
. Hence, for a subsequence (not indicated),

\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}}\to\overline{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta^{n}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta^{n}}}}}\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{2}(Q\times\widetilde{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})$}

as
n\to\infty
. The uniqueness of the weak limit and (
4.42
) then imply that (
4.41
) holds.

Having shown both (
4.40
) and (
4.41
), the assertion (
4.38
) follows thanks to the argument described in the paragraph between equations (
4.39
) and (
4.40
).

This then completes the proofs of (
4.37
) and (
4.38
). Using (
4.37
) and (
4.38
), we can now pass to the limit in (
4.4
); the argument proceeds as follows:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{Q}\left(\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}^{n}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right):\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=2\int_{Q}\left(\int_{D}\sqrt{\theta}\,\sqrt{\varphi}\left(\boldsymbol{q}\otimes\sqrt{\theta\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{{\varphi\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right):\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=2\int_{Q}\left(\int_{D}\sqrt{\theta}\,\sqrt{\varphi}\left(\sqrt{\theta\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{{\varphi\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}}\otimes\boldsymbol{q}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right):\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=2\int_{Q}\left(\int_{D}\sqrt{\frac{\theta}{\varphi}}\left(\sqrt{\theta\,\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{{\varphi\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}}{\theta}}}}\otimes\boldsymbol{q}\right)\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right):\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=2\int_{Q}\left(\int_{D}\theta\left(\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{\widehat{\varphi}}}{\sqrt{\widehat{\varphi}}}\otimes\boldsymbol{q}\right)\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right):\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{Q}\left(\theta\int_{D}\left(\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\widehat{\varphi}}{\widehat{\varphi}}\otimes\boldsymbol{q}\right)\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right):\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{Q}\left(\theta\int_{D}\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\log\widehat{{\varphi}}\otimes\boldsymbol{q}\right)\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right):\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Comparing the expression on the right-hand side of this equality with the right-hand side of (
3.38
) and recalling that, by hypothesis,
\mathbb{I}:\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{w}=\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{w}=0
, it follows that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{Q}\left(\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}^{n}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right):\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{Q}\left(\theta\int_{D}\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\log\widehat{{\varphi}}\otimes\boldsymbol{q}\right)\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right):\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{Q}\left(\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right):\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

for all
\boldsymbol{w}\in C([0,T];C^{1}_{0}(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{d}))
such that
\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{w}=0
on
Q
. Thus, we have shown that

\overline{\mathbb{S}}=2\nu(\theta)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})+\int_{D}(\boldsymbol{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}=\mathbb{S},

whereby, thanks to the density of the set of all divergence-free
C^{1}_{0}(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{d})
functions in
W^{1,s}_{0,\mathrm{div}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
s>1
, we have from (
4.6
) that, for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
,

\displaystyle-\int_{Q}\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t-\int_{Q}(\boldsymbol{v}\otimes\boldsymbol{v}):\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{Q}\mathbb{S}:\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{w})\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}(0)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{Q}\boldsymbol{f}\cdot\boldsymbol{w}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

for all test functions
\boldsymbol{w}\in C^{1}_{0}([0,T);W^{1,s}_{0,\mathrm{div}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
for some
s>1
.

4.5
Limit passage in the Fokker–Planck equation

Next we consider passing to the limit in the Fokker–Planck equation (
2.28
) satisfied by
\varphi^{n}
(and with
\boldsymbol{v}
,
\theta
and
\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}
there replaced by
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}
,
\theta^{n}
and
\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}^{n}
, respectively). To this end, we need to pass to the respective limits in the sequences

\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\varphi^{n},\quad\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi^{n},\quad(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi^{n},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi}^{n}.

We begin by considering the term in the Fokker–Planck equation involving
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\varphi^{n}
. Let
\zeta\in C([0,T];C^{1}(\overline{\Omega}\times\overline{D}))=C([0,T];C^{1}(\overline{\Omega\times D}))
. Thanks to (
4.34
), we have that

\boldsymbol{z}^{n}:=\int_{D}\varphi^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\to\int_{D}\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}=:\boldsymbol{z}\quad\mbox{strongly in $L^{1}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d})$}

as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Because

\|\boldsymbol{z}^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)}\leq\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\zeta\|_{L^{\infty}(Q\times D)}\|\varphi^{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(Q;L^{1}(D))}\leq C

and

\|\boldsymbol{z}\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)}\leq\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\zeta\|_{L^{\infty}(Q\times D)}\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(Q;L^{1}(D))}\leq C,

it follows by Hölder’s inequality that
\boldsymbol{z}^{n}\to\boldsymbol{z}
strongly in
L^{s}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
s\in[1,\infty)
, and in particular for
s=\frac{r}{r-1}
where
r=\frac{10}{3}\frac{2+3\beta}{5+3\beta}
and
\beta>\frac{5}{6}
. Then it follows further from (
4.4
) that
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{z}^{n}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{z}
strongly in
L^{1}(Q)
. Therefore,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{Q\times D}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\varphi^{n}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{Q\times D}\boldsymbol{v}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

for all
\zeta\in C([0,T];C^{1}(\overline{\Omega\times D}))
.

To pass to the limit in the term that involves
\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi^{n}
, we note that

\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi^{n}=\big(\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\big)\left(2\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\right).

According to (
4.37
), the first factor strongly converges to
\sqrt{\theta}\sqrt{\varphi}
in
L^{2}(Q\times D)
. By virtue of (
3.42
),

\|2\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\|_{L^{2}(Q\times D)}=\left\|\sqrt{\frac{\theta^{n}}{\varphi^{n}}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi^{n}\right\|_{L^{2}(Q\times D)}\leq C

for all
n\geq 1
, with
C
independent of
n
, and therefore, for a subsequence (not indicated),

2\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\rightharpoonup 2\overline{\sqrt{\theta}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi}}\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{2}(Q\times D;\mathbb{R}^{d})$}

as
n\to\infty
, where
\overline{\sqrt{\theta}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi}}\in L^{2}(Q\times D)
is a weak limit to be identified. Hence,
also

2\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\rightharpoonup 2\overline{\sqrt{\theta}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi}}\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{1}(Q\times\widetilde{D}\mathbb{R}^{d}))$}

as
n\to\infty
for any
\widetilde{D}\subset\joinrel\subset D
, and the weak limit on the right-hand side is independent of the choice of
\widetilde{D}
. By (
4.13
),
\theta^{n}\to\theta
strongly in
L^{1}(Q)
and therefore
\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\to\sqrt{\theta}
strongly in
L^{2}(Q)
as
n\to\infty
. Also, thanks to (
3.45
) applied to
\varphi^{n}
,

\|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\|_{L^{2}(Q\times\widetilde{D})}\leq C_{\widetilde{D}}

for all
n\geq 1
and for any
\widetilde{D}\subset\joinrel\subset D
. Therefore, there exists a subsequence (not indicated) such that
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}
is weakly convergent in
L^{2}(Q\times\widetilde{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Because by (
4.34
)
\varphi^{n}\to\varphi
strongly in
L^{1}(Q\times D)
and therefore
\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\to\sqrt{\varphi}
strongly in
L^{2}(Q\times D)
, also
\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\to\sqrt{\varphi}
strongly in
L^{2}(Q\times\widetilde{D})
. The uniqueness of the weak limit then implies that the weak limit of
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}
must be
