\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi}
; that is,

\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\rightharpoonup\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi}\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{2}(Q\times\widetilde{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})$}

as
n\to\infty
. Thus, we deduce that

2\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\rightharpoonup 2\sqrt{\theta}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi}\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{1}(Q\times\widetilde{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})$}

as
n\to\infty
for any
\widetilde{D}\subset\joinrel\subset D
. Therefore, because of the independence of the weak limit of the choice of
\widetilde{D}
,

\displaystyle 2\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\rightharpoonup 2\sqrt{\theta}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi}\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{2}(Q\times D;\mathbb{R}^{d})$}.

(4.43)

Since
\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\to\sqrt{\theta}\sqrt{\varphi}
strongly in
L^{2}(Q\times D)
, it follows that

\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi^{n}=\big(\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\big)\left(2\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\right)\rightharpoonup\big(\sqrt{\theta}\sqrt{\varphi}\big)\left(2\sqrt{\theta}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi}\right)=\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi

weakly in
L^{1}(Q\times D;\mathbb{R}^{d})
as
n\to\infty
. Hence,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{Q\times D}\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi^{n}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{Q\times D}\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

for all
\zeta\in C([0,T];C^{1}(\overline{\Omega\times D}))
.

Next, we consider the term in the Fokker–Planck equation containing
(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi^{n}
. Using an identical argument as in the case of the term
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\varphi^{n}
discussed above, thanks to (
4.33
) we have the following:

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}^{n}:=\int_{D}(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\zeta\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\to\int_{D}(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\zeta\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}=:\mathbb{Z}\quad\mbox{strongly in $L^{s}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})$}

(4.44)

as
n\rightarrow\infty
for all
\zeta\in C([0,T];C^{1}(\overline{\Omega\times D}))
, for all
s\in[1,\infty)
, and in particular for
s=\frac{r}{r-1}
, where
r=\frac{4+6\beta}{5+3\beta}
,
\beta>\frac{5}{6}
. Hence, thanks to (
4.3
),

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{Q}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}^{n}:\mathbb{Z}^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{Q}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}:\mathbb{Z}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

as
n\to\infty
. Consequently,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{Q\times D}(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi^{n}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{Q\times D}(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

as
n\to\infty
for all
\zeta\in C([0,T];C^{1}(\overline{\Omega\times D}))
.

It remains to pass to the limit in the term containing
\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi}^{n}
in the Fokker–Planck equation. We write

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi}^{n}=\left(\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}}}\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi}^{n}\right).

(4.45)

According to (
4.37
),
\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\to\sqrt{\theta}\sqrt{\varphi}
strongly in
L^{2}(Q\times D)
, and, thanks to the bound (
4.19
), also,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}}}\boldsymbol{j}^{n}_{\varphi}\rightharpoonup\overline{\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta\varphi}}\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}}\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{2}(Q\times D;\mathbb{R}^{d})$},

(4.46)

and therefore also weakly in
L^{2}(Q\times\widetilde{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})
for any
\widetilde{D}\subset\joinrel\subset D
, and the weak limit, to be identified, is independent of the choice of
\widetilde{D}
; we shall use this observation to identify the weak limit. We begin by noting that, thanks to the definitions of
\boldsymbol{j}^{n}_{\varphi}
and
\boldsymbol{F}^{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}}}\boldsymbol{j}^{n}_{\varphi}=-\left[\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta^{n}}}\,(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e})+\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\,(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta})+2\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\right].

(4.47)

By (
4.43
), the final term on the right-hand side of (
4.47
) satisfies

2\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\rightharpoonup 2\sqrt{\theta}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\sqrt{\varphi}\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{2}(Q\times D;\mathbb{R}^{d})$}

as
n\to\infty
. Since
\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\in C^{1}(\overline{D})
, it follows from (
4.38
) that for the middle term on the right-hand side of (
4.47
) we have

\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\,\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\,(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta})\to\sqrt{\theta}\,\sqrt{\varphi}\,(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta})\quad\mbox{strongly in $L^{2}(Q\times D;\mathbb{R}^{d})$}.

Therefore, it remains to consider the first term on the right-hand side of (
4.47
). Thanks to (
4.34
),
\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\to\sqrt{\varphi}
strongly in
L^{2}(Q\times D)
. Furthermore, since
\theta^{n}\geq\theta_{\min}\,\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha T}>0
with
\alpha>0
for all
n\geq 1
(cf. (
3.16
)), whereby
0<1/\theta^{n}\leq\mathrm{e}^{\alpha T}/\theta_{\min}
a.e. on
Q
, the a.e. convergence of
\theta^{n}
to
\theta
on
Q
asserted in (
4.14
) implies by the dominated convergence theorem that
1/\theta^{n}\to 1/\theta
strongly in
L^{1}(Q\times D)
. Therefore,
1/\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\to 1/\sqrt{\theta}
strongly in
L^{2}(Q\times D)
. Hence, the product
\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}/\sqrt{\theta^{n}}
converges strongly to
\sqrt{\varphi}/\sqrt{\theta}
in
L^{1}(Q\times D)
. Furthermore, because
(1/\sqrt{\theta^{n}})_{n=1}^{\infty}
is a bounded sequence in
L^{\infty}(Q)
, it has a weakly-
\ast
convergent subsequence (not indicated), which by the uniqueness of the weak limit must converge to
1/\sqrt{\theta}
. Therefore,

\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta^{n}}}\rightharpoonup\sqrt{\varphi}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta}}\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{2}(Q\times D)$}.

Because
\mathcal{U}_{e}\in C^{1}(\widetilde{D})
for each
\widetilde{D}\subset\joinrel\subset D
, we therefore deduce that

\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta^{n}}}\,(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e})\rightharpoonup\sqrt{\varphi}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta}}\,(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e})\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{2}(Q\times\widetilde{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})$}

as
n\to\infty
for each
\widetilde{D}\subset\joinrel\subset D
. We are now ready to pass to the limit in (
4.47
):

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}}}\boldsymbol{j}^{n}_{\varphi}

\displaystyle=-\left[\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta^{n}}}\,(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e})+\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\,(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta})+2\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\right]

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup-\left[\sqrt{\varphi}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta}}\,(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e})+\sqrt{\theta}\sqrt{\varphi}\,(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta})+2\sqrt{\theta}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\sqrt{\varphi}\right]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta\varphi}}\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}

weakly in
L^{2}(Q\times\widetilde{D};\mathbb{R}^{d})
as
n\to\infty
for each
\widetilde{D}\subset\joinrel\subset D
. Hence, because of the uniqueness of the weak limit, (
4.46
) implies that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}}}\boldsymbol{j}^{n}_{\varphi}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta\varphi}}\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{2}(Q\times D;\mathbb{R}^{d})$}

(4.48)

as
n\to\infty
. Returning with this to (
4.45
) and recalling that
\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\sqrt{\varphi^{n}}\to\sqrt{\theta}\sqrt{\varphi}
strongly in
L^{2}(Q\times D)
, we finally find that

\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi}^{n}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi}\quad\mbox{weakly in $L^{1}(Q\times D;\mathbb{R}^{d})$}

as
n\to\infty
. Therefore,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{Q\times D}\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}^{n}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{Q\times D}\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

for all
\zeta\in C([0,T];C^{1}(\overline{\Omega\times D}))
. Having passed to the limits in the sequences

\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\varphi^{n},\quad\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi^{n},\quad(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi^{n}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi}^{n},

one can now pass to the limit
n\to\infty
in the weak formulation of the Fokker–Planck equation satisfied by the function
\varphi^{n}
to obtain:

\displaystyle-\int_{Q\times D}\varphi\,\partial_{t}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t-\int_{Q\times D}\boldsymbol{v}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{Q\times D}\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{Q\times D}((\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{q}\varphi+\boldsymbol{j}_{\varphi,\boldsymbol{q}})\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\zeta\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\Omega\times D}\varphi_{0}\,\zeta(0)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\qquad\forall\,\zeta\in C^{1}_{0}([0,T);C^{1}(\overline{\Omega\times D})).

4.6
Limit passage in the renormalized temperature equation

Finally, we pass to the limit
n\to\infty
in the renormalized temperature equation (
4.8
). As noted in the paragraph following (
4.18
), we already have in place the required convergence results to pass to the limit
n\to\infty
in the weak form of the left-hand side

\partial_{t}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n})+\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}\,T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n}))-\mathrm{div}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\kappa(\theta^{n})\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta^{n}))

of (
4.8
). Therefore, it remains to deal with the various terms on the right-hand side of (
4.8
). Motivated by the analysis in
[
13
]
, we shall pass to the limit in those terms from one side; this will result in a renormalized variational
inequality
satisfied by the absolute temperature. To this end, we take
\psi\in C([0,T];W^{1,z}(\Omega))
as a test function for (
4.8
) with
z>d
(whereby, thanks to the Sobolev embedding
W^{1,z}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow C(\overline{\Omega})
,
\psi\in C(\overline{Q})
), and assume that
\psi\geq 0
on
\overline{Q}
. We define

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\int_{Q\times D}\left(2\nu(\theta^{n})T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})|^{2}-T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(\theta^{n})\kappa(\theta^{n})|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta^{n}|^{2}\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{2}

\displaystyle:=\int_{Q\times D}\left(T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}):\theta^{n}(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi^{n}\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{3}

\displaystyle:=\int_{Q\times D}\left(T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{4}

\displaystyle:=\int_{Q\times D}T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\left(\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi^{n}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\varphi^{n}\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t.

We note that these integrals are well-defined. Specifically,
\mathcal{T}_{3}
and
\mathcal{T}_{4}
, which involve the singular potential
\mathcal{U}_{e}
, are finite thanks to (
3.49
), with
\theta
and
\varphi
replaced there by
\theta^{n}
and
\varphi^{n}
, respectively.

We start by considering the term
\mathcal{T}_{1}
. As
T^{\prime}_{\kappa,\varepsilon}(s)\geq 0
and
T^{\prime\prime}_{\kappa,\varepsilon}(s)\leq 0
for all
s\geq 0
, it follows that the integrand of
\mathcal{T}_{1}
is nonnegative. To pass to the limit in
\mathcal{T}_{1}
as
n\to\infty
, note that

\displaystyle 2\nu(\theta^{n})T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\left|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\right|^{2}\psi

\displaystyle=2\nu(\theta^{n})T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\,\theta^{n}\left|\frac{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})}{\sqrt{\theta^{n}}}-\frac{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})}{\sqrt{\theta}}+\frac{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})}{\sqrt{\theta}}\right|^{2}\psi

\displaystyle=2\nu(\theta^{n})T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\,\theta^{n}\left|\frac{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})}{\sqrt{\theta^{n}}}-\frac{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})}{\sqrt{\theta}}\right|^{2}\,\psi+2\nu(\theta^{n})T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\,\theta^{n}\left|\frac{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})}{\sqrt{\theta}}\right|^{2}\psi

\displaystyle\quad+4\nu(\theta^{n})T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\,\theta^{n}\left(\frac{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})}{\sqrt{\theta^{n}}}-\frac{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})}{\sqrt{\theta}}\right):\frac{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})}{\sqrt{\theta}}\,\psi

\displaystyle\hskip-39.83385pt\geq 2\nu(\theta^{n})T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\,\theta^{n}\left|\frac{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})}{\sqrt{\theta}}\right|^{2}\psi+4\nu(\theta^{n})T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\,\theta^{n}\left(\frac{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})}{\sqrt{\theta^{n}}}-\frac{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})}{\sqrt{\theta}}\right):\frac{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})}{\sqrt{\theta}}\,\psi.

As
n\to\infty
, the first term on the right-hand side converges to

2\nu(\theta)T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}\psi

strongly in
L^{1}(Q\times D)
using (
4.14
) by the dominated convergence theorem, while the second term on the right-hand side converges weakly in
L^{1}(Q\times D)
to
0
because of (
4.14
), the boundedness of the sequence
(4\nu(\theta^{n})T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\,\theta^{n}\psi)_{n=1}^{\infty}
in
L^{\infty}(Q\times D)
and the weak convergence

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})}{\sqrt{\theta^{n}}}-\frac{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})}{\sqrt{\theta}}\right):\frac{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})}{\sqrt{\theta}}\rightharpoonup 0\quad\mbox{in $L^{1}(Q\times D)$},

(4.49)

using Proposition 2.61 on p.183 in
[
25
]
.
The weak convergence result (
4.49
) follows by recalling (
4.14
) and the fact that the sequence
(\theta^{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
is equi-bounded from below by a positive constant, which, by the dominated convergence theorem, together guarantee that the sequence
(1/\sqrt{\theta^{n}})_{n=1}^{\infty}
converges strongly to
(1/\sqrt{\theta})
in
L^{s}(Q)
for all
s\in[1,\infty)
, and in particular for
s=\frac{4+6\beta}{3\beta-1}
, where
\beta>1/3
. On the other hand, by (
4.3
)
3
,
\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\rightharpoonup\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})
weakly in
L^{\frac{4+6\beta}{5+3\beta}}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
, and therefore
\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})/\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\rightharpoonup\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})/\sqrt{\theta}
weakly in
L^{1}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
. However,
(\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})/\sqrt{\theta^{n}})_{n=1}^{\infty}
is a bounded sequence in
L^{2}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
and therefore has a weakly convergent subsequence in
L^{2}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
; by the uniqueness of the weak limit (for a subsequence, not indicated),
\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})/\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\rightharpoonup\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})/\sqrt{\theta}
weakly in
L^{2}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
. This then implies the weak convergence result (
4.49
) asserted above.

Therefore, by the weak lower-semicontinuity of the
L^{1}(Q\times D)
norm,

\mbox{lim.inf}_{n\to\infty}\int_{Q\times D}2\nu(\theta^{n})T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\left|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\right|^{2}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\geq\int_{Q\times D}2\nu(\theta)T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta)\left|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})\right|^{2}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

which completes the passage to the limit in the first term of
\mathcal{T}_{1}
.

Passage to the limit in the second term of
\mathcal{T}_{1}
proceeds analogously, by writing

\displaystyle-T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(\theta^{n})\kappa(\theta^{n})|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta^{n}|^{2}\psi

\displaystyle=-T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(\theta^{n})\kappa(\theta^{n})\,\theta^{n}\left|\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta^{n}}{\theta^{n}}-\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta}{\theta}+\frac{\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta}{\theta}\right|^{2}\psi

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt=-T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(\theta^{n})\kappa(\theta^{n})\,\theta^{n}\left|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\log\theta^{n}-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\log\theta+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\log\theta\right|^{2}\psi

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt=-T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(\theta^{n})\kappa(\theta^{n})\,\theta^{n}|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\log\theta^{n}-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\log\theta|^{2}\psi-T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(\theta^{n})\kappa(\theta^{n})\,\theta^{n}\left|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\log\theta\right|^{2}\psi

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\quad-2T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(\theta^{n})\kappa(\theta^{n})\,\theta^{n}\,\psi\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\log\theta^{n}-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\log\theta\right)\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\log\theta

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\geq-T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(\theta^{n})\kappa(\theta^{n})\,\theta^{n}\left|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\log\theta\right|^{2}\psi

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\quad-2T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(\theta^{n})\kappa(\theta^{n})\,\theta^{n}\,\psi\left(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\log\theta^{n}-\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\log\theta\right)\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\log\theta.

As
n\to\infty
, the first term on the right-hand side converges to

-T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(\theta)\kappa(\theta)\,\theta\left|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\log\theta\right|^{2}\psi
strongly in
L^{1}(Q\times D)
by
(
4.14
) and the dominated convergence theorem, while the second term converges to 0 weakly in
L^{1}(Q\times D)
by (
4.15
).
Therefore, by the weak lower-semicontinuity of the
L^{1}(Q\times D)
norm,

\mbox{lim.inf}_{n\to\infty}\int_{Q\times D}-T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(\theta^{n})\kappa(\theta^{n})|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta^{n}|^{2}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\geq\int_{Q\times D}-T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(\theta)\kappa(\theta)|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta|^{2}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Because the
\mathrm{lim.inf}_{n\to\infty}
of the sum of the two terms is larger than the sum of the two
\mathrm{lim.inf}_{n\to\infty}
terms, we have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{lim.inf}_{n\to\infty}\int_{Q\times D}\left(2\nu(\theta^{n})T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})|^{2}-T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(\theta^{n})\kappa(\theta^{n})|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta^{n}|^{2}\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad\geq\int_{Q\times D}\left(2\nu(\theta)T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}-T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(\theta)\kappa(\theta)|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta|^{2}\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t,

which completes the passage to the limit
n\to\infty
in the term
\mathcal{T}_{1}
.

For the term
\mathcal{T}_{2}
, we use that
0\leq T^{\prime}_{k,\varepsilon}(s)s\leq k
for all
s\geq 0
. We also note that because
\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\in C^{1}(\overline{D})
, we have, analogously as in (
4.44
),

\int_{D}(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi^{n}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\to\int_{D}(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\quad\mbox{strongly in $L^{s}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})$}

as
n\rightarrow\infty
for all
s\in[1,\infty)
, and in particular for
s=\frac{r}{r-1}
where
r=\frac{4+6\beta}{5+3\beta}
with
\beta>\frac{5}{6}
. Then, it directly follows from (
4.3
)
2
and (
4.14
), again invoking Proposition 2.61 on p.183 in
[
25
]
, that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{Q\times D}\left(T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n}):\theta^{n}(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi^{n}\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{Q\times D}\left(T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}):\theta(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t.

For the term
\mathcal{T}_{3}
we use that
T^{\prime}_{k,\varepsilon}(s)\geq 0
for all
s\geq 0
and
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\geq 0
on
D
(because
U_{\eta}
and
U_{e}
have been assumed to be monotonically increasing functions on
[0,b/2)
, whereby
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}=|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}\,U_{\eta}^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)\,U_{e}^{\prime}(|\boldsymbol{q}|^{2}/2)\geq 0
on
D
). Also, thanks to (
4.14
) and (
4.33
),

T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\,\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}\to T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta)\,\theta\varphi\quad\mbox{as $n\to\infty$ a.e.~on $Q\times D$}.

Hence, by Fatou’s lemma,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{Q\times D}\left(T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\,\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\int_{Q\times D}\left(T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta)\,\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t.

We are left to deal with the term
\mathcal{T}_{4}
. We perform partial integration with respect to the variable
\boldsymbol{q}
in the first term in the parentheses using the implicitly imposed homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition
\varphi^{n}|_{Q\times\partial D}=0
(cf. (
3.2
)). Hence,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{4}

\displaystyle=\int_{Q\times D}T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\left(|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}-\theta^{n}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right)\varphi^{n}\,\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{(Q\times D)\cap\{\theta^{n}\leq k+\varepsilon\}}T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\left(|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}-\theta^{n}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right)\varphi^{n}\,\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{(Q\times\widetilde{D})\cap\{\theta^{n}\leq k+\varepsilon\}}T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\left(|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}-\theta^{n}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right)\varphi^{n}\,\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{(Q\times(D\setminus\widetilde{D}))\cap\{\theta^{n}\leq k+\varepsilon\}}T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\left(|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}-\theta^{n}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right)\varphi^{n}\,\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t=:\mathcal{T}_{4,1}+\mathcal{T}_{4,2},

for any
\widetilde{D}\subset\joinrel\subset D
. The passage to the limit
n\to\infty
in
\mathcal{T}_{4,1}
is easy because
|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}
and
\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}
are uniformly continuous (and therefore bounded) functions on
\overline{\widetilde{D}}
and
\theta^{n}\leq k+\varepsilon
over the region of integration
(Q\times\widetilde{D})\cap\{\theta^{n}\leq k+\varepsilon\}
. Thus, by the dominated convergence theorem,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{(Q\times\widetilde{D})\cap\{\theta^{n}\leq k+\varepsilon\}}T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\left(|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}-\theta^{n}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right)\varphi^{n}\,\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{(Q\times\widetilde{D})\cap\{\theta\leq k+\varepsilon\}}T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta)\left(|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}-\theta\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right)\varphi\,\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

for any
\widetilde{D}\subset\joinrel\subset D
, and for all
k\geq 1
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.

Concerning
\mathcal{T}_{4,2}
, for
k\geq 1
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
fixed, we now choose a specific
\widetilde{D}\subset\joinrel\subset D
such that

|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}-(k+\varepsilon)\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\geq 0\quad\mbox{on $D\setminus\widetilde{D}$}.

As in the argument leading to the bound (
3.49
), this is always possible to achieve thanks to (
2
)
2
taking, for example,
\widetilde{D}=(1-\delta)D
with
\delta\in(0,1)
sufficiently small; indeed because of the radial symmetry of
\mathcal{U}_{e}(\boldsymbol{q})=U_{e}(\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{q}^{2})
the expression on the left-hand side of the last inequality is a function of
|\boldsymbol{q}|
alone and
\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}/|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}
converges to
0
as
|\boldsymbol{q}|\to(\sqrt{b})_{-}
thanks to (
2
)
2
. Hence, by Fatou’s lemma, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{(Q\times(D\setminus\widetilde{D}))\cap\{\theta^{n}\leq k+\varepsilon\}}T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\left(|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}-\theta^{n}\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right)\varphi^{n}\,\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\int_{(Q\times(D\setminus\widetilde{D}))\cap\{\theta\leq k+\varepsilon\}}T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta)\left(|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}-\theta\Delta_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right)\varphi\,\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Having dealt with both
\mathcal{T}_{4,1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{4,2}
we can pass to the limit in
\mathcal{T}_{4}
to infer that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{Q\times D}T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta^{n})\left(\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi^{n}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\varphi^{n}\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\int_{Q\times D}T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta)\left(\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\varphi\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Having passed to the limit
n\to\infty
in each of the terms
\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{2},\mathcal{T}_{3},\mathcal{T}_{4}
that appear on the right-hand side in the weak formulation of the renormalized temperature equation (
4.8
) we can now pass to the limit
n\to\infty
in the weak formulation of (
4.8
) to obtain a renormalized variational inequality involving the mollified cutoff function
T_{k,\varepsilon}
and its first two derivatives,
T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}
and
T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}
:

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{t}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta),\psi\rangle-\int_{Q}\boldsymbol{v}\,T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{Q}\kappa(\theta)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad\geq\int_{Q}2\nu(\theta)T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{Q}\left(T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}):\int_{D}\theta(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi^{n}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad-\int_{Q}T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(\theta)\kappa(\theta)|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta|^{2}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{Q}\left(T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta)\int_{D}\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t,

which holds for all test functions
\psi\in C([0,T];W^{1,z}(\Omega))
with
z>d
such that
\psi\geq 0
on
\overline{Q}
, and for all
k\geq 1
and all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.

Alternatively, testing (
4.8
) with
\psi\in C^{1}([0,T];W^{1,z}(\Omega))
where
z>d
, and where
\psi\geq 0
on
\overline{Q}
and
\psi(T,\cdot)=0
, integrating by parts with respect to
t
in the time derivative term prior to passing to the limit
n\to\infty
, and then letting
n\to\infty
, we have that

\displaystyle-\int_{Q}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)\partial_{t}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t-\int_{Q}\boldsymbol{v}\,T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{Q}\kappa(\theta)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad\geq\int_{\Omega}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta_{0})\psi(0)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{Q}2\nu(\theta)T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{Q}\left(T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}):\int_{D}\theta(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad-\int_{Q}T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(\theta)\kappa(\theta)|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta|^{2}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{Q}\left(T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\theta)\int_{D}\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t,

for all test functions
\psi\in C^{1}_{0}([0,T);W^{1,z}(\Omega))
with
z>d
such that
\psi\geq 0
on
\overline{Q}
, and for all
k\geq 1
and all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.

It remains to remove the
\varepsilon
-mollification from
T_{k,\varepsilon}
by passing to the limit
\varepsilon\to 0_{+}
. As
T_{k,\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(\theta)\leq 0
on
Q
thanks to the positivity of
\theta
, trivially

\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)=T^{\prime}_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta,

and

T_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)\to T_{k}(\theta)\quad\mbox{and}\quad T^{\prime}_{k,\varepsilon}(\theta)\to T^{\prime}_{k}(\theta)

strongly in
L^{p}(Q)
for
p\in[1,\infty)
and weakly-
\ast
in
L^{\infty}(Q)
,
we can drop the penultimate term from the right-hand side and then pass to the limit
\varepsilon\to 0_{+}
to deduce that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &-\int_{Q}T_{k}(\theta)\partial_{t}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t-\int_{Q}\boldsymbol{v}\,T_{k}(\theta)\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{Q}\kappa(\theta)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}T_{k}(\theta)\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\quad\geq\int_{\Omega}T_{k}(\theta_{0})\psi(0)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{Q}2\nu(\theta)T_{k}^{\prime}(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\qquad+\int_{Q}\left(T_{k}^{\prime}(\theta)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}):\int_{D}\theta(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\qquad+\int_{Q}\left(T_{k}^{\prime}(\theta)\int_{D}\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t,\end{aligned}

(4.50)

for all test functions
\psi\in C^{1}_{0}([0,T);W^{1,z}(\Omega))
with
z>d
such that
\psi\geq 0
on
\overline{Q}
, and for all
k\geq 1
. This is the renormalized variational inequality satisfied by
\theta
we set out to derive.

Remark 2

In the absence of additional information concerning the functions
\theta
and
\varphi
it does not seem possible to pass to the limit
k\rightarrow\infty
to remove the cutoff function
T_{k}
in (
4.50
). In particular, we do not have sufficiently strong a priori bounds at our disposal to ensure that

\mathcal{I}:=\int_{Q\times D}\left[\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\varphi\right]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty.

Looking at this integral, we see that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{I}&=\int_{Q\times D}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta\varphi}}\left(\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi\right)\cdot\sqrt{\theta\varphi}\,\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq\left(\int_{Q\times D}\frac{1}{\theta\varphi}|\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{Q\times D}\theta\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\leq C\left(\int_{Q\times D}\theta\varphi|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},\end{aligned}

(4.51)

where in the transition from the second line to the third line we have used (
3.35
) to bound the first factor. Unfortunately, because no pointwise upper bound on the absolute temperature
\theta
is available, we have no means of ensuring that the integral appearing in the last line is finite. However, if, in addition to the uniform positive lower bound on
\theta
implied by the assumed positivity of the initial temperature
\theta_{0}
, one
assumes
that
\theta
is (as is physically reasonable) also bounded from above, i.e., that

\displaystyle\|\theta\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)}<\infty,

(4.52)

then, by revisiting Step 15 in Section
3
under this added assumption, the argument presented there implies (cf., in particular, (
3.49
) with
Q_{\theta_{\mathrm{max}}}
replaced by
Q
) that the integral in the last line of (
4.51
) is bounded by a constant.
Thus, by using test functions
\psi\in C^{1}_{0}([0,T);W^{1,\infty}(\Omega))
such that
\psi\geq 0
on
\overline{Q}
, one can pass to the limit
k\rightarrow\infty
(using the dominated convergence theorem, for example,) to completely remove the cutoff function and deduce the
conditional
variational inequality

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &-\int_{Q}\theta\partial_{t}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t-\int_{Q}\boldsymbol{v}\,\theta\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{Q}\kappa(\theta)\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\quad\geq\int_{\Omega}\theta_{0}\psi(0)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{Q}2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{Q}\left(\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}):\int_{D}\theta(\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\otimes\boldsymbol{q})\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\qquad+\int_{Q}\left(\int_{D}\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}+|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}|^{2}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t,\end{aligned}

(4.53)

for all
\psi\in C^{1}_{0}([0,T);W^{1,\infty}(\Omega))
such that
\psi\geq 0
of
\overline{Q}
, the condition being that the assumption stated in (
4.52
) holds.

Remark 3

A natural question to pose is why we chose to work with a renormalization of the temperature equation instead of the evolution equation (
2.22
) for the entropy
\eta
. The reason is that the weak formulation of the equation (
2.22
) would involve a term of the form

-\int_{Q}\boldsymbol{j}_{\eta}\cdot\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}z\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t,

where
z\in C^{1}_{0}([0,T);W^{1,\infty}(\Omega))
is a test function and
\boldsymbol{j}_{\eta}
is the entropy flux, defined in (
2.21
). Unfortunately, the second summand of
\boldsymbol{j}_{\eta}
, that is

\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\int_{D}[\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi+\varphi\log\varphi]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q},

cannot be generally guaranteed to belong to
L^{1}(Q)
, even if the function
\theta
were replaced in this term by its truncation
T_{k}(\theta)\in L^{\infty}(Q)
. Indeed, while the energy inequality
(
4.1
) implies that the function
\varphi\log\varphi
is an element of the function space
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega\times D))
, we have no means of ensuring that

\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\int_{D}\varphi\log\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}

belongs to
L^{1}(Q)
. In contrast, such a difficulty does not occur in the weak formulation of the renormalized temperature equation (
4.8
), where all the terms involved in the weak formulation are well-defined.

4.7
Limit passage in the energy inequality

Our objective here is to pass to the limit
n\to\infty
in the sequence of inequalities

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)|^{2}+\mathcal{H}(\theta^{n}(t))\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Omega\times D}[\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi^{n}(t)+\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi^{n}(t)+\mathcal{F}(\varphi^{n}(t))]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&\qquad+\int_{0}^{t}\!\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{2\nu(\theta^{n})|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})|^{2}}{\theta^{n}}+\frac{\kappa(\theta^{n})|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta^{n}|^{2}}{(\theta^{n})^{2}}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\qquad+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\theta^{n}\int_{D}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi^{n}|^{2}}{\varphi^{n}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+\int_{D}\frac{4}{\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}}\left|\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi^{n}+\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+\varphi^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right|^{2}\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\leq\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}^{n}|^{2}+\mathcal{H}(\theta_{0}^{n})\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Omega\times D}[\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi_{0}^{n}+\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi_{0}^{n}+\mathcal{F}(\varphi_{0}^{n})]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&\qquad+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{f}(s)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(s)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s,\end{aligned}

(4.54)

where
\mathcal{H}(s):=s-1-\log s
and
\mathcal{F}(s):=s(\log s-1)+1
for
s>0
, and
\mathcal{F}(0):=1
. We shall do so using a standard argument. We multiply (
4.54
) by a test function
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{0}((0,T);\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
and integrate over
t\in(0,T)
. Hence,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\left\{\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(t)|^{2}+\mathcal{H}(\theta^{n}(t))\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Omega\times D}[\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi^{n}(t)+\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi^{n}(t)+\mathcal{F}(\varphi^{n}(t))]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{t}\!\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{2\nu(\theta^{n})|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})|^{2}}{\theta^{n}}+\frac{\kappa(\theta^{n})|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta^{n}|^{2}}{(\theta^{n})^{2}}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad\left.+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\theta^{n}\int_{D}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi^{n}|^{2}}{\varphi^{n}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+\int_{D}\frac{4}{\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}}\left|\theta^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi^{n}+\theta^{n}\varphi^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+\varphi^{n}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right|^{2}\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s\right\}\phi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\bigg\{\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}^{n}|^{2}+\mathcal{H}(\theta_{0}^{n})\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Omega\times D}[\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi_{0}^{n}+\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi_{0}^{n}+\mathcal{F}(\varphi_{0}^{n})]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{f}(s)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}^{n}(s)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s\bigg\}\phi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\qquad\forall\,\phi\in C^{\infty}_{0}((0,T);\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}).

(4.55)

We now pass to the lim.inf as
n\to\infty
term by term in (
4.7
).

For the first term in the first pair of square brackets in the first line of (
4.7
), we use (
4.5
) to pass to the limit. For the second term in the first pair of square brackets in the first line, we make use of (
4.14
) in conjunction with Fatou’s lemma. For each of the three terms in the second pair of square brackets in the first line, we recall (
4.33
) and again employ Fatou’s lemma.

For the terms in the second line of (
4.7
) note that, by (
4.14
),
\nu(\theta^{n})\to\nu(\theta)
and
\kappa(\theta^{n})\to\kappa(\theta)
a.e. on
Q\times D
. By (
4.35
),
\nu(\theta^{n})\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})\rightharpoonup\nu(\theta)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})
weakly in
L^{\frac{4+6\beta}{5+3\beta}}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
with
\beta>\frac{5}{6}
. By the dominated convergence theorem and the uniform positive lower bound on the sequence
\theta^{n}
over the set
Q
coming from the minimum principle, we have
1/\sqrt{\nu(\theta^{n})\,\theta^{n}}\to 1/\sqrt{\nu(\theta)\,\theta}
strongly in
L^{1}(Q)
, and because this sequence is bounded in
L^{\infty}(Q)
, we infer its strong convergence in
L^{s}(Q)
for all
s\in[1,\infty)
, and therefore, in particular, in

L^{\left(\frac{4+6\beta}{5+3\beta}\right)^{\prime}}(Q)
. Therefore,
\sqrt{\nu(\theta^{n})}\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})/\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\rightharpoonup\sqrt{\nu(\theta)}\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})/\sqrt{\theta}
weakly in
L^{1}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
. However, the energy inequality (
4.7
) guarantees the boundedness of this sequence in
L^{2}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
, and therefore the weak convergence of a subsequence, not indicated, in
L^{2}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
. By the uniqueness of the weak limit, it then follows that

\sqrt{\nu(\theta^{n})}\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})/\sqrt{\theta^{n}}\rightharpoonup\sqrt{\nu(\theta)}\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})/\sqrt{\theta}
weakly in
L^{2}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
. Using this in conjunction with weak lower-semicontinuity in the
L^{2}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
norm and Fatou’s lemma we can pass to the lim.inf as
n\to\infty
in the first term in the square brackets in the second line.
8
8

8

The argument proceeds as follows:

\displaystyle\mbox{lim.inf}_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\bigg[\int_{0}^{t}\!\int_{\Omega}\frac{2\nu(\theta^{n})|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})|^{2}}{\theta^{n}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s\bigg]\phi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\geq\int_{0}^{T}\mbox{lim.inf}_{n\to\infty}\bigg[\int_{0}^{t}\!\int_{\Omega}\frac{2\nu(\theta^{n})|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v}^{n})|^{2}}{\theta^{n}}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s\bigg]\phi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{T}\bigg[\int_{0}^{t}\!\int_{\Omega}\frac{2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}}{\theta}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s\bigg]\phi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\quad\forall\,\phi\in C^{\infty}_{0}((0,T);\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}),

where the first inequality follows by Fatou’s lemma and the second inequality follows by weak lower-semicontinunity of the
L^{2}(Q_{t})
norm for all
t\in(0,T)
, where
Q_{t}=(0,t)\times\Omega
.

Concerning the second term in the square brackets in the second line of (
4.7
), by (
4.15
) we have
\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\log\theta^{n}\rightharpoonup\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\log\theta
weakly in
L^{2}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d})
as
n\to\infty
. We claim that we also have
strong convergence of
\sqrt{\kappa(\theta^{n})}\to\sqrt{\kappa(\theta)}
in
L^{2}(Q)
, and therefore
\sqrt{\kappa(\theta^{n})}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\log\theta^{n}\rightharpoonup\sqrt{\kappa(\theta)}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\log\theta
weakly in
L^{1}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d})
as
n\to\infty
; thus, by the upper bound on the sequence coming from the energy inequality and uniqueness of the weak limit, we also have
\sqrt{\kappa(\theta^{n})}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\log\theta^{n}\rightharpoonup\sqrt{\kappa(\theta)}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\log\theta
weakly in
L^{2}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d})
as
n\to\infty
. Hence, weak lower-semicontinuity of the
L^{2}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d})
norm implies the desired lower bound on this term when passing to the lim.inf as
n\to\infty
in (
4.7
). To prove that
\sqrt{\kappa(\theta^{n})}\to\sqrt{\kappa(\theta)}
in
L^{2}(Q)
as
n\to\infty
, note that
|\sqrt{\kappa(\theta^{n})}-\sqrt{\kappa(\theta)}|^{2}\leq|\kappa(\theta^{n})-\kappa(\theta)|
; it therefore suffices to prove that
\kappa(\theta^{n})\to\kappa(\theta)
in
L^{1}(Q)
as
n\to\infty
. To this end,
recall that the strong convergence of
\theta^{n}
to
\theta
in
L^{1}(Q)
stated in (
4.13
) implies almost everywhere convergence of a subsequence (not indicated) (cf. (
4.14
)). Thanks to the assumed continuity of
\kappa
, it then follows that
\kappa(\theta^{n})\to\kappa(\theta)
a.e. on
Q
as
n\to\infty
. In addition,
thanks to the upper bound in (
2.38
), with
\beta>5/6
, and (
3.40
), which guarantees boundedness of the sequence
(\theta^{n})_{n\geq 1}
in
L^{\frac{2}{3}+\beta}(Q)
, it follows with

s=1+\frac{2}{3\beta}
that

\int_{Q}[\kappa(\theta^{n})]^{s}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\int_{Q}(1+(\theta^{n})^{\beta})^{s}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\big(1+\int_{Q}(\theta^{n})^{\frac{2}{3}+\beta}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}t\big)\leq C.

Because
s>1
, this bound guarantees the uniform integrability of the sequence
(\kappa(\theta^{n}))_{n\geq 1}
in
L^{1}(Q)
, which then, together with the a.e. convergence of
\kappa(\theta^{n})\to\kappa(\theta)
on
Q

as
n\to\infty
, implies the strong convergence of
\kappa(\theta^{n})\to\kappa(\theta)
in
L^{1}(Q)
by Vitali’s theorem. The desired strong convergence of
\sqrt{\kappa(\theta^{n})}\to\sqrt{\kappa(\theta)}
in
L^{2}(Q)
as
n\to\infty
then follows directly.

For the first term in the square brackets in the third line of (
4.7
), we use (
4.43
) in conjunction with weak lower-semicontinuity of the
L^{2}(Q\times D;\mathbb{R}^{d})
norm and Fatou’s lemma, while for the second term in the third line we use (
4.48
) and the definition (
4.2
) of
\boldsymbol{j}^{n}_{\varphi}
together with weak lower-semicontinuity of the
L^{2}(Q\times D;\mathbb{R}^{d})
norm and Fatou’s lemma.

On the right-hand side, we use the strong convergence of the initial data and the weak convergence of
\boldsymbol{v}^{n}
to
\boldsymbol{v}
in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. This yields

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\left\{\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{v}(t)|^{2}+\mathcal{H}(\theta(t))\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Omega\times D}[\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi(t)+\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi(t)+\mathcal{F}(\varphi(t))]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{t}\!\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}}{\theta}+\frac{\kappa(\theta)|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta|^{2}}{(\theta)^{2}}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad\left.+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\theta\int_{D}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+\int_{D}\frac{4}{\theta\varphi}\left|\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right|^{2}\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s\right\}\phi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\bigg\{\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}|^{2}+\mathcal{H}(\theta_{0})\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Omega\times D}[\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi_{0}+\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi_{0}+\mathcal{F}(\varphi_{0})]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{f}(s)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}(s)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s\bigg\}\phi(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\qquad\forall\,\phi\in C^{\infty}_{0}((0,T);\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}).

This then implies the desired energy inequality

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{v}(t)|^{2}+\mathcal{H}(\theta(t))\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Omega\times D}[\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi(t)+\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi(t)+\mathcal{F}(\varphi(t))]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{t}\!\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{v})|^{2}}{\theta}+\frac{\kappa(\theta)|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2}}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\theta\int_{D}\frac{|\nabla_{\boldsymbol{x}}\varphi|^{2}}{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}+\int_{D}\frac{4}{\theta\varphi}\left|\theta\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\varphi+\theta\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}+\varphi\nabla_{\boldsymbol{q}}\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\right|^{2}\!\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{v}_{0}|^{2}+\mathcal{H}(\theta_{0})\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Omega\times D}[\,\mathcal{U}_{e}\varphi_{0}+\mathcal{U}_{\eta}\varphi_{0}+\mathcal{F}(\varphi_{0})]\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{q}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{f}(s)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}(s)\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\,\mathrm{d}s.

for almost every
t\in(0,T)
. That completes the proof of Theorem
1
.
\Box

5
Conclusions

Our aim in this paper was to prove the weak sequential stability of solutions to a thermodynamically consistent kinetic model of nonisothermal flow of a dilute polymeric fluid, which involves a system of nonlinear partial differential equations coupling the unsteady incompressible temperature-dependent Navier–Stokes equations to a temperature-dependent generalization of the classical Fokker–Planck equation satisfied by a probability density function
\varphi
modelling the random configuration of polymer molecules in the viscous Newtonian solvent, and an evolution equation for the absolute temperature
\theta
. We showed that sequences of smooth solutions to the initial-boundary-value problem for the model converge to a global-in-time large-data weak solution that satisfies an energy inequality, where the limiting absolute temperature satisfies a renormalized variational inequality. In the absence of a positive pointwise upper bound on the function
\theta
, it generally does not seem possible to remove the renormalization. We have also shown that if
\theta
is assumed to be bounded above by a positive constant over the space-time domain
Q
, then the renormalization can be eliminated from the variational inequality satisfied by
\theta
.
